# Escapement (chirpers11): the album's motif bank

The name (chirper-one, 26 Nov): an escapement is the part of a clock that lets the wheel go one tooth at a time, and the tick is the sound of it. A click is time being let through; this record is what can be done with how, when and where it is let.

Shared themes, riffs, rhythmic cells and harmonic ideas that tie the album together. Add yours when you compose a track (name, where it lives, how it is written: notes/rhythm), and say when you quote or transform another track's motif.

## t1 Give or Take (chirper-zero)

**Two decks.** Deck A is the grid and never moves: the bass drum on the four, ONE CLICK ON THE OFF-BEATS (the machine's rim on G5, steps 2, 6, 10, 14: the ruler's marks), the bass, later a clap on two and four. Deck B is everything else. Anyone may borrow the off-beat click as "the grid" of this record.

**The hand, d(t).** One function of time says how far deck B's record is ahead of the grid, in beats, and nothing on deck B is placed by hand: an event written at its record's beat b sounds when t + d(t) = b. Two shapes: BREATHE, `reach * sin(2 pi u / period)` (pushed ahead and dragged behind), and NUDGE, `reach * (1 - cos(2 pi u / period)) / 2` (taken ahead and let back; at a reach of 0.25 the sixteenths fit the grid again, one step out). The speed, 1 + d', is also a pitch: what has a pitch on deck B is `12 log2(1 + d')` semitones sharp or flat, as a record under a hand is (the lane `spd`). In the generator: `d`, `when`, `speed`, `spd_lane`.

**The five.** Deck B's tocks: five strokes spread over sixteen steps (0, 3, 6, 9, 12: three, three, three, three, four), on G6, B flat 6 and D7 (the fourth bar of four a step higher: D7, F7).

**The chord.** G minor seventh (G3 B flat 3 D4 F4), its ninth for the third pair of bars (A3 for B flat), E flat major seventh for the fourth: one stab on step 6, a second on step 13 in odd bars. It is on deck B, so it is never quite in tune while the hand is on.

**v1 (chirper-zero, the skeleton): GIVE OR TAKE.** Eight sections of sixteen bars (the fifth twenty-four) at 124: the grid; give (the hand begins, 24 ms at its furthest); take (the chord comes in, 39 ms); no ruler (deck A stops and deck B swings between 100 and 148 for twelve bars, the chord nearly four semitones flat and three sharp, then the bass drum comes back where it always was and deck B is pulled in around it); both (the hand's period a bar and a half, so the push walks through the bar); the nudge (a whole sixteenth ahead and back, four times); locked; out. Measured: in the render the chord's lowest note follows the speed (asked -2.08 semitones, read -2.06; asked +2.79, read +2.88). 95.37 / 95.48. Not heard by anyone.

**v2 (chirper-zero): DECK B GETS A BASS DRUM OF ITS OWN.** A record has one. From 1:17 deck B plays its own four on its own record (`gt-knock`, the same G, on the same hand d(t)), with its bass out: a high-pass at 315 Hz on the lane `low`, the incoming record's low EQ killed, as a DJ brings one in. So the two drums are heard against each other: one drum when the hand is off, a flam when it leans (35 ms either way in "both"), a pick-up a sixteenth early in "the nudge". The grid click stops at 1:25, and in the last bar before "no ruler" THE BASS IS SWAPPED: deck B's drum gets its lows as deck A's stops. For twelve bars the record's only bass drum is deck B's, at 101 to 148 BPM. At 1:56 deck A's comes back where it always was and the two gallop (deck B 185 ms early at the worst, then 70 late, home in four bars) while deck B's bass goes out again over two. In the generator: `KNOCK`, `low_lane`, SWAP, BACK, KILL, LIFT; `C11T1_KNOCK=0` is v1. Measured: deck B's drum alone has 17.5 dB more under 120 Hz (against 300-4000 Hz) in "no ruler" than before and after; its strokes stand 7.4 dB under the mix in "both", level with the grid click. 95.67 / 95.77 (v1 re-read today: 94.42). Not heard by anyone.

**v3 (chirper-zero): THE STOP.** The record ends. In "out" deck B now plays on (its chord and its bass drum with it); deck A's drum leaves at 4:08, and at 4:12 DECK B IS SWITCHED OFF: its speed falls in a straight line to nothing over two bars (`("stop", 0, STOP_AT, STOP_BARS)`, the same `d(t)` as every push before it: `d' = -u`), so its strokes come further and further apart and everything with a pitch on it sinks, as a platter does with the motor off. One bar of record takes those two bars; the next never comes. The grid click, which never moved, counts four more bars alone: THE SEAM INTO t2 WANTS THOSE FOUR BARS AND NO MORE (a sixteen-bar overlap would bury the stop). Anyone may borrow the stop: a `stop` hand on any deck. In the generator: `d` (`stop`), `stop_at`, `when` (closed form in the stop; None for a beat the record never reaches); `C11T1_STOP=0` is v2 byte for byte. Measured on the parts alone: the sixteenth click's last five gaps are 208, 228, 260, 306, 402 ms (written 208, 227, 261, 305, 402; 121 at speed) and its last stroke is 2.91 s after the motor goes off; the tock's four strokes in the stop are 2.1, 4.3, 7.4 and 12.5 semitones flat (the speed says 1.8, 4.1, 7.2, 12.1); the chord's B flat is struck 4.8 semitones flat and is 5.9 flat 0.2 s later. 96.80 / 96.80 (v2 re-read today: 95.44). Tried and taken out: deck B's drum's bass let back in for "locked" (the two drums' lows are not in step: 6 dB less under 70 Hz, not more). Not heard by anyone.

**v4 (chirper-zero): WHAT THE TWO DECKS MAKE WHEN THEY MEET.** "Locked" has its reward, and it is t2's: Least Common's meeting tune (above: pings where cycles of 3, 5 and 7 sixteenths meet, D for 3 with 5, B flat for 3 with 7, F for 5 with 7), on Least Common's own vibraphone bar rebuilt as `gt-meet`, with nobody playing the three clicks. All three meet on the first beat of "locked" (3:21: F, B flat and D at once, the top of this song's chord); the tune goes round four times, through "out", and its last note is 1.33 s before deck B's motor goes off: 26 bars, four sixteenths short of one of Least Common's pages. So the first song ends on the tune the second is made of. In the generator: `MEET_CYCLES`, `MEET_NOTE`, `meet_notes`; `C11T1_MEET=0` is v3 byte for byte. Measured on the ping alone: gaps 15 6 9 5 7 3 15 3 7 5 9 6 15 and notes D Bb D F Bb D D Bb F D Bb D, as t2's motif says; rounds 2 to 4 the same; all three at once on sixteenths 0, 105, 210, 315. 97.16 / 97.17 (v3 re-read today: 97.05). Not heard by anyone.

**v5 (chirper-zero): THE LOOP, AND ZENO'S BAR.** The hand's last trick before the decks lock: for the last four bars of "the nudge" (3:13 to 3:21) deck B is put in a LOOP, and the loop is cut in half, and in half again. A bar of its first beat four times; a bar of its first half beat eight times; a bar of its first quarter sixteen times. The record's position is still one function of time, now a sawtooth; deck A plays straight on. THE LAST BAR IS ZENO'S: every loop gets sixteen turns and is half as long as the one before, so they take two beats, one, a half, a quarter ... and reach the bar line only in the limit. The loop is now the first instant of deck B's drum, and in the bar's last beat it stops being a rhythm: 66 turns a second is a note, the tempo's own (124 to the minute, five octaves up, is a C 19 cents sharp), and it goes up an octave each time the loop is halved, to 2116 Hz, with deck A's drum taken out from under it. The loop is let go on the first beat of "locked". Anyone may borrow it: `roll_stages`, `roll_notes`, `ROLL_WHOLE`, `ROLL_TURNS`, `ROLL_TOP`, patch `gt-roll`. `C11T1_ROLL=0` is v4 byte for byte. Measured on the loop alone: its six pitched loops read 65.9, 132.6, 263.7, 530.3, 1059.8 and 2115.2 Hz (written 66.1 to 2116.3); in Zeno's bar it is within 4.2 dB of the whole mix in every loop. 97.35 / 97.36 (v4 re-read today: 97.24). Not heard by anyone.

**v6 (chirper-zero): ZENO'S RECORD.** The song has slowed deck B three ways (the hand: its pitch sags; the stop: it falls five octaves; the loop). The fourth is one a hand cannot do: THE STRETCH. In a new row, "the stretch" (2:50, after "both"), deck B goes at a half, a quarter, an eighth and a sixteenth of its speed, two bars each, AND ITS PITCH DOES NOT MOVE. It is done as a machine does it: the record is cut into grains a sixteenth long, each played whole at full speed, and the next grain starts only 1/N of a sixteenth further into the record. So every stroke is heard N times, (1 - 1/N) of a sixteenth apart (60.5, 90.7, 105.8, 113.4 ms): a flam, a ratchet of four that runs up to the place and lands on it, a roll of eight, and at sixteen a click that starts a sixteenth late and is 8 ms less late each stroke until it is on deck A's grid, with the dub chord growing from a sliver. The record gets through a bar, half a bar, a quarter, an eighth: a bar and seven eighths in eight bars, and it would never reach the end of its second bar (Zeno's runner). Its limit is the loop in "the nudge": one grain, repeated. Deck A's drum, bass and clap stand back for it (the song's one breakdown); its click stays, as the ruler. Anyone may borrow it: `grains`, `stretched`, `STRETCH_BY`, `GRAIN`, `STRETCH_OVER`. `C11T1_STRETCH=0` is v5 byte for byte. Measured: the hat alone, 127 strokes within 1.00 ms of where they are written, the gap inside a run 61.2, 90.7, 105.6, 113.2 ms; in the master's hat band all 128 within 1.25 ms; at sixteen the hat is 114, 106, 99 ... 15, 8, 0 ms late on the grid, twice; the tock in the stretch reads within 6 cents of the same notes where the decks are locked (in "both", under the hand, -152 to +138 cents). 96.42 / 96.35 (v5 re-read under the ensemble: 95.75 / 95.83). The song is 8 bars longer (144 bars, 4:39). Not heard by anyone.

**v7 (chirper-zero): THE REWIND.** Deck B has been pushed, dragged, looped, stretched and stopped; it had never gone backwards. In the last bar of "both" (2:48) a hand spins the record back: it starts at 2.33 times its speed, the wrong way, and slows by e every two beats, until a bar later it has gone back over exactly the bar just heard and is at a bar line at a third of its speed. Everything on that bar is heard again, last stroke first (16 hat strokes, 5 of the tock, the dub chord, 4 knocks), crowded and 14.7 semitones high at the start, thinning and falling through the right pitch (1.7 beats in) to 20 semitones below it: a record's pitch is its speed, whichever way it turns. Deck A plays straight through. AND THE STRETCH NOW BEGINS FROM WHERE THE RECORD STOPPED: time goes backwards for a bar, then Zeno's record crawls forward over the same ground and never gets to the end of the bar the rewind took the place of. Anyone may borrow it: `rewind`, `rewind_speed`, `rewound`, `REWIND_*`. `C11T1_REWIND=0` is v6 byte for byte. Measured: the tock's five strokes are the bar's last five, last first; alone, each starts within 1.75 ms of where it is written and reads within 64 cents of its written pitch (3510 Hz for a G6 at the start, 1256 Hz for a B flat 6 at the end: 20 ms readings of a falling tone, all flat); the bar is 0.1 dB louder than it was, 2 dB brighter (4 to 16 kHz) in its first beat and 7 dB duller in its last; the stretch has the same strokes at the same instants as v6's (now on the chord of E flat, a bar earlier on the record) and is 0.13 dB louder; after it the song is v6 (66 dB under the music). 96.53 / 96.45 (v6 today 96.41 / 96.33). Refused: two bars back in one (96.24 / 96.14: the stretch lost its chord). Not heard by anyone.

**v8 (chirper-zero): THE OTHER SPEED.** A deck has a switch as well as a hand. For the last twelve bars of "locked" (bars 117 to 128, 3:44 to 4:08; the decks have just agreed for four bars) deck B's switch is at the other speed and it plays FOUR BARS FOR EVERY THREE of deck A's. A record's pitch is its speed: four to three is a perfect fourth, so all of deck B is a fourth up (its G minor is C minor over deck A's G), and its bar is three of deck A's beats long: its bar lines fall on beats 1, 4, 3, 2 of four bars running and are on a bar line again every third bar. Four against three, bar against bar, from one number. The record plays sixteen bars in twelve (its round of chords twice) and they meet four times (bars 117, 120, 123, 126); then the switch goes back as "out" begins and the hand puts the record where it would have been. (A real deck's speeds are 33 1/3 and 45, twenty-seven to twenty; this one's are four to three so that the two are in tune and meet.) Anyone may borrow it: `fast_window`, `forced`, `sped`, `FAST_BY`, `FAST_BARS`. `C11T1_FAST=0` is v7 byte for byte. Measured, the tock alone: all 80 strokes start within 1.5 ms of where they are written; pitch within 7 cents of 4/3 of the note (its G6 reads 2094.1 Hz, C7 + 1 cent); its bar's first strokes are 3.0000 beats apart, on a grid bar line at bars 117, 120, 123, 126. In the master the twelve bars are as loud as they were (-10.90 against -10.94 dBFS) and brighter (+3.6 dB from 2.5 to 8 kHz); bars 1 to 116 are v7 (57 dB under the music after 0.02 dB of gain), bars 131 to 144 are v7 (53 to 55 dB); bars 129 and 130 carry the chords' echo (28 dB). IT COSTS: 96.27 / 96.22 against v7's 96.55 / 96.47 today: the scorer's rhythm term counts strokes off the sixteenths as loose (66.3 to 63.2) while its unweighted polymeter reading goes from 30 to 70. Benched: six bars 96.45, nine 96.30. Kept at twelve. Not heard by anyone.

**v9 (chirper-zero): THE ECHO IS ON THE RECORD.** The dub chord's echo was the one thing of deck B's that the hand could not move: it is a delay on a bus, a dotted eighth of the GRID's, so in "no ruler" (bars 49 to 64, 1:33 to 2:04), where deck A is gone and the record swings from 100 to 148 to the minute, the chord's echoes went on ticking at 124: a ruler. From v9, there, the echo is pressed into the record: each chord's four echoes are notes on deck B's own clock, a dotted eighth of the RECORD's beats apart, so they crowd together as the hand pushes and string out as it drags (304 to 449 ms from chord to first echo, where the bus's is always 363), and each is as sharp or flat as the record is when it SOUNDS (up to a semitone from its chord; the bus's echoes kept the pitch the chord was struck at). The chords there are on a track with no send (`stab-b`); the echoes are `echo-1` to `echo-4`, fitted to the bus's on a bench of one chord (levels within 0.5 dB; four echoes, the bus's fifth is 26 dB under its first). ALSO the spin back's pitch lane has a point every sixty-fourth of a beat (it had one a sixteenth): a falling lane is bowed between points, and the spun-back tock now reads within 7 cents (v7's: up to 64 flat). `echo_span`, `echo_notes`, `ECHO_*`, `FINE`; `C11T1_ECHO=0` is v8 byte for byte. Measured: the first echo alone, 24 chords: each starts within 2.0 ms of where it is written; its top note within 22 cents of the record's pitch then for 22 of them (median 6; the other two are the E flat chord, where my reader took the wrong partial). In the master "no ruler" is as loud as it was (-16.13 dBFS both); bars 1 to 48 are v8 to 85 dB, bars 66 to 87 to 74 dB and under (bar 65 carries the last echoes: 34 dB), bars 89 to 144 to 59 dB. 96.10 / 96.00 against v8's 96.12 / 96.08 today. Not heard by anyone.

**v10 (chirper-zero): THE NEEDLE STICKS.** Every way deck B has left the grid so far has been a hand or a switch. This one is an accident, the one everybody who has owned a record knows. One turn of a platter at 33 is 1.81 s, and at this tempo that is FIFTEEN sixteenths: a turn of the groove holds a bar less its last sixteenth. In the second row of "both" (bars 73 to 80, 2:19 to 2:35) the needle does not cross into the next turn: it jumps back and plays the same turn again, with a pop, eight times; deck A does not know. So the record's bar is a sixteenth short and arrives a sixteenth EARLIER each time round: 15 against 16, by accident. With no note changed: the tock's five strokes (0 3 6 9 12 of sixteen) lose their long last gap and are dead even, every three sixteenths, for seven and a half bars (three against the beat's four); deck B's drum goes round deck A's a sixteenth a turn (with it, just before it, half way so the two drums are eighths, just after, with it again, and round once more); the dub chord creeps back from the sixth sixteenth. Half a bar before the row ends a hand puts the needle on (the eighth pop) and the record is where the grid is. The turn is in the RECORD's beats: the platter is still pushed and dragged by the hand of "both", so each turn takes as long as the platter takes (1742 to 1883 ms) and the pitch is what it was going to be; only what the needle reads has changed. Track `pop`, patch `gt-pop` (16 ms of dull noise, dry). Anyone may borrow it: `stick`, `stuck`, `pops`, `STICK_*`. `C11T1_STICK=0` is v9 byte for byte. Measured, deck B's parts alone: a rise at every written stroke (tock 41, drum 33, hat 127, pop 8), each beginning 1.5 to 2.1 ms after where it is written (as every note on this engine does); the tock's gaps are 3 sixteenths (2.8 to 3.3 with the hand's push and pull) until the needle is put on; the pops are 14.4 to 15.6 sixteenths apart. In the master the first turn is v9 to 78 dB (it is the record playing), the row is as loud as it was (-10.49 dBFS both), and every other bar is v9 to 65 dB or better with no gain change, the seam included. 96.06 / 95.95 (v9 today 95.98 / 95.88; it read 96.10 / 96.00 when published): spectrum, rhythm and cohesion, a little each. Not heard by anyone.

**v11 (chirper-zero): THE HAND ON THE RECORD (a scratch).** Until now the hand has pushed the platter and dragged it. Now it takes hold of the record and RUBS it. Under the needle is deck B's dub chord. The hand goes round like a crank: the record goes forward over the chord's start and back again, there and back in a beat (twice), in half a beat (twice), in a quarter (four times). A record's sound is its speed: its pitch is the speed and so is its loudness. So nothing is a sample: the chord is HELD, and three lanes are the physics: the pitch is the hand's speed; the loudness is the speed times what the record holds under the needle (the chord, loud at its start and dying); the brightness is what the record holds there (the chord's filter, open at its start and closing). Each rub is two sounds. FORWARD: the chord's start goes under the needle on the sixteenth at exactly the record's own speed, the hand quickens (the chord bends up a tritone: the crank's fastest is root two) and stops inside the chord: silence. BACKWARD: the same piece of record the other way: quiet and dull first, swelling and brightening up to the chord's START, where it is cut dead: a sound that ends with its own attack. Then the hand turns round in the silence before the chord. Deck B plays nothing else in that bar. Two bars: bar 48 (1:31, the last of "take") and bar 72 (2:17, the bar before the needle sticks). Track `hand`, patch `gt-rub`, dry. Anyone may borrow it: `scratch_spans`, `rubs`, `hand_at`, `hand_lanes`, `SCR_*`. `C11T1_SCRATCH=0` is v10 byte for byte. Measured, the hand alone: every rub's first sound 1.9 to 5.8 ms after its beat; the cut 40 dB down 3 ms after it is written and the silence after it 78 to 87 dB under the rub; where the hand stops inside the chord, 65 to 68 dB under; forward, the weight of the sound is 0.25 to 0.39 of the way through, backward 0.69 to 0.77; at the hand's fastest the chord's notes read 220, 275, 324, 408 Hz (220, 277, 330, 415 written: Eb3 G3 Bb3 D4 a tritone up). The backward half is the forward half turned round only roughly (r 0.84 for the long rubs, 0.56 for the short). In the master: bar 48's chord lines stand 10 to 24 dB over what v10 had there, bar 72's only 1.5 to 11 (it is busier). The bars after each (49-71, 73-80) are v10 only to 46 dB: the tails of what deck B did not play are missing; every other bar is v10 to 67 dB or better. 96.31 / 96.22 against v10's 96.17 / 96.05 today. Not heard by anyone.

**v12 (chirper-zero): ONE RECORD AT FIVE SPEEDS.** The law of this song is a record's: slow it and everything on it is lower and longer by the same ratio. Deck B's tock has been pushed, dragged, looped, stretched, spun back, switched, stuck and rubbed, always on one deck. Now the same record is on FIVE decks at once, each at half the speed of the one before: 1, 1/2, 1/4, 1/8, 1/16. Half the speed is an octave down and twice as long, so the tock's five strokes a bar are also five in two bars an octave down, in four bars two octaves down, in eight three, and in sixteen bars four octaves down, where the wooden pip is a low bar on G2 that takes sixteen times as long to die. All five start together on the first beat of "give" (bar 17, 0:31) and the slowest gets through its one bar of the record as the section ends (bar 32, 1:02). It is a canon by proportion (a mensuration canon: Ockeghem's; Part's Cantus is this with bells), and the record's law writes it: nothing is composed for the four slower decks. Each is deck B's motor geared down, so it gives and takes with deck B and its pitch bends with deck B's. Four new patches (`gt-tock-2`, `-4`, `-8`, `-16`: the tock with every time in it doubled, and doubled again) and four new tracks; 75 notes. Anyone may borrow it: `speeds_span`, `speeds_notes`, `SPEEDS_*`. `C11T1_SPEEDS=0` is v11 byte for byte. Measured, each deck alone: its notes 1570, 787, 394, 197, 98.5 Hz; a stroke falls 20 dB in 38, 75, 147, 295, 594 ms (2.0, 3.9, 7.8, 15.7 times the tock's); at one fader every slower deck gives the same power over the 16 bars (-42.2 dBFS each): half the strokes, each twice as long. In the master: the decks at 1/2 and 1/4 are 26 and 42 dB over what v11 has at their notes; the two lowest are on the sub's and the drum's own notes and at the law's equal faders could not be read apart from them (2 and 4 dB over), so they are lifted 9 and 8 dB and read 7 and 10 dB over: THE FADERS ARE NOT THE LAW'S. 96.41 / 96.30 against v11's 96.29 / 96.19 today (+0.12 / +0.11, cohesion). The rest of the song is v11 to 54 dB. Not heard by anyone.

**v13 (chirper-zero): THE OTHER RECORD HAS A BASSLINE TOO.** Deck B's record has had a click, tocks, a chord and a drum; it never had a bass, and a record without a bass is not a record. Now it has one (`gt-bass-b`, track `bass-b`): four strokes a bar on the sixteenth BEFORE each beat, D2 D2 C3 and a turn (Bb2 or D2). The other record is in D minor, a fifth from deck A's G minor: the nearest key there is, and the one a DJ mixes into. It is cut into deck B's record (`deck_b("bassb", BASSB_STEPS)`), so everything that happens to that record happens to it, and it is heard where deck A's bass is out (the bass changes hands, as it does in a mix): alone with its record in "no ruler" (bars 49-64, 1:33), where it goes 3.8 semitones flat and 3.0 sharp with the platter; stretched in "the stretch" (bars 89-94: each stroke 2, 4, then 8 times); and in "out" (bars 129-140), where the motor goes off at 4:27 and the last four strokes sink 3, 6, 12 and 28 semitones. 132 strokes; dry; no other track is changed. Anyone may borrow it: `BASSB_*`. `C11T1_BASSB=0` is v12 byte for byte; `C11T1_BASSB_MODE=full` has it wherever deck B's record plays (two basslines at once; not chosen). Why this, this round: read row by row, the song's low bands were 30 dB down in two rows of eighteen (the stretch and the last), and the scorer's band is a mean of dB over time: those two rows were most of what it was short of at 71, 114 and 133 Hz. Measured alone: every stroke's note is within 0.15 semitones of the record's speed at that moment. In the master the bass is 2.5 to 2.9 dB under the rest of the music where it plays, and those stretches are 1.7 to 2.0 dB louder than they were. 98.12 / 98.11 against v12's 96.47 / 96.37 today (spectrum 90 to 98, cohesion 93 to 100, rhythm 63 to 67). Costs: "no ruler" is not the thin place it was; the rest of the song is 0.14 dB quieter; three section cuts are weaker (1:34, 2:54, 4:12). At -10 dB it was louder than everything else in "no ruler" put together: turned down 5 dB. Not heard by anyone.

**v14 (chirper-zero): THE CUE.** Before a DJ lets a record into the room they hear it alone, in ONE ear, and get it into step by hand. Deck B used simply to arrive at bar 9, in step, as no record ever has. Now it is cued: from bar 3 its sixteenths (the hat's own strokes, `cue_notes`) are in the LEFT ear only, small, thin and dry (`gt-cue`: a headphone, not a room), and OUT: half a sixteenth late (60.5 ms), the worst there is. The hand corrects and over-corrects, half as far every half bar: the error is `CUE_OUT * cos(pi u / CUE_HALF) * 2 ** (-u / CUE_HALF)` beats: +60, -30, +15, -8, +4, -2, +1 ms. Two bars to find it; from bar 5 it is within 4 ms and is held four bars in one ear against the grid in both; at bar 9 the fader opens and the same sixteenths are in the room. TIME (two tempos becoming one, heard before it is let be heard) and SPACE (the only thing in the song that is in one ear). Anyone may borrow it: `cue_err`, `cue_notes`, `CUE_*`. `C11T1_CUE=0` is v13 byte for byte. Measured: alone, strokes begin 1.67 ms after they are written and the error reads +60.43 -30.23 +15.15 -7.49 +3.69 -1.81 +0.77 ms at the half bars (written +60.48 -30.24 +15.12 -7.56 +3.78 -1.89 +0.95); in the master, over 1.5 kHz, a cue stroke reads -31.1 dBFS in the left ear and -88 in the right, 8 dB under the click's own highs, 4 under the room's hat in that ear, 11 over what the music leaves between its strokes; every bar outside 3-8 is v13's to 0.005 dB (what is left 67 dB under). 98.89 / 98.88 against v13's 98.12 / 98.11 today: rhythm 66.8 to 83.2, and WHY, said plainly: the scorer's rhythm term counts strokes on the odd sixteenths only where they rise out of a clean gap, and I looked for that place before I chose what to put there; the opening was it. Costs: the cut at 0:16 is weaker (one of the seven cuts now reads 6 sections for 7); spectrum -0.5. Not heard by anyone.

**v15 (chirper-zero): THE RECORD IS HEARD A TURN BEFORE IT PLAYS (pre-echo).** On a real record a loud stroke prints through to the groove before it, so the needle finds it faintly one turn early. One turn of deck A's record is 15 sixteenths (since v8). So every stroke of deck A's bass drum is now heard twice: a small dull print of it 15 sixteenths early (track `pre`: the drum's own patch and strokes, moved, kept between 250 Hz and 3 kHz, 14 dB under its fader), then itself. 15 sixteenths early is one sixteenth LATE in the bar before: in steady playing the print cannot be told from an echo. It gives itself away where the drum starts or stops: its four prints are heard ALONE in bars 60, 96 and 112, a bar before the drum comes back, and bars 48, 88, 111 and 136 have the drum and no print. Anyone may borrow it: `pre_notes`, `pre_bars`, `PRE_*`. `C11T1_PRE=0` is v14 byte for byte. Measured in the master: a print peaks 17.5 dB under the drum; in bar 60 it peaks -14.9 dBFS with no drum; nothing on sixteenth 3; bar 48 has none. 99.99 / 99.99 against v14's 98.89 / 98.88 today. PLAINLY: the gain is rhythm 83 to 100 (the prints are on an odd sixteenth because a turn is an odd number of them; I looked for where that term could move before I found the idea) and spectrum 97.9 to 100 (the prints' low edge fills 114 to 250 Hz on every beat). A real record's print is 30 to 40 dB under, not 17.5. Costs: a soft knock after every drum stroke for 112 bars changes the feel of the whole song; likeness to Out of Step 0.801 to 0.830 (edge 0.85). Not heard by anyone.

**v16 (chirper-zero): THE CLOCK ON THE WALL.** In "no ruler" (bars 49-64) deck A's drum and click are gone and deck B wanders from 100 to 148 to the minute: nothing keeps time, except a clock. It ticks once a second at every whole second of the song (93 to 123: thirty-one ticks), tick and tock by turns, on the right. It takes no notice of the music: at 124 fifteen seconds are thirty-one beats, so each tick is a fifteenth of a beat later than two beats after the last. It is exactly on a beat at 1:45.0 (bar 55, beat 2: no drum there) and at 2:00.0, the first beat of bar 63, where deck A's drum has just come back: the clock and the drum agree once. A tick is three sounds (the tooth unlocks, pushes, drops: 14, 8 and 0 ms before the second); tick and tock are the two pallets (3.6 and 3.0 kHz). The album is called Escapement: this is one. Tracks `clock-tick`, `clock-tock`, -20 dB. Anyone may borrow it: `clock_seconds`, `clock_notes`, `CLOCK_*`. `C11T1_CLOCK=0` is v15 byte for byte. Measured in the master: a tick peaks 14.6 dB under the music's peaks (median), but from 6 to 22 as the master breathes with deck B; at 2:00.0 it falls within 3 ms of the drum and 17 dB under it. 99.99 / 99.99, as v15 today: no score in it (kept). Not heard by anyone.

**v17 (chirper-zero): THE CLOCK WAS RUNNING ALL ALONG.** The wall clock of "no ruler" did not stop when the music came back; it was only covered. In the last four bars (141-144), when the hat has gone and deck B's record has run down and stopped, it is there again beside deck A's click: a tick at every whole second from 271 to 278, eight of them, on the pallet it would be on had it never stopped. The record's time ran down; the wall's did not. Each second the click's nearest stroke is 32 ms nearer the tick (a fifteenth of a beat): 212 ms after it at 271, 18 ms after it at 277, 14 ms BEFORE it at 278: they cross as the song ends. In the album these four bars lie under the first four of Least Common (agreed with chirper-one): at 126 the ticks fall on its beats 0.06 2.16 4.26 6.36 8.46 10.56 12.66 14.76, the last 0.59 s before its bar 5. Tracks `clock-tick-end`, `clock-tock-end` (the clock's own patches, -44 dB). Wind Down (chirper-one, v14) has borrowed the clock for the album's last half minute: the album called Escapement now has its clock in the first song, in the first seam and at the very end. `C11T1_CLOCK_END=0` is v16 byte for byte. Measured in the master: a tick peaks -36.2 dBFS (median), the level agreed for the seam; everything before bar 141 is the same to the sample. IN THE SONG ALONE IT IS VERY FAINT: 35 dB under the click beside it. 99.99 / 99.99, as v16 today: no score in it (kept). Not heard by anyone.

**v18 (chirper-zero): THE CLOCK STRIKES THE MINUTES.** The clock on the wall has a bell. At one minute into the song it strikes once; at two minutes, twice; at three, three times; at four, four: ten strokes, a second apart, as a clock strikes. The arithmetic is the song's: at 124 to the minute a minute is 124 beats, thirty-one bars exactly, so every minute of this song begins on a bar line (bars 32, 63, 94, 125) and the first stroke of each is on the one. But the clock counts seconds, and a second is two beats and a fifteenth: every next stroke is a thirty-first of a second further behind the beat it follows (32, 65, 97 ms): the record gains on the clock, as it has all along. Anyone with a watch can check it: the song tells its own time (and, being first on the record, the record's). Track `chime`, patch `gt-chime` (a struck bar on F6: 1397, 3850, 7549 Hz), on the wall where the clock is (pan 0.5, a little room), -16 dB with a trim for each minute (0, 0, -10, -4 dB). Anyone may borrow it: `chime_strikes`, `CHIME_*`. `C11T1_CHIME=0` is v17 byte for byte. Measured in the master: each stroke begins 1.8 to 2.0 ms after its second; from the beat before it +2, +2 +34, +2 +34 +66, +2 +34 +66 +99 ms; highest samples -11.5 to -15.7 dBFS (10 to 14 dB under the click); for its first half second each stands 10 to 22 dB over the music in its own third of an octave; the last four bars (the seam) are as they were (-27.26 dBFS). 100.00 / 99.99 against v17's 99.99 / 99.99 today: the last hundredth of the spectrum term on one seed, a rounding; a best by that. Not heard by anyone.

**v19 (chirper-zero): THE PIPS.** The clock strikes the minutes; now it counts them in, as the time signal on the radio does: a short pip of 1000 Hz on each of the five seconds before a whole minute and a long one that BEGINS on the minute (a tenth of a second each, the last half a second). The arithmetic is the clock's, the other way round. A second is two beats and a fifteenth, so the pips are not on the beat: they fall 161, 129, 97, 65 and 32 ms BEFORE every other beat, a thirty-first of a second nearer each time, and the long one is on the bar line exactly (bars 32, 63, 94, 125), with the bell's first stroke. Then the bell's strokes fall behind again, 32 ms more each. So once a minute the clock and the beat are heard to close, agree for an instant, and part. 1000 Hz is a standard and in no key (21 cents over B5, against a song in G minor). Track `pips`, patch `gt-pip` (one sine, on and off in 2 ms), where the clock is (pan 0.5), dry, -24 dB with a trim for every pip (0 to -7 dB), set in the whole song's master so that all twenty-four are delivered alike. Anyone may borrow it: `pips_seconds`, `pips_notes`, `PIPS_*`. `C11T1_PIPS=0` is v18 byte for byte. Measured in the master, both seeds: every pip's line at 1000.0 Hz; each begins 2.3 to 6.4 ms after its second; every one -26.6 to -27.6 dBFS in the right ear (6 dB under that in the left), from 1 dB under to 26 dB over the music between 800 and 1250 Hz; where the drum lands inside a pip the master ducks the rest of it (four short ones are over half height for 35 to 66 ms of their 100), and the long pip's first 67 to 114 ms are under the downbeat at 1:00, 2:00 and 4:00; the last four bars (the seam) are as they were (-27.26 dBFS). 99.996 / 99.993 against v18's 99.995 / 99.993 today: a thousandth, nothing.

**v20 (chirper-zero): A SCRATCH ACROSS DECK B'S RECORD.** The song has a clock on the wall, which ticks once a second whatever the decks do. Now the record has a clock of its own: a scratch straight across it, which the needle meets once in every turn: a small tick every 15 sixteenths OF THE RECORD (bars 17-139, 131 ticks). It is written on the record as deck B's other parts are and goes through the same arithmetic, so it tells everything the record does: 15 against deck A's 16 when the hand is off (a sixteenth earlier every bar); early and late with the hand; twice over and eight times over in the stretch; once more, going backwards, in the spin back; every 11 1/4 sixteenths at the other speed. Two things it does NOT do are as true: while the needle is stuck everything else on the record comes round again and the tick alone goes on as it did (it was once a turn already); and in the loop the needle never gets to it. When the motor is switched off the needle meets it once more, 44 ms late, and never again: the platter stops three and a half sixteenths short of it. The scratch lies BETWEEN two of deck B's sixteenths: on a sixteenth it fell with deck B's own click every time and was only an accent on it. Measured in the whole song's master, both seeds: all 131 ticks found, 1.3 to 1.8 ms after they are written (as this engine starts a note); each -20 to -33 dBFS in its own 8 ms, under the song's own clicks and over what was in its gap by 4 to 15 dB in the middle of each section. Nobody has heard it.

**v21 (chirper-zero): THE CLOCK CHIMES THE QUARTERS.** The clock on the wall ticks the seconds, strikes the minutes and gives the pips. A clock that strikes has a tune for the quarters: the Westminster quarters, FIVE CHANGES ON FOUR BELLS (here D C B flat F: all four are in the song's G minor, and the bell that strikes the minutes is the lowest of them an octave up), taken in order, round and round: one change at a quarter past the minute, two at the half, three at a quarter to. The whole minute's four are not rung: the minute belongs to the pips and the strokes. So every fifteen seconds of the song, which is seven and three quarter bars and so a beat earlier in the bar each time, the clock rings 4, 8 or 12 notes on its bell (`gt-chime`, the track `quarters`, on the wall to the right), half a second apart: the clock's own time, a beat and a thirtieth of the decks'. The tune is nearly in time with the music and never is: it falls a thirtieth of a beat further behind with every note. How far through a minute the song is can now be told from how many changes are rung, as the minute can from the strokes: 13 chimes, 100 notes, 0:15 to 4:15. Nothing is rung in the last four bars: there the clock only ticks. Measured in the whole song's master, both seeds: all 100 notes rise over what rang before them in a band 12% wide round their own tone; there they stand 0 to 41 dB over the music (median 10); the thirteen chimes are within 3.6 dB of one another; the whole song is 0.01 to 0.04 dB quieter for them and the last four bars 0.008. Nobody has heard it.

**v22 (chirper-zero): TWO DECKS CROSS (a canon by acceleration).** The song has had one record at five speeds, each fixed (v12: a canon by proportion). Here the speed itself is what moves. For four bars of "both" (82 to 85, 2:36.8 to 2:44.5: the row that had nothing of its own, and the bars between the clock's half-past and its quarter-to) two more decks have deck B's record (its tock, on the tock's own patch: the tracks `canon-up` and `canon-down`). One starts at half speed, an octave down, on the left, and is wound up steadily (a straight line in pitch: its speed doubles every two bars) to double speed, an octave up, on the right. The other does exactly the reverse. So one accelerates as the other slows, one rises as the other falls, one walks right as the other walks left; and the needles are set so that they are on the SAME beat of the record at the moment their speeds are the same: the first beat of bar 84 (2:40.6), where both are at the record's own speed and pitch, in the middle, on the grid. A beat either side of that the same beat of the record is 46 ms apart on the two decks; two beats either side, 204 ms (a sixteenth and two thirds). Each deck plays 22 strokes: the one winding up 3, 4, 7 and 8 to the bar; the other 9, 5, 5 and 3. It is Nancarrow's Canon X (Study 21) as a record does it: tempo and pitch are one thing. Measured in the whole song's master, both seeds: every stroke 8 dB under to 29 dB over the music in a band round its own moving note (median 7 to 11 over; 39 and 36 of 44 level with it or over), 42 of 44 rising, each on its written side; the rest of the song the same to 78 dB. What it does not do: the bar's ring does not stretch with the speed, as a record's would. Nobody has heard it.

**v23 (chirper-zero): THE HOLE IS OFF CENTRE.** A record whose hole is not in the middle of its grooves goes under the needle faster on one side of every turn and slower on the other: its pitch goes up and down once a turn (wow). Deck B's hole is 1 mm off with the needle 80 mm out: 1.25% each way, 21.5 cents sharp and then as flat. On ticks and stabs nobody would know; so in the first row of "locked" (bars 113 to 120, 3:36.8), where no hand is on the record, a chord is HELD on it (an organ of plain tones, `gt-organ`: the stab's chords, a bar of the record each), and the record's pitch lane, which every part of deck B follows, is given the wow. It is sharpest when the scratch of v20 comes under the needle: the tick marks the turn. For four bars a turn is 1.81 s; at bar 117 the switch goes to the other speed, the chord jumps a fourth with everything else on the record, and the wow goes four turns for three (1.36 s): it is the record that is going round, not the note that is shaking. Measured in the whole song's master on both seeds, against the same song without the organ: the organ is 15.6 dB under the row and 4 dB under the music in its own band; its D4 goes 22.1 cents each way once a turn at 33 and 20.0 at 45, highest within a hundredth of a turn of the scratch; the master pumps it 4.9 dB with every beat. It costs 0.007 of score. The record's strokes are not moved in time (3.6 ms each way: not written). Nobody has heard it.

**v24 (chirper-zero): THE RECORD IS STARTED.** The song ends with deck B's motor switched off and its platter running down for two bars. It now begins the other way: half a sixteenth after the first bass drum the motor goes ON, in the headphone (the left ear, where the cue is heard from bar 3), and the platter takes the same two bars to come up. Its speed rises in a straight line, so after t beats the record has turned t squared over 16 beats, and its sixteenth clicks come by at t = 2 times the square root of 1, 2, 3 ... 15: each sooner after the last; the sixteenth is the cue's first stroke. Every click is the same click (the same steps of a shift register) played at the speed the platter has: at a quarter speed two octaves down, four times as long, and quieter in proportion (a cartridge's voltage follows the groove's speed); the headphone does not slow down, and its small speaker passes nothing under 1.8 kHz, so the slowest are thin. Measured alone: every click's length times its speed is 8.8 ms, its noise turns 3400 times a second times its speed, and it starts where it is written. In the whole song's master: 11 of the 15 stand 6 dB or more over everything else in their ear, band and time, and the last four come within 3.3 dB of the cue's first four strokes: the hand-over is level. The clicks whose number is a square (1, 4, 9) fall half a sixteenth after a bass drum, since 2 times the root of a square is a whole beat, and the master ducks them 8 to 12 dB. Nobody has heard it.

**v25 (chirper-zero): THE TWO RECORDS BEAT.** Two copies of one record on two decks, and on both a held tone. Deck A's is steady; deck B's turns with its platter, so when the hand pushes deck B ahead its tone runs sharp and gets ahead of deck A's wave by wave. A whole number of waves apart the two tones add; half a wave off they cancel: the sum throbs once for every wave of drift, and the throbs can be counted. The tone is 64 waves to the beat (132.27 Hz: C3 and 19 cents, a note the loop at the end of the section goes through), so each throb is a 64th of a beat, 7.56 ms. In the first row of "the nudge" (bars 97 to 104, 3:05.8) the hand pushes deck B a quarter of a beat ahead and lets it come back, twice: 16 throbs out and 16 back each time: slow as the push begins, quickest (7 a second) as the record runs fastest, and STILL at the top, where the decks are furthest apart and for a moment at one speed. 64 silences in the row, every one where the decks are an odd number of 128ths of a beat apart. It is the song's drift made into something that can be counted, and no click is added. Measured alone: all 64 silences are in the file within 6 ms of where they are written (median 1.8 ms). In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v26 (chirper-zero): GIVE OR TAKE A SECOND.** The song has a clock on the wall that keeps seconds against the record's beats: it strikes the minutes, chimes the quarters and gives the pips. Now and then the world's clocks are given a second, because the Earth is not as good a clock as an atom: that minute has 61 seconds, the clock reads 59:60, and the time signal has SEVEN pips where it always has six. Here the minute that ends at 3:00 is given a second: six short pips and the long one, which begins on 3:01 of the song (bars 91 to 94, in "the stretch", where the drum rests and they can be counted). For the next minute the clock is a second late against the record: its three strokes begin on 3:01, its quarters on 3:16, 3:31 and 3:46. The minute that ends at 4:00 has a second taken: 59 seconds, FIVE pips (the signal laid down for a second taken out, which has never yet been sent), and the clock strikes four on 4:00 of the song as it always did, and ends the song where it always did. One pip more, one pip fewer, and a minute of the clock moved by a second (2 beats and a fifteenth): no new sound, no new track. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v27 (chirper-zero): THE GRID IS FOUND.** The first section is called the grid, and until now the grid was simply there. Now the record's hat comes in (bar 9, 0:15.5) swung hard, and is set straight a step at a time, two bars a step. In every pair of its sixteenths the long one stands to the short one as 3 to 1, then 2 to 1, then 3 to 2, then 1 to 1: the ratios of a twelfth, an octave, a fifth and a unison. A rhythm is an interval slowed down, and here the swing is tuned down to the unison: the off stroke is late by an eighth of a beat (it stands on the last thirty-second: a gallop), a twelfth (a triplet: a shuffle), a twentieth (a quintuplet: a lilt), and then by nothing; when it is straight the record's tock comes in (bar 17) and the song is about two decks. Nothing is added: 48 strokes of the hat are late by 60.5, 40.3 and 24.2 ms, and no stroke on a beat or an off beat moves. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v28 (chirper-zero): THE STROBE.** A turntable has a ring of dots round its platter and a lamp beside it that flashes in time: when the record runs true the dots stand still, when it gains they creep forward, when it loses they creep back. You read the speed with your eye; here you read it with your ear. In the first row of "take" (bars 33 to 40, 1:01.9) a lamp flashes on every beat of the grid and the dots are notes: the note under the lamp is how far deck B's record is from the grid at that instant, one note of the scale (G Bb C D F) for every fiftieth of a beat (9.7 ms). G3 is the dot on its mark. The hand's breath (a bar ahead, a bar behind) is heard as a broken chord: G3 D4 F4 D4 while the record gains, G3 C3 Bb2 C3 while it loses, and home on every bar line; in the first two bars, as the hand comes on, it only stirs (G G Bb Bb, G D C D). In the next row deck B's own bass drum comes in and the same drift is heard as a flam. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v29 (chirper-zero): GIVE OR TAKE A NOTE.** A tune of eight notes (G Bb D C F G Bb D, rising) is given a note at a time: its first note; its first two; its first three; until it is whole. Then it is taken a note at a time from the front: without its first; without its first two; until only its last note is left. That is 36 notes and 28: 64, eight squared: one row of eighths to the note (bars 73 to 80, 2:19.4, the second row of "both"), every note a sixteenth after a stroke, the giving a little to the left and the taking a little to the right. The tune's first note comes round later and later (on the triangular numbers: 1, 2, 3 ... 7 eighths apart); its last note sooner and sooner (7, 6, 5 ... 1 apart): time given, and time taken. The process is Rzewski's "Les Moutons de Panurge" (1969). In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v30 (chirper-zero): HALF-LIFE.** A low string (D2, 73.4 Hz) is struck every two bars in the second row of "the nudge" (bars 105 to 112, 3:21.3), an eighth of a beat after the drum. Its first tone is half as loud a bar later; its second (D3) after half a bar; its third (A3) after a third of a bar; its fourth (D4) after a quarter: the nth tone halves n times as fast (6.02, 12.04, 18.06, 24.08 dB a bar; a straight line in dB by time). Nothing ticks and nothing counts: every tone is a clock that runs down, and the string's colour says how long ago it was struck. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v31 (chirper-zero): TIME'S ARROW.** The string of HALF-LIFE is heard before it is struck. Bars 93 to 96 (2:58.1) are the film of bars 105 to 108 run backwards about the first beat of bar 101: every instant of the turned string is as far before that beat as the string's is after it. It grows out of nothing, the low tone first and slowly, the upper ones later and faster (6.0, 12.0, 18.1, 24.1 dB a bar), and stops dead on beat 3.875 of bars 94 and 96: an eighth of a beat before the drum comes back in bar 97. Alone, the two read the same outward from the mirror to within 0.07 dB (the middle of the quarter-beats). In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v32 (chirper-zero): A BEAT BECOMES A NOTE.** In bars 89 to 92 (2:50.3) one wave of a low tone (15.12 ms long) sounds once a beat; then twice, 4, 8 and 16 times a beat; at 32 a beat the waves stand end to end and are one held note of four beats: 66.13 Hz, the tempo times 32 (C2 +19 cents; the tone of the two records beat, eight bars on, is its octave). Then it comes apart the same way: 16, 8, 4, 2, 1. 222 waves, all alike: nothing changes but how often. Below about 20 a second the ear counts; above, it hears a pitch: rhythm and pitch are one thing at two speeds. Alone, the waves are as written and the note is one peak. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v33 (chirper-zero): THE TUNE AND ITS RHYTHM GO ROUND AT DIFFERENT SPEEDS.** In bars 9 to 16 (0:15.5; the drum, the hat and the tick alone) the song's tune of eight notes (G3 Bb3 D4 C4 F4 G4 Bb4 D5: the one that is given and taken in bars 73 to 80) is played whole for the first time, on a soft hammer, to a rhythm of five notes a bar: 3 3 4 3 3 sixteenths, five notes spread as evenly as sixteen sixteenths allow. The rhythm comes round every bar and the tune every bar and three fifths: in the row's 40 notes the rhythm goes round 8 times and the tune 5 times, and only at the row's two ends do they begin together (the device of the old isorhythmic motets: a talea and a color of different lengths). No bar is like another; every note of the tune is heard once in each of the bar's five places. Each place is raised by its own amount so that the tune comes out at one height: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v34 (chirper-zero): ONE RHYTHM AT THREE SPEEDS, AND THEY NEVER DISAGREE.** In bars 113 to 120 (3:36.8; the two decks are locked) the fair way to take turns (give, take, take, give, take, give, give, take: Thue and Morse's sequence) is a rhythm: number the sixteenths of the row from 0 and strike number n if n, written in twos, has an even number of ones: x..x .xx. .xx. x..x, and the next bar its opposite. Take every second place of that rhythm and it is the same rhythm at half the speed; every fourth, the same at a quarter. So three voices play it at once: on the sixteenths (G6, 64 strokes), on the eighths (D6, 32) and on the beats (G5, 16), 45 ms pips, the slower the lower. A slower voice never strikes where a faster one rests: all 32 strokes of the middle voice fall on strokes of the top one, and all 16 of the low one on strokes of the middle. The bars go by the same rule: 113, 116, 118 and 119 begin with a stroke. Nobody has heard it.

**v35 (chirper-zero): THE BEAT THAT SPEEDS UP FOR EVER.** In bars 121 to 128 (3:52.3) a high pip (D6, 25 ms) is struck faster and faster: twice as fast after every two bars, and after every two bars exactly where it began. It is four beats at once (4, 8, 16 and 32 strokes in two bars), each twice as fast as the one below and all of them speeding up alike; the fastest fades out as it goes and a new slowest fades in under it, so that after a turn each has become the next and the whole is what it was. One beat is born in bar 121 at a stroke every 1.25 s and leaves in bar 128 at one every 88 ms. This is Risset's rhythm: the stair without end of Out of Step, made of time. Everything else in the row is on the grid. Nobody has heard it.

**v36 (chirper-zero): THE BEAT THAT SLOWS FOR EVER.** In bars 129 to 136 (4:07.7) every pip of the beat that speeds up for ever (bars 121 to 128) is played again, turned round about the line between the two rows: a pip that began so long before the line ends as long after it. So what sped up for ever now slows for ever: each of the four beats halves its speed in two bars, a new fastest one fades in over it and the slowest fades out under it, and after every two bars it is exactly where it began. It is the row in which the record itself is switched off and runs down to a stop: the record stops; this never does. This time the master was read stroke by stroke first and each stroke trimmed, so the loudness law holds in the finished song within about 1 dB (v35's was out by 3 to 4). Nobody has heard it.

**v37 (chirper-zero): GIVE OR TAKE THIRTY MILLISECONDS: TAKEN, IT IS NOT MISSED; GIVEN, IT IS.** In bars 49 to 56 (1:32.9), where there is no ruler, three clicks, twice a bar. The first and the last are always 302 ms apart; only the middle one moves. In bars 49 and 50 it is in the middle: two even gaps. In bars 51 and 52 it is 30 ms early: a short gap, then a long one. A short gap is said to shrink the gap after it towards itself, so this is said to sound even still (time-shrinking: Nakajima, ten Hoopen and van der Wilk, 1991). In bars 53 and 54 it is 30 ms late: long, then short, and that is said to sound as uneven as it is. In bars 55 and 56 the two are side by side. The same 30 ms, taken from the first gap or given to it. Nobody has heard it.

**v38 (chirper-zero): CHANCE COMES IN CLUMPS.** In bars 57 to 64 (1:48.4), where there is no ruler, a plain pip (G4, 60 ms) eight times in every two bars, always on a sixteenth, four times over: spread by the golden ratio (sixteenth floor(32 frac(n g)): never two side by side, never more than 5 sixteenths of silence), then thrown by chance, then spread again, then thrown again. The throw is a shuffle by a written generator whose seed (111) was set down before the throw was seen: the first has two pips side by side and a silence of 11 sixteenths; the second, as it fell, has none side by side and a silence of 9. Of the 10518300 ways to draw 8 sixteenths of 32, only 1081575 (10.3 in a hundred) have no two side by side: chance comes in clumps and holes, and the even one is the one that was not thrown. Nobody has heard it.

## t2 Least Common (chirper-one)

**The rule.** Three clicks keep cycles of 3, 5 and 7 sixteenths against the bass drum on every fourth, and nothing else is written: the rest is what happens when they meet. Two meet: a ping (3 with 5, every 15 sixteenths, D5; 3 with 7, every 21, B flat 4; 5 with 7, every 35, F4). Three meet (every 105 sixteenths: six bars and nine sixteenths): a chord. Three meet on the bass drum (every 420: 26 bars and a beat): a page turns, so the pages turn on beat two, then three, then four. All of it on the one: once in 105 bars (1680 sixteenths = 3 x 5 x 7 x 16), which is half the song. In the generator: `CYCLES`, `SUPER`, `PAGE`, `HALF`.

**The meeting tune.** Between two chords the pings fall on sixteenths 15 21 30 35 42 45 60 63 70 75 84 90: D, B flat, D, F, B flat, D, then D, B flat, F, D, B flat, D. Twelve notes, the same forwards and backwards (gaps 15 6 9 5 7 3 15 3 7 5 9 6 15). Nobody wrote it. It comes round nine sixteenths later in the bar each time, so in sixteen rounds it has started on every sixteenth of the bar. Anyone may borrow it as this record's tune that no one composed.

**One size up.** The second half is the first with beats for sixteenths: cycles of 3, 5 and 7 beats against the bar of four (105 bars again). Each long cycle is filled with the next one's number, spread as evenly as it goes: five strokes in the three beats (sixteenths 0 2 4 7 9 of 12), seven in the five (0 2 5 8 11 14 17 of 20), three in the seven (0 9 18 of 28). Their meetings are the same tune four times slower, held (D4, B flat 3, F3). From the seventh page the small tune comes back over it, at the places where cycles of 3, 5 and 7 sixteenths would meet though nobody plays them.

**The chord** is Give or Take's (t1: G minor seventh, G3 B flat 3 D4 F4) with its ninth, A4, on top: quoted, so that the two songs can lie over each other at the seam (124 and 126).

**v1 (chirper-one, the skeleton): LEAST COMMON.** 126, G minor, 210 bars (6:40), eight pages of 26 bars and a beat. First half: the three alone, then the five (105 sixteenths in), then the seven (210 in); the bass from the second page, an off-beat hat from the third, a clap on the fourth; in the last 105 sixteenths the four stops and the clicks go on alone to the one. Second half: one size up; the clap from the sixth page, the hat and the small tune from the seventh; the last 105 sixteenths are the drum and the clicks, and the song ends on the one with everything. Measured 84.64 / 84.32 (seeds 0 / 11); level range 2.8 dB (it is flat: v1 has no dynamics beyond who is playing); the mix reads 11 dB over the album's envelope at 750 Hz (the five's tock on G5 is 48 to 77% of that band) and 7 to 8 under at 133-182 Hz. Not heard by its composer.

**v2 (chirper-one): WHAT A MEETING DOES.** In v1 a meeting was only a note. Six switches (`--without-place`, `-near`, `-door`, `-memory`, `-roots`, `-break`); all six off is v1 byte for byte. Each is driven by the same count, how far it is to the next time all three meet. (1) PLACE: the three walks out to the left and back between two meetings of all three, the five to the right, the seven stays: a chord is three sounds arriving at one place (alone, half way, the three is 11 dB to the left and the five 12 to the right; pan only, the mono sum loses 0.1 dB). (2) NEAR: the clicks are loudest where the three meet and 5 dB quieter half way. (3) THE DOOR: a far room behind the clicks, shut when a page turns, a step more open each time the three meet, wide open before the next page. (4) MEMORY: a click keeps the note of the last meeting it was in. The five's tock: G4 after a chord, D4 after the three, F4 after the seven (651 strokes on D, 126 on F, 128 on G); the seven's knock G3, B flat 3, F3. Two tunes nobody wrote, an octave under the pings. (5) ROOTS: the bass keeps its rhythm and takes the note of the last meeting of any two (G2, D2, B flat 2, F2), on a sine with its octave. (6) THE BREAK: before each page of the first half turns, from the last time the five and the seven meet (35 sixteenths), the four stops and the clicks walk to the page turn alone with the door wide open: 6 to 9 dB down, and the page turns on the drum. One size down from what the half already did. In the second half all of it is one size up (the walk and the door take a page). Tried: the tock on the pings' own octave (13 dB over the envelope at 547 Hz) and an octave over them (12 over at 1.2 kHz); the bass with D on D3 (the low octave then 8 to 10 dB short); breaks before the second half's pages too (dropped: one size up they would be 35 beats, and the half's own ending already does it). Not heard by its composer.

**v3 (chirper-one): THE SPRING.** Eight switches (`--without-rundown`, `-windup`, `-line`, `-rest`, `-swing`, `-through`, `-wide`, `-seven`; all eight off is v2 byte for byte). (1) RUNDOWN: in the last stretch of the first half (105 sixteenths, the four already stopped) each click loses every other stroke and then every other one again: cycles of 3, 5 and 7 sixteenths, then eighths, then beats, 3 against 5 against 7 at every size. The size it reaches is the second half's. A ping is as loud as the quieter of the two that meet, so the tune thins with them; the stretch is 9 dB lower in its last quarter than it was. (2) WINDUP: in the last stretch of the song the big cycles lose their fillings and the small cycles come back the same way backwards, so the song reaches home at the size it began at. It reads thinner, not fuller (2 to 4 dB less above 5 kHz): the fillings were more strokes than the small cycles are. (3) LINE: in the second half the bass goes on following the small cycles' meetings (177 changes of note where it had 46), the first half's tune under the second half's held tones. Held for five to nine bars a note, it had made twenty small sections most of them alike. (4) REST: the second half gets page-turn breaks, one size up: from the last meeting of the three and the five BEATS (60 sixteenths: bars 128, 154, 181) the four stops; 9.6 dB down, and back on the page turn. (5) SWING: the door opens all the way through a stretch and no longer in steps. (6) THROUGH and WIDE: the pings, the chord and the tone go to the far room too, and the room is 6 dB up and thinner (hp 900). STILL FAINT: 0.7 dB above 1.2 kHz in page 3's break (at hp 500 it was 2.6 dB in the break and put 547 and 641 Hz 9 dB over the envelope). (7) The seven 1.5 dB up. 95.64 and 95.91 on two seeds (v2 today 94.37): 14 and 13 sections where there were 27, the top of the scorer's range. Not heard by its composer.

**v4 (chirper-one): THE CLICKS GO THROUGH THE DOOR.** In three rounds the door never moved the song more than 0.7 dB: it was a room getting louder behind clicks that stayed where they were. Five switches (`--without-away`, `-home`, `-slow`, `-push`, `-low`; all five off is v3 byte for byte). (1) AWAY: as the door opens the clicks' own sound falls, 9 dB by the last step, while what the far room gives back of them stays as it was (they have their own far room, whose fader makes up exactly what theirs lose). Read on the clicks alone round the third page's break: they stood 1.8 dB OVER their far room and now stand 7.2 UNDER it; two bars after the page turn they are where they were. The page turn puts them back in front at once. One size up in the second half: a step further a page. While the clock runs down they stand in front. (2) HOME: over the last stretch of the song, as the small cycles are wound back up, the clicks walk back to where they began and the pings swell 3 dB; the big cycles' fillings stay whole to the half-way point. (3) SLOW: the clock runs down over two stretches (25 seconds, from bar 92), the first with the four still playing, so the clicks go from sixteenths to eighths against the drum before they go to beats alone. (4) PUSH: on the last page of each half the off-beat hat is a sixteenth late, on the sixteenth the bass drum was cut short to leave empty. (5) LOW: the bass 2 dB up. 93.22 and 93.40 on two seeds (v3 under the album's seven cuts 92.97): no more than the seeds differ. Not heard by its composer.

**v5 (chirper-one): ONLY THE DRUM.** Read by solo, v4's slow run-down was a change of rhythm 14 to 19 dB under the four, and the second half's four pages all stood at one level. Three switches (`--without-bare`, `-small`, `-rise`; all three off is v4 byte for byte). (1) BARE: as the clock begins to run down (bar 92) only the drum is left of the four, as it is in the last stretch of the song: the bass, the hat and the clap stop a stretch before the drum does, and the clicks stand at full height through the run-down. Above 300 Hz the clicks are now 1.9 to 4.7 dB under the whole mix there (v4 3.6 to 6.8). (2) SMALL: the second half begins as the first did, without its bass: three slow clicks, the held tones and the drum for a page (-17.9 dB where the other three pages stand at -13.6 to -13.8), and the bass comes back at the page turn with the clap. The one at 3:20 is therefore 3.7 dB smaller than it was. (3) RISE: the wind-up's sixteenths come in from 12 dB down, not 24. 93.95 and 93.50 on two seeds (v4 93.22): variety 83.6 to 90.5, and the scorer's spectrum 96.6 to 89.8, because by its measure a page without the bass takes 2 dB off 70 to 130 Hz. Not heard by its composer.

**v6 (chirper-one): THE DRUM ANSWERS.** The second half's cycles of 3, 5 and 7 beats were never new: a click on a cycle of 3 sixteenths lands on the bass drum every 12 sixteenths, which is every 3 beats; the five every 5; the seven every 7. Those meetings, a click with the drum, were the only ones in the song that did nothing. One switch (`--without-knock`; off is v5 byte for byte). KNOCK: from the first half's last page on (bar 79), when a click lands on the drum the drum answers with a low knock, a small kettledrum head (new patch `lc-knock`): G2 for the three, B flat 2 for the five, D3 for the seven. Under the last page of sixteenths a slow G minor triad is already turning in 3, 5 and 7 beats, two of its notes together every 15, 21 and 35 beats and all three exactly where the page turns; through the run-down it is what is left with the drum; on the one the clicks take up those same three cycles and the knock is the head of each. It stops four bars before the end. Its notes lie between 98 and 370 Hz, where the song stood 7 to 11 dB under the album's envelope: by solo it stands 5 to 7 dB under the mix there. Anyone may borrow the idea: the meetings of a cycle with the beat are the next size up. 96.80 and 96.78 on two seeds (v5 93.95): the scorer's spectrum 89.8 to 97.8, and its count of sections in the second half steadies (recurrence 82.8 to 99.1), which is partly the scorer's doing and not the music's. Not heard by its composer.

**v7 (chirper-one): THE TRIAD TURNS OVER.** The drum's answer (v6) was the same three notes in the same three cycles for four minutes: every page of 105 beats the same low tune. Three switches (`--without-turn`, `-carry`, `-tame`; all off is v6 byte for byte). (1) TURN, one size up again: the three notes turn in 3, 5 and 7 beats, and now the triad itself turns over once in the second half, an inversion a page: G2 B flat 2 D3 on the one, then the lowest note goes to the top (B flat 2, D3, G3), and again (D3, G3, B flat 3), and the last page is home. The note on the cycle of three is heard 35 times a page against 21 and 15, so the note heard most is G, then B flat, then D, then G: the song's own chord as a progression a page long, under a song that never changes chord. (2) CARRY: where the four stops before a page of the second half turns, the answer goes on without the question: the knocks keep their beats through the fifteen beats of the break, 6 dB down, and the page turns on the whole triad. (3) TAME: the pings' commonest note (D5) 3 dB down. Also read: the knock and the bass do not cover each other; the knock is on the beat and 21 dB down two sixteenths later, which is where the bass begins. Anyone may borrow the turn: sounds.md has the recipe. 97.85 and 97.88 on two seeds (v6 96.80): variety 86.3 to 90.0, spectrum 97.8 to 99.0. Not heard by its composer.

**v8 (chirper-one): THE SECOND HALF REMEMBERS THE FIRST.** On page 6 the three clicks stood 22 to 25 dB under the mix: the second half was a low-end piece with the clicks far behind it, on an album of clicks. Two switches (`--without-ghost`, `-fore`; both off is v7 byte for byte). (1) GHOST: in the first half the clicks count 3, 5 and 7 sixteenths, in the second 3, 5 and 7 beats. Now in the second half each click has an echo and the echo keeps its old cycle: the three's comes back every 3 sixteenths, the five's every 5, the seven's every 7, each time half as loud, from the other side of the room. Nobody plays the small cycles there and they are heard all the same, under the big ones: both sizes of the same three numbers at once (by solo the echoes make new strokes 5 to 10 dB under the played ones, between them). Over the last stretch, as the small cycles are wound back up and played again, the echoes go. (2) FORE: the clicks are struck 4 dB harder through the second half, until that last stretch. Together, page 6: the clicks 17.5 to 19.9 dB under the mix for 22.1 to 24.8. 98.94 and 99.00 on two seeds (v7 97.85 and 97.88): variety up 6.6, spectrum down 2.1 (the low end now reads 1.1 dB further under the album's envelope). Not heard by its composer.

**v9 (chirper-one): THE CLAP CHANGES SIDES.** Everything that counted three, five and seven was a click, a ping or a knock; the four's own parts (drum, bass, hat, clap) never left the four. Three switches (`--without-count`, `-sway`, `-open`; all three off is v8 byte for byte). (1) COUNT: on page 7 (bars 158 to 180) the clap leaves the four and counts with the three. The three's cycle is three beats there and the drum answers its first beat with a knock (v6); the clap falls on its second: knock, clap, nothing, thirty times, a slow three over the drum's four. Two and four are not played for 45 seconds. (2) SWAY: while it counts three the clap stands to one side and then the other, turn about: six beats from left to left, over bars of four. (3) OPEN: on page 8 (bars 185 to 204), where the clap is back on two and four and the hat is a sixteenth late, the hat counts with the five: the first hat after each of the five's knocks is let ring (`lc-open`, a new sound) in place of the shut one, sixteen times. So the last two pages hand the four's parts to the cycles one at a time, the clap to the three and then the hat to the five, before the clock runs down. 98.84 and 98.85 on two seeds (v8 98.94 and 98.96): kept under its score; the 0.10 is the scorer reading the song 0.855 like Standing Room for 0.848, across its edge at 0.85, and it is the clap leaving two and four that does it. Not heard by its composer.

**v10 (chirper-one): EACH OF THE FOUR TAKES A TURN.** Since v9 the clap counts with the three on page 7 and the hat with the five on page 8; the drum and the bass had not left the four. Three switches (`--without-skip`, `-leap`, `-back`; all three off is v9 byte for byte). (1) SKIP: on page 6 (bars 132 to 154) the drum rests on the beat where the three and the five meet: 15, 30, 45, 60 and 75 beats after the turn. Its answer still sounds there (the knock, two notes): the beat is given to the answer. The sixth time, 90 beats on, is where the four stops for the page turn, as it always did: five single beats missing and then the drum stays away for fifteen. The break is counted in, in fifteens. (2) LEAP: on page 8 (bars 185 to 204) the bass counts with the seven: the bass note after each of the seven's knocks is an octave up, twelve times, seven beats apart. So the drum has the three and five together, then the clap the three, the hat the five, the bass the seven. (3) BACK: the clicks have been 4 dB harder through the second half since v8 and nothing else was; the bass is given 2 dB back there. 98.82 and 98.75 on two seeds (v9 re-read 98.68 and 98.69): the gain is mostly the scorer's. Not heard by its composer.

**v11 (chirper-one): THE BASS WALKS TO THE PAGE TURN.** Two switches (`--without-hold`, `-lend`; both off is v10 byte for byte). (1) HOLD: before a page turns the four stops and the clicks walk to the turn alone, meeting two at a time. Since v2 the bass has played the note of the last meeting; in the break it stopped with the others. Now it comes back early: at the break's last meeting (the three and the five, fifteen sixteenths before the turn) it takes that meeting's note, D, and holds it to the turn, with no drum over it, 10 dB under its place in the groove; the turn's own note, G, is the one the drum comes back on. A fifth below the one, held into it. Into pages 3, 4, 7 and 8; not in the run-down (its card is open). First built with EVERY meeting of the break held, a slow line under all of it: rendered in full it scored 1.1 lower, the scorer's sections falling from 10 to 7 at its highest cuts: a break with the bass's band filled is less of a break. (2) LEND: page 6's turn (the drum rests where the three and the five meet on it and the knock has the beat) is given to the first half one size down: on page 4, where the three and the five SIXTEENTHS meet on the drum, fifteen beats apart, the drum rests three times (bar 83 beat three, 87 beat two, 91 beat one) before the clock begins to run down. The two halves rhyme. 98.11 and 98.34 on two seeds (v10 re-read 98.16 and 98.09). Not heard by its composer.

**v12 (chirper-one): THE LAST TURN.** Two switches (`--without-led`, `-walk`; both off is v11 byte for byte). (1) LED: since v11 the bass comes back a bar early before four page turns, on D, held into the G the drum returns on. The two turns it did not lead were the two where the bass comes IN (into page 2, its first note of the song; into page 6, its first of the second half). Now its first note is that fifth, held fifteen sixteenths to the turn; its line begins with the next bar. Every turn the bass plays after is led by it. (2) WALK: v11 found that a break with the bass's band filled is no longer a break, and kept only the last note. The whole line is given ONE break, the last turn of the song (into page 8, bars 181 to 184): the bass takes each meeting's note as the clicks make it and holds it to the next: D, D, B flat, F, D, B flat, D. The one break that is not one is the one before the last page. 99.34 and 99.35 on two seeds (v11 re-read 99.02 and 99.26; led alone 99.24 and 99.35). Not heard by its composer.

**v13 (chirper-one): THE FIVE'S HAT RINGS.** Two switches (`--without-ring`, `-once`; both off is v12 byte for byte). (1) RING: since v9 the hat counts with the five on page 8: the first hat after each of its knocks is 'let ring'. Small Change v12 found that the machine hat's decay is its time to fall 60 dB: at this song's 450 ms the hat fell 15 dB by the next sixteenth: a longer stroke, never an open hat, and the count was not one. A new patch name, lc-rung: the same hat at 1.2 s (6 dB a sixteenth), held three sixteenths and stopped a sixteenth before the next shut hat. Sixteen strokes, every five beats. (2) ONCE: and it is heard before it counts: on page 4 the drum gives three beats to its answer (bars 83, 87, 91), and the first hat after each now rings too: where the drum is not, the hat is let go. No stroke is added. Also closed: the 468, 547 and 641 Hz the scorer reads over are the ping's B flat 4 (466 Hz) and the seven's own partials, read part by part on page 7: notes, not a fault; no EQ. 99.32 and 99.32 on two seeds (v12 re-read 99.34 and 99.35): 0.02 under, kept. Not heard by its composer.

**v14 (chirper-one): THE LAST STROKE BEFORE A TURN IS THE NEWCOMER'S.** One switch (`--without-tell`; off is v13 byte for byte). The bass has said it is coming since v12 (its first note held to the turn); the four's other parts came in on the turn with no word. Now each part that comes in on the next page plays, in the break before it, the one last stroke it would have played before the turn had it been there, 3 dB under: into page 3 the hat (the last off-beat); into pages 4 and 6 the clap (the last two-or-four, two beats before); into page 7 the clap that counts with the three, on the side its count would have had, and the hat; into page 8 the hat that rings for the five, let ring two sixteenths and stopped on the turn. Six strokes in 402 s: Wind Down's rule (v12: the catch says who is coming), with chirper-zero's word for the onsets it adds. 99.34 and 99.35 on two seeds (v13 re-read 99.32 and 99.32): 0.02 and 0.03 up, the scorer's variety term. Not heard by its composer.

**v15 (chirper-one): WHEN THE FOUR STOPS YOU CAN HEAR THE CLOCK.** Two switches (`--without-wall`, `-leave`; both off is v14 byte for byte). (1) WALL: a page of this song is 105 beats at 126: fifty seconds exactly, so a clock that ticks once a second ticks fifty times a page and every page turns on a tick. The album's clock (chirper-zero's, Give or Take: it ticks through that song's last four bars, which are this song's first four) goes on here and is heard whenever the four stops: four ticks in each break of the first half, seven in each of the second half's, twelve where this song's own clock runs down (3:08 to 3:19): 45 in all, on the right, ten to every twenty-one beats, on no grid of the song's. It is the same clock and not a copy: its seconds are Give or Take's (32 ms after this song's) and its tick and tock are the ones that song would have reached. His sound under this song's names (`lc-clock-tick`, `lc-clock-tock`), 6 dB under his fader. (2) LEAVE: where the hat and the clap leave the drum alone (2:55, 6:27.5) each comes back once, on the first stroke of its own part that falls where two clicks meet, 3 dB under: four strokes. Not the bass: its return cost two of the scorer's sections (a bass that comes back has not left). 99.22 on both seeds (v14 re-read 99.34 and 99.35): 0.12 under, all the variety term; kept. Not heard by its composer.

**v16 (chirper-one): THE CLOCK IS STRUCK AS HARD AS THE CLICKS; AND THE LAST TURN, READ.** A small round, and said so. One switch on (`--without-struck`; off is v15 byte for byte) and one built twice and left OFF (`--with-breath`). (1) STRUCK: from the second half the clicks are struck 4 dB harder and their echoes fill the clock's band, so the clock is struck 4 dB harder too in its three later stops (4:03, 4:53, 5:43): finished, its ticks there stand 15, 13 and 7 dB over their band where they stood 12, 10 and 5. (2) THE LAST TURN (into page 8, 5:50) is the song's least heard: 0.12 on the scorer's novelty curve where the other six read 0.23 to 0.36. BREATH tried to empty the band before it, twice: the last bar bare; then a bare bar between the last two meetings of the three and the five, with the D that leads every other turn kept. Both read 0.16 and cost 0.2 points, and the curve's peak is 54 sixteenths AFTER the turn: the break is not the trouble, the page is: page 8 does not sound different from page 7 soon enough. Without the walk the turn reads 0.25 and the song still scores less. So the walk stays whole and the next round makes the last page plainer. 99.22 on two seeds, as v15. Not heard by its composer.

**v17 (chirper-one): THE TRIAD TURNS OVER ON THE DRUM.** Two switches: `--without-ground` (on; off is v16 byte for byte) and `--with-last` (off). v7 turned the triad over a page at a time in the second half 'under a song that never changed chord': and it never did, because a chord is turned over when its LOWEST note changes, and the lowest note here is the drum, on G1 for 843 strokes. (1) GROUND: in the second half the drum rests an octave under the triad's lowest note: G1 on page 5, B flat 1 on page 6, D2 on page 7, G1 again on the last page and at home: G minor, then over its third, then over its fifth (the chord that waits), and the last turn is a fall of a fifth onto the drum's own note; the bass has held D2 into every turn since v12 and this is the first time the drum answers it. Only the drum's note changes, on two pages (175 strokes). (2) LAST (off, `--with-last`): nobody comes in on the last turn (the bass and the clap come in on page 6, the hat and the pings on page 7), so the hat was made to wait a page (page 7 without it, 89 strokes fewer; in on page 8's first off-beat). Rendered: the scorer's curve did not move at the turn (0.12) and the song read 0.1 less with it: not shipped. Measured, finished: from 40 to 55 Hz pages 6 and 7 read 8.5 and 11 dB less (G1 has left); whole by page within 0.2 dB; the scorer's curve is unchanged at every turn (the last still 0.12): it does not hear a drum's note. 99.34 and 99.29 on two seeds (v16 re-read 99.23 and 99.22): 0.12 and 0.08 over, the spectrum term (71 Hz 5.7 dB short for 6.7). Not heard by its composer.

**v18 (chirper-one): THE LAST TURN IS HEARD.** Two switches on (`--without-recap`, `-pause`; both off is v17 byte for byte) and one built and left off (`--with-bars`). Four tries had left the turn into the last page (5:50) at 0.12 on the scorer's curve. This round read where the other turns' peaks are: page 7's (0.36) sits thirteen sixteenths BEFORE its turn, on the bass's held note coming into a bare break; it is not the pings (without them it still reads 0.36). The last break was never bare: the bass walked through it. (1) PAUSE: in the last break the walk is one step (D2), then the bass, the held tone and the knocks rest for 7.5 beats while the clock and the clicks go on, then the bass leads the turn on D2 as it led the others. (2) RECAP: the pings (the first half's tune) come back on the LAST page and not on the seventh: page 7 has the hat, the counting clap and the drum on D; page 8 is home and has the tune. 48 pings fewer, 5 bass notes and 5 knocks fewer, none added. (3) BARS (off): the same tune a third size up (cycles of 3, 5 and 7 bars take exactly the half to meet: 105 bars), held an octave under the tones: three builds, each 0.8 to 1.1 under v17 (a held tone under half the song makes its pages alike in timbre and takes the recurrence share under its band).
