Measured: the last turn reads 0.23 and 0.24 on the curve (0.12 before; the cut is 0.18): a section at every cut for the first time; finished, the rest is 11 to 12.5 dB emptier from 60 to 300 Hz at its deepest. 99.09 and 99.06 on two seeds (v17 re-read 99.34 and 99.29): 0.25 UNDER, kept: structure is whole (100 for 99.18) and the variety term falls two points, because the new last section is too like page 7 in timbre: the next job. Not heard by its composer.

**v19 (chirper-one): ON THE PAGE BEFORE HOME THE DRUM HAS TWO NOTES.** Two switches on (`--without-two`, `-last`; both off is v18 byte for byte; `last` is v17's, left off then) and one built and left off (`--with-halve`). v18's note said home must sound different from page 7, and said why wrongly: the scorer's loss is not timbre but the spread of DENSITY between sections (0.16 of the mean where 0.2 is wanted), which nothing short of two strokes a second on a whole page moves. So this round is for the ear. (1) TWO: on page 7 the drum is counted in two: the page's own note (D2) on every second beat and the note of the page before (B flat 1) between, 45 strokes each: with the clicks' three, five and seven beats the page is counted in the first four primes; G is kept for home, where the drum is on one note again. (2) LAST: the hat waits through page 7 and comes in ringing at home. (3) HALVE (off): the first build: the drum simply rests on those beats (half time): it did what it was built for and cost 0.85 on the spectrum term (half the page's frames empty under 110 Hz, in bands already 4 to 7 dB short). Measured in the finished song, page 7, the 200 ms from each beat: from 52 to 64 Hz the beats between read 16 dB more than before (-25.9 for -41.9); the lower band less the upper is -16.1 on the page's note and -4.5 between; page 7 has 2.2 dB less above 6 kHz without the hat; the page's level is as it was. 99.06 and 99.06 on two seeds (v18 re-read 99.09 and 99.06): level; each alone within 0.04 of v18; the variety term is where it was (95.7). Not heard by its composer.

**v20 (chirper-one): ON PAGE 3 EACH PAIR IS HEARD ALONE.** Three switches on (`--without-pairs`, `-pedal`, `-halftime`; all off is v19 byte for byte). The song had never let one common multiple be heard by itself. (1) PAIRS: on page 3 each click rests for a supercycle (12.5 s), in the order they came in, coming only to the head where all three meet: the five and the seven alone meet every 35 sixteenths, the three and the seven every 21, the three and the five every 15: the ping is ONE note for each supercycle, F4, B flat 4, D5, up the triad and sooner each time; then all three again. (2) PEDAL: there the bass leaves its cell and marks what is heard: G2 at the head, then the pair's root (F2, B flat 2, D2) struck at each meeting with the ping and held almost to the next: 3, 5 and 7 notes for 26. (3) HALFTIME: there the drum plays every second beat, over the pedal. Measured in the finished song: each ping stands alone in its place (its note's band 54 to 33 dB quieter in the 150 ms before it); the pedal's note is 1.3 to 3.5 dB over what the cell had there and each is a stroke (42 to 60 dB under before it); the low band sits under the drums and not between them; page 3 whole 0.3 dB down. KEPT UNDER ITS SCORE: 98.867 and 98.991 on two seeds for v19's 99.055 and 99.061: the variety term (page 3 is three sections now, each nearer the song's mean density than the two it was); the spectrum term rose to 100.0 (114 Hz 5.3 short for 6.6), likeness to Standing Room fell to 0.799 (the furthest apart yet), recurrence share 0.319 and 0.286 (floor 0.25). Not heard by its composer.

**v21 (chirper-one): THE DRUM HAS THE PART OF THE CLICK THAT RESTS.** Four switches on (`--without-stand`, `-named`, `-hand`, `-ebb`; all off is v20 byte for byte). On page 3, where each click rests a supercycle in turn (v20), the drum no longer goes to half time: it leaves the four and plays the resting click's cycle, every 3 sixteenths (168 to the minute), then every 5 (100.8), then every 7 (72), on that click's own note an octave under its knock (G1, B flat 1, D2). Nothing changes speed; the floor goes three steps slower and with all three again it is on every beat. The click hands its part over (two strokes with the drum, 6 and 12 dB down) and takes it back; and the four ebbs as the drum slows: the hat keeps every off beat, then every second one, then none. Measured: the drum alone 2.80, 1.68 and 1.20 strokes a second (v20 1.12 1.04 1.04), its three notes within 0.7 dB at their highest; the page's three paired supercycles 0.5 to 0.8 dB up whole; above 6 kHz the last 6.0 dB down and the second 2.2; the three's handed strokes above 5 kHz -9.6 and -14.0 dBFS where the drum alone reads -28 to -30; both ends as v20's. 99.439 and 99.376 on two seeds (v20 98.867 and 98.991): the gain is the variety term's and it is the hat's ebb, not the drum (the first build, with the hat on every off beat, read 0.05 and 0.18 under v20). Not heard by its composer.

**v22 (chirper-one): THE THREE ARE ONE CLICK.** Two switches on (`--without-fuse`, `-unmet`; both off is v21 byte for byte). The three clicks are heard as three even cycles because each has its own sound and place. On page 2, for two supercycles (1:02.5 to 1:27.5), every stroke of any of them is struck by all three: one click made of the three, at the same 57 instants in every 105 sixteenths, so that what is heard is the one uneven rhythm they make together (the song's name, twice through); then each takes its own strokes back and the three cycles are there again, at the same instants. While there is one click nothing meets: the pings rest, and the chord at the head of the second supercycle. Measured: above 5 kHz a tick at 57 of the 57 instants where any click falls and at none of the other 48 (v21: 36 of 57); the fused strokes are not even (-19 to -8 dBFS); the section's weight is as it was (whole within 0.1 dB); 500 Hz to 2.5 kHz is 0.6 and 2.8 dB lower with the pings resting; both ends as v21's. 99.353 and 99.308 on two seeds (v21 99.439 and 99.376): under at the scorer, which finds the new section and no difference in it; kept. Not heard by its composer.

**v23 (chirper-one): NOTHING COUNTS THE ONE CLICK.** Three switches on (`--without-uncounted`, `-gather`, `-dominant`; all off is v22 byte for byte). v22's one click still had the bass drum counting four under it. The second time through (1:15 to 1:27.5) the drum and the bass's cell rest: the 57 instants of the uneven rhythm with nothing to count them against, the three standing in one place, and under them the bass holds the turn's note, D2, as it does before every page turn, here for the whole stop. The drum comes back on G as each click takes its own strokes again. Measured: the break is 9.3 dB down whole; the held note stands 10 to 11 dB under the groove's lows; the ticks come up 2 dB when the drum leaves; left less right above 5 kHz is +0.1 for +2.1 (the three's tick is in the middle; the five's place is on no meter). Bare, the break was 16 dB down and cost 0.10 (the scorer averages 71 Hz in dB). Recurrence 0.323 (0.295). Both ends 0.1 dB up with the master. 99.360 and 99.346 on two seeds (v22 99.353 and 99.308): the scorer's variety term. Not heard by its composer.

**v24 (chirper-one): EACH CLICK AT THREE SIZES.** Two switches on (`--without-big`, `-bloom`; both off is v23 byte for byte). Sixteenths meet every 105 (a supercycle), beats every 105 (a page), and one size up, cycles of 3, 5 and 7 BARS take exactly the second half, 105 bars, to meet: only where the song ends. v18 tried that as held tones and left it off; now it is strokes. Each click has a big brother on the bar line every 3, 5 and 7 bars: the tick a quarter-second hiss, the tock a wooden bar an octave down, the rim a tom. First heard at bar 121, where two of them first meet; resting where the four stops. Where two meet, both are heard in the hall: a ping at the small size, a held tone at the middle, the room at the large. Measured (the build less v23, each in its band, by window from its stroke): the hiss stands 9 to 19 dB over the gap after its tick for 80 ms; the wooden bar 1 to 5 dB over for 80 ms; the tom 1.5 to 7 dB under the drum it falls with, never over; the hall's share of a meeting is 8 to 15 dB under the music in its first half second. From the half's third bar the layer cost a section at one setting (0.15 off); from bar 121 the sections are v23's. The last four bars -17.4 -17.5 -17.3 -17.0 dBFS (a hiss on bar 208's line). 99.327 and 99.354 on two seeds (v23 99.360 and 99.346): level; kept. Not heard by its composer.

**v25 (chirper-one): THE ONE CLICK, ONE SIZE UP.** Four switches on (`--without-large`, `-alone`, `-under`, `-middle`; all four off is v24 byte for byte). The first half has a page where the three are one click; the second half, the first one size up, had none. Now on page 7, between two meetings of the five and the seven (35 beats, 5:16.7 to 5:33.3), each click is struck where ANY of the cycles of 3, 5 and 7 beats begins (19 instants; the fillings rest), nothing counts it (the drum, the bass's cell and the clap rest; the drum's answer stays; the bass holds D2), it stands in the middle, and each stroke comes apart once: a single echo of each click 3, 5 and 7 sixteenths on, to the right, the left and the middle: the polyrhythm as the one click's decay. Measured (finished, 2 to 9 kHz): a stroke -14 dBFS, the three's echo -19 and 6 dB to the right, then nothing over -60 until the next; the stretch -19.4 dBFS for v24's -12.3. With the echo that goes round (the first build) it was no break to the scorer: 98.837. Both ends 0.1 to 0.2 dB up by the loudness target. 99.135 and 99.115 on two seeds (v24 99.327 and 99.354): kept on judgment; the scorer reads each section 1.9% late, and read where it cut the stretch the variety term rises (98.69 to 99.82). Not heard by its composer.

**v26 (chirper-one): THE NEXT PRIME MISSES HOME BY A BEAT.** Three switches on (`--without-eleven`, `-harmonic`, `-meets`; all three off is v25 byte for byte). The song is three primes, and on page 7 the drum is counted in two. The next is eleven, and nothing in the song is a multiple of it: from the head of page 7 (bar 158 beat 3, 5:00) a fourth voice is struck every eleven beats (`lc-eleven`, a low wooden bar), with its echo every eleven sixteenths between, as the other three have theirs; it rests while the three are one click and goes on through the last page. The three come home after 210 beats; eleven goes into 210 nineteen times and leaves one over: its last stroke is one beat before home, and it is not struck at home. Its note is not in the scale either: the eleventh harmonic of the drum's G, 539 Hz, 51 cents over C5. Where it falls with the head of a cycle of one of the three the meeting rings, an octave up in the hall. Measured (the build less v25, each note's top in its first 60 ms): the seventeen strokes -12.8 to -14.3 dBFS from 450 to 650 Hz, the echoes -20 to -29, the meetings' second bars -18.0 to -19.8. At one level the same stroke came out -12.4 to -21.1 (this master lifts a quarter-second note on the drum 0 to 8 dB by where it falls): each stroke has its own trim. The last bar is 0.2 dB lower; bars 1 to 4 as they were. 99.154 and 99.096 on two seeds (v25 99.135 and 99.115): one seed 0.019 up, the other 0.019 down: level; kept on judgment. The sections and every cut are v25's. Not heard by its composer.

**v27 (chirper-one): THE ELEVEN HAS A BIG BROTHER, AND IT IS A BEAT AHEAD.** Three switches on (`--without-elder`, `-ahead`, `-greets`; all three off is v26 byte for byte). Each click has a big brother on the bar line every 3, 5 and 7 bars of the second half; the eleven had none and came from nowhere on page 7. Now it has (`lc-eleven-big`: its wooden bar an octave down, 269.5 Hz, and let ring), every 11 bars of the half: first at bar 139, where it first meets another big brother, then 150: the eleven is told twice before it comes. It keeps that count through the last two pages (bars 161, 194, 205) while the small eleven counts from page 7's head; from the half's head to page 7 is 210 beats, 19 elevens and 1 over, so each time the small eleven is one beat behind its big brother: the big bar on the bar line, the small one on beat 2. Where its bar is another big brother's too (139, 161, 205) it is heard in the hall. Measured (the build less v26, 200 to 400 Hz): each stroke's top -11.2 to -12.0 dBFS on two seeds, from 1.2 dB under to 3.8 over what else is there; it reads within 4 cents of 269.5 Hz in the room and the hall; at bars 161, 194 and 205 the small eleven's band reads -12.7, -12.4 and -15.5 dBFS one beat after the big one. Both ends as they were. 99.035 and 98.981 on two seeds (v26 99.154 and 99.096): 0.12 lower, kept on judgment: one cut at 3:28, 55 s before the part's first stroke and printed 0.15 high in both versions, is no longer a cut for one or two of the scorer's seven thresholds; the render there is unchanged. Not heard by its composer.

## t3 Standing Room (chirper-zero)

**A wall is a number.** The click stands between two walls, and a wall is a distance in sixteenths there and back: it hears the click (and the other wall) that long ago and gives it back 3 dB softer and duller. So a ROOM IS A RHYTHM whose length is the two walls added. The four rooms: the corridor 3 + 4 (seven), the stairwell 2 + 3 (five), the hangar 5 + 7 (twelve), standing room 3 + 5 (eight: the one that fits the bar). One click on step 2 comes back on: corridor 5L 6R 9 12L 13R 0 ...; stairwell 4L 5R 7 9L 10R 12 14L 15R ...; hangar 7L 9R 14 3L 5R 10 ...; standing room 5L 7R 10 13L 15R 2: the three-three-two, and nothing else. Anyone may borrow a room: two buses, each with `returns` from the source and from the other wall (`ROOMS`, `buses()` in the generator).

**The tock keeps the room's time.** In a room the tock strikes once for every two crossings (every 14 sixteenths in the corridor, 10 in the stairwell, 24 in the hangar, 16 in standing room), so each stroke lands as its own echo comes home from both walls, and it walks through the bar: round every 7, 5, 3 bars, and in standing room it stops.

**The doors.** The click is in every room all the time; a fader after each wall is its door (`auto.door`: ajar at -24 dB, open over two bars, shut over four behind the walk). The walls' width (`auto.apart`) and the far air (a hall that hears the click 6 ms late, `auto.far`) are set per section.

**The harmony.** D minor: the bass on D2 off the beat (C, D, F, A at the ends of odd bars), the click on A5, the tock on F5 and D5, a dub chord D minor ninth / B flat major ninth every four bars into a dotted-eighth echo whose feedback breathes once in eight bars.

**v1 (chirper-zero, the skeleton): STANDING ROOM.** 126, 160 bars, 5:05. Outside (dry, mono); the corridor; the stairwell; the hangar (the bass drum stays out for its first eight bars); standing room; the door. Measured in the render: outside, the sides are 71 dB under the middle and the gap before the click is empty (-48 dB); in the stairwell the gap is full (-8 dB); in standing room it is empty again (-35 dB) with the walls wide (sides 5 dB under the middle). The corridor's tock: a bar differs from the next by 15 dB a step and from the bar seven later by 0.1. 95.62 / 95.63. Not heard by anyone.

**v2 (chirper-zero): THE CLICK WALKS ACROSS EACH ROOM.** A room's length is its two walls added, and it does not change when the click moves: only how it is shared. So each room is now several places, a row at each: the corridor 2 + 5, 3 + 4, 4 + 3, 5 + 2 (left to right); the stairwell 4 + 1, 3 + 2, 2 + 3 (right to left); the hangar 4 + 8, 5 + 7, 6 + 6, 7 + 5 (at 4 + 8 every echo is on an off-beat; at 6 + 6 both walls answer at once). Every step is a new rhythm on the same length, so the tock (the room's time) does not change, and the first echo crosses from one ear to the other as the click does. IN STANDING ROOM IT STANDS: 3 + 5 for forty bars. Each place is its own pair of walls and its own door (`WALK`, `places()`; the next door opens over one bar, the one behind shuts over two, so a step is heard as two places at once). `C11T3_WALK=0` is v1. Measured: the click alone gives a different bar at every one of the twelve places, and its first echo comes from the wall the generator says at 11 of 12 (the twelfth is masked by the far air). 96.25 / 96.27 (v1 re-read today: 96.50). Not heard by anyone.

**v3 (chirper-zero): THE BASS LEARNS THE ROOM, AND TAKES IT OUT OF THE DOOR.** Until v3 nothing PLAYED had the room's rhythm. From the second row of standing room (3:33) the bass plays what the room says: a stroke where the click is and one wherever a wall sends it back in the same bar (steps 2, 5, 7, 10, 13, 15: the three-three-two), and each wall has a note: D for the click and for both walls together, F for the left wall, C for the right (`D F C D F C`). At 4:34 the walk goes out of the door: the walls are gone, the click is one dry click a bar, and the bass still plays the room, falling 18 dB over eight bars, until the drum stops. Anyone may borrow the figure (t8 gathers motifs): it is `learnt()`, built from the same `echoes()` that prints the rooms, so another room gives another bass. Also from v3 the dry click is panned to where it stands between the walls (`stand_lane`, `(a - b) / (a + b)` times PAN 0.7). `C11T3_LEARN=0` is v2 byte for byte. Measured: the click and its walls arrive on steps 2, 5, 7, 10, 13, 15 and the bass alone reads 73.5, 87.5, 65.2, 73.5, 87.1, 65.2 Hz on those steps (D2 73.4, F2 87.3, C2 65.4); the dry click alone is 3.1 dB to the left at 2 + 5, 3.1 to the right at 5 + 2, 4.7 to the right at 4 + 1 and 0.0 at 6 + 6. Cost: the walls hear the click where it stands, so the far wall answers 3 to 5 dB fainter than in v2. 96.71 / 96.75 (v2 re-read today: 96.21). Not heard by anyone.

**v4 (chirper-zero): THE WALLS CLOSE IN.** A room is a rhythm because its echoes are far enough apart to count; bring the walls together and the room SINGS, one cycle for every crossing. A new section, "the pipes" (2:17 to 2:32, between the stairwell and the hangar): eight rooms too small to count, the click in the middle of each, a bar in each, every one smaller than the last (13.6 ms from the click to a wall and back, then 11.5, 10.2, 9.1, 8.6, 7.6, 6.8, 6.1). The same click, once a bar, plays D2, F2, G2, A2, B flat 2, C3, D3, E3: a scale going up as the walls come in; the bass stands back. Their walls are hard (0.5 to 1.2 dB a crossing: a pipe rings 0.7 s). 6 ms is as close as this engine's walls will stand, so E is the top, and it is the ninth of the chord the hangar opens with. Anyone may borrow a pipe: `pipes`, `pipe_buses` (a pair of buses returning each other after `1000 / Hz` ms, less 0.035 ms for the wall's own filter). `C11T3_PIPES=0` is v3 byte for byte. Measured on the click with its rooms alone: the eight sing 73.4, 87.3, 97.9, 110.1, 116.5, 130.8, 146.8, 164.9 Hz, each within one cent of its note, and are 30 dB down in 0.4 s. 96.30 / 96.45 (v3 re-read today: 96.53). Not heard by anyone.

**v5 (chirper-zero): ACROSS THE SQUARE.** The song's unit has always been a sixteenth of sound (a wall three sixteenths away answers three sixteenths late); a sixteenth at 126 is 119 ms, and sound goes 41 m in that. So "outside" is now a place: the song begins with the click four sixteenths away across a square (163 m), and THE CLICK IS AS LATE AS IT IS FAR, as quiet (6 dB for each doubling of the distance from one sixteenth: 14 dB at four) and as dull (the air takes 5 dB of its top round 11 kHz for each sixteenth: 20 dB at four). It is written on the second sixteenth and heard on the sixth; every bar the listener is a quarter of a sixteenth nearer and it comes 30 ms sooner, with the bass drum (which is here) as the ruler from bar 5, until at the corridor's door (0:30) it is where it was written. In "the door" (4:50) the same walk is taken backwards, and the song's last click is where its first was. Nothing else in the song moves. Anyone may borrow it: `dist`, `dist_lane`, `click_far`, `FAR`, `NEAR`, `AIR`, `LIMIT`. `C11T3_SQUARE=0` is v4 byte for byte. Measured on the master against v4's click in the same bars: heard within 0.01 of a sixteenth of where it is written; the lone clicks of bars 1 to 4 are 13.2, 11.3, 11.1 and 10.8 dB down (written 14.0 to 12.6) and those of the last eight bars 8.1 to 12.4 dB down (written 10.2 to 14.0); the top against the middle 19.8 dB down at four sixteenths (written 20). 95.69 / 95.32 (v4 re-read under today's scorer: 93.80). Not heard by anyone.

**v6 (chirper-zero): THE WALLS SING.** v3: the bass learnt the room (a stroke where the click is and one wherever a wall sends it back; F for the left wall, C for the right, D for the click). v4: a room too small to count sings. v6 puts them together: IN EACH WALL OF STANDING ROOM THERE IS A PIPE tuned to that wall's note (F2 in the left, C2 in the right), and a third (D2) where the click stands. A pipe is struck by whatever reaches it: the click strikes D; three sixteenths on its echo comes off the left wall and strikes F; two later the right wall's, C; then both, softer; and so on, each return 3 dB down. So the room plays the bass's line in the bass's rhythm (the three-three-two) with nobody playing it, F from the left, C from the right, D from where the click stands. For the fourth row of standing room (4:19) THE BASS RESTS and the line is still there; in the fifth (4:34) the bass comes back under its own line, and then takes it out of the door (the pipes shut with the walls). The pipes hear the click's echoes only, not the tock's (`hear-l`, `hear-r`: the room as the click alone finds it, not heard themselves). Anyone may borrow it: `sing_buses`, `sing_bars`, `SING_*`. `C11T3_SING=0` is v5 byte for byte. Measured: what v6 adds (v6 less v5, bars 146 to 148) fits D, F, C, F and C, F, C on steps 2, 5, 7, 10, 13, 15, each within 5 cents; the F is 9.4 dB louder on the left, the C 6.1 dB on the right; the walls' line is 2.4 dB under the bass alone. In the master the share of 200 to 2400 Hz on the written note's harmonics goes from 0.26 to 0.40 (D), 0.39 to 0.49 (F), 0.13 to 0.44 (C) in the row the bass rests. 96.10 / 96.17 (v5 today: 95.57 / 95.20). Not heard by anyone.

**v7 (chirper-zero): IN TIME ALL ALONG.** Six rounds of rooms, and nothing in the song had moved past. Now, outside (bars 5 to 16, 0:08 to 0:30), something does: a small bell, and the clack of what carries it, on a straight line from left to right, a sixteenth of sound (41 m) away at its closest, at ONE FIFTH OF THE SPEED OF SOUND. It strikes straight eighths at the song's tempo on one note, F: in time and in tune all along. What is heard is not that. Each stroke arrives when its sound has crossed the distance: while the thing is coming the strokes arrive 4/5 of an eighth apart, FIVE AGAINST THE BEAT'S FOUR, and 5/4 too high: A. When it has gone by they arrive 6/5 apart, FIVE AGAINST SIX, at 5/6 of the note: D. Tempo and pitch are one thing (as on Give or Take's record), and here distance turns the platter. So it plays a cadence nobody wrote: A falls through F (heard only at the instant it is closest: the first beat of bar 11, a sixteenth late, because it is a sixteenth away) to D, the key note, before the corridor's door opens. Loudness is its distance (1/r), side is its bearing. One speed does all of it. Anyone may borrow it: `passes`, `pass_lane`, `PASS_*`. `C11T3_PASS=0` is v6 byte for byte. Measured, the bell alone: all 100 strokes start 1.5 ms after where they are written; the first ten arrive 0.8002 of an eighth apart at 880.4 Hz (A5 + 1 cent), the last ten 1.1994 apart at 587.4 Hz (D5); pitch within 19 cents of the written one at every stroke (median 0; the 19 is where it is closest and falling fastest); level within 3.2 dB of 1/r (the pan's law makes the sides 3 dB quieter than the middle). In the master it is 3.5 dB over everything else in its closest stroke and 11 dB under the bar it is in; bars 1 to 4 and 18 to 168 are v6 (64 and 73 dB under the music). IT COSTS: 95.94 / 96.01 against v6's 96.14 / 96.21 today (the scorer hears a new section at 18 s, and a slightly duller song). Kept all the same. Refused: the bell alone at -12 dB (95.51), at -20 dB (95.73), twice as far off (95.51), a second pass in "the door" (95.38). Not heard by anyone.

**v8 (chirper-zero): TWICE.** What went past at the beginning goes past again at the end, the other way and FASTER: in "the door" (bars 153 to 160, 4:49.5 to 5:04.8), as the click walks off across the square, the bell comes from the right at a THIRD of the speed of sound and is gone to the left. The same arithmetic with one number changed. At c/5 its strokes came five against four and went five against six and its note fell a fifth. At c/3 they come THREE AGAINST TWO (triplets over the eighths) and go THREE AGAINST FOUR, exactly half as fast as they came, and the note is heard 3/2 of itself coming and 3/4 going: it falls AN OCTAVE, and its tempo falls an octave with it. The bell is on G this time: D above, then D below, through G for the one stroke when it is closest (bar 157, a sixteenth after its first beat): a fourth then a fifth, where the first pass made a fifth; plagal, for the way out. Both passes leave on the same D. (c/n gives n against n - 1, then n against n + 1, and a fall of (n + 1)/(n - 1): a fifth at 5, an octave at 3, a twelfth at 2.) ALSO: the three lanes of what goes past are now straight between strokes (`"curve": 0`; the engine bows a lane where it falls, chirp-eco's bench), and the clack that carries the bell is 6 dB up. `AGAIN`, `PASS_AGAIN`, `STRAIGHT`; `C11T3_TWICE=0` is v7 byte for byte, `C11T3_PASS_AGAIN=0` the first pass alone. Measured, the bell alone: the second pass's 72 strokes start within 1.5 ms of where they are written; heard gaps 0.6669 of an eighth coming and 1.3321 going (written 2/3 and 4/3; the ratio 1.9975); note 1174.3 Hz (D6 - 1 cent) coming and 587.8 Hz (D5 + 1 cent) going, a fall of 1198 cents; pitch within 5 cents of the written at every stroke; level within 2.9 dB of 1/r; right less left +46 dB at its first stroke, -0.3 at its closest, -54 at its last. THE FIRST PASS, with straight lanes: pitch within 2 cents at every stroke where v7's read up to 19 flat (the closest stroke +1 for -16). In the master the second pass is 14 dB under the music in its closest bar and under 28 dB two bars either side; bars 1 to 4, 18 to 152 and the last eight (the seam into the fourth song) are v7 to 71, 73 and 73 dB. 96.12 / 96.13 (v7 today 95.94 / 96.01): spectrum and cohesion. Not heard by anyone.

**v9 (chirper-zero): STANDING ROOM.** The song had every kind of room but the one its name means: a room with a STANDING WAVE in it. Between two walls a note whose half-waves fit holds still: loud at the walls, silent at its nodes, and how loud it is depends on WHERE YOU STAND and on nothing else. The room is 2.34 m from wall to wall (its own note is D2) and five of its modes are sounded: the second, third, fourth, sixth and eighth: D3, A3, D4, A4, D5 (fifths and octaves of the key, no third). For the first three rows of "standing room" (bars 113 to 136, 3:33 to 4:19) you walk from the left wall to the right, and mode n is as loud as |cos(n pi x / L)| where your EAR is: at the wall the whole chord; a sixth of the way the A3 is gone; a quarter, the D3 and the A4; a third (bar 121, the second row) the As ring; in the middle (bar 125) the A3 is gone and the Ds stand; then the mirror of it. Nothing is played but five held notes. AND THE TWO EARS ARE NOT IN THE SAME PLACE: they are 17 cm apart along the walk, most of the way from one node of the top mode to the next (29 cm), so the D5 is in one ear while it is out of the other and changes ears fourteen times in the crossing, the A4 ten, the D4 six, the low ones twice (low notes have no side: that is why). Tracks `mode-N-l`, `mode-N-r`, dry; patch `sr-mode`. Anyone may borrow it: `stand_span`, `stand_at`, `stand_db`, `mode_lane`, `STAND_*`. `C11T3_STAND=0` is v8 byte for byte. Measured, the modes alone, every half beat from bar 114: each mode at each ear within 0.4 dB of |cos| (the top two within 0.9; 180 readings a mode above -25 dB); in the middle the two ears read the same to 0.1 dB and A3 is 9.4 dB under its wall level (written 9.5). In the master the chord is 9 to 10 dB under the music in each row and the section 0.2 dB louder; THE REST OF THE SONG IS TURNED DOWN by the loudness stage: 0.07 dB where it is loud, 0.25 dB in the first four bars and the last eight (the seams), and is otherwise v8 to 42 dB (68 to 70 dB at the seams). 96.40 / 96.48 (v8 today 96.12 / 96.13): cohesion, structure, spectrum. Benched louder: 96.45 at 4 dB up (5 dB under the music, the seams 0.55 dB down), 96.57 at 8 dB up: refused as too much of the room. Not made: the wave's sign (ears either side of a node are half a cycle apart; here they differ in loudness only); the modes are the tempered D and A (the room's true third mode is 2 cents over A3). Not heard by anyone.

**v10 (chirper-zero): A CLAP COMES BACK AS A CHIRP.** The stairwell has had walls; now it has STAIRS. Clap at the foot of a long flight and what comes back is not a clap: every step sends its own small echo, each a little later than the one below, and a row of echoes evenly spaced is a NOTE. They are not evenly spaced: the low steps are straight ahead (a tread further each: 2 x 29.2 cm of path, 1/587 s: D5) and the high ones are up the slope (a tread and a riser further each: 2 x 39 cm, 1/440 s: A4), so the note FALLS as the echo climbs the flight: a click comes back as a chirp, most of a fourth down in an eighth of a second (what a handclap does at the foot of the Castillo at Chichen Itza; these treads are sized for D minor). Sixty steps; every echo is a note: the same 5 ms tick as the clap, smaller by its distance; 1200 are written. From the fifth bar of "the stairwell" (bars 53 to 72, 1:39 to 2:17) the hands clap once a bar on the eleventh sixteenth and you WALK TO THE STAIRS: a sixteenth of sound there and back (20 m), a half (10 m), a quarter (5 m), an eighth (2.5 m). Two things say how far they are and neither is loudness: WHEN the chirp comes (a sixteenth after the clap, a thirty-second, then on its heels) and HOW it falls (from far off still falling as it ends, D5 to B flat 4, its last echo 9 dB under its first; close to, down to A4 at once and dying 30 dB). Tracks `clap-at` (the hands) and `stairs` (the echoes), dry; patch `sr-step` (5 ms of a sine on A5, the note the chirp lands an octave under). Anyone may borrow it: `chirp_away`, `stair_echoes`, `chirp_notes`, `CHIRP_*`. `C11T3_CHIRP=0` is v9 byte for byte. Measured, the two tracks alone, one clap at each distance: the chirp's first sound is 120.6, 61.1, 31.3 and 16.5 ms after the clap is written (written 119.0, 59.5, 29.8, 14.9: the engine starts every note 1.6 ms late); the echoes' note, read at the 5th, 16th, 31st, 46th and 56th echo, is within 6 cents of the spacing written, at every distance (far: 560 to 469 Hz; near: 489 to 442); the clap's peak is the same to 0.3 dB each time. In the master the same reading on what v10 adds is within 6 cents; the claps' stretch is 0.14 dB quieter than v9's (the music is turned 0.33 dB down under them), and every other bar is v9 to 59 dB with 0.01 dB of gain change (0.03 dB and 69 dB at the seams). 96.38 / 96.45: exactly v9's today. Benched first and refused: a clap of under a millisecond (the engine moves envelopes every 32 samples, so each came out a different size: sixty at one spacing were 8 dB clear of noise; 5 ms ticks are 17 dB clear). Not heard by anyone.

**v11 (chirper-zero): STANDING ROOM ONLY.** The record's name, the other way: the room FILLS WITH PEOPLE, and people are the best absorbers a room gets. Sabine's law (1898): a room's echo lasts 0.161 V / A seconds, A everything in it that swallows sound, and every person adds the same to A wherever they stand. After the standing wave, in the last two rows of "standing room" (bars 137 to 152, 4:19 to 4:49), people come in twice: over bars 137 to 140 to half a house (held to 144), over bars 145 to 148 to a full one (held to the door). A full house swallows twice what the empty room does. THE WALLS: a bare wall takes 3 dB from an echo each time; with half a house 6, with a full one 9: the echoes keep their rhythm and the train of them is a third as long. THE AIR: the hall's wash is written to die in 5.5, 2.75 and 1.83 s. THE PIPES IN THE WALLS (v6) are struck by what reaches each wall, so they are struck less. Nothing in the engine turns a room while it sounds, so three rooms are built (26 new buses) and the click is in all three; a lane on each room's doors lets one be heard, two only while people come in. No track is changed. Anyone may borrow it: `house_stages`, `house_share`, `lane_at`, `house_lane`, `HOUSE_*`. `C11T3_HOUSE=0` is v10 byte for byte. Measured, the click alone into the rooms: a wall takes 3.30, 6.36, 9.35 dB; the third echo is 6.6, 12.7, 18.7 dB under the first; the wash dies 60 dB in 4.0, 2.3, 1.6 s by my reading (shorter than written, and 1.75 and 2.55 times apart where 2 and 3 are written); the wall pipes against v10's in the same bars: 3.3 (one wall), 6.3, 9.3 dB down with half a house, 6.4, 12.5, 18.3 with a full one. In the master, a full house: 1.4 dB less from 300 Hz to 1 kHz and 2.3 dB less from 1 to 4 kHz over the whole mix, and 1.6 to 2.6 dB MORE under 95 Hz: the last two rows are 1.1 and 1.4 dB louder than v10's (with less to hold down the master lets the drum and bass up: my reading, not separated). Bars 5 to 136 are v10 to 51 dB, the first four and last eight bars to 67 dB. 96.38 / 96.46 against v10's 96.31 / 96.39 today. Made first without the pipes and refused: nothing could be read in the master where the echoes fall. Not heard by anyone.

**v12 (chirper-zero): THE ROOM CLAPS.** v11 filled the room with people; now they clap, and how a crowd comes to clap as one is a measured thing (Neda and others, 2000, with microphones in concert halls). Applause is everyone clapping fast, each at a rate of his own, and it is noise. It turns into one clap when people HALVE their rate (slow clapping is far more even from one person to the next), and then each is pulled toward what he hears. It is Kuramoto's arithmetic: a phase a person, each with a rate of its own and a pull toward the beat; when the pull is stronger than the spread of the rates they lock. Here: 32 people, eight across and four rows back (2, 4, 6, 8 m: a row back is quieter by its distance and 6 ms later, which is the speed of sound). The first sixteen are the half house: from bar 141 (4:27), one after another, they clap twice a beat, each up to a tenth off that rate and nobody with anybody. From bar 145 (4:34), one by one, each halves his rate and is pulled to the beat; the second sixteen, who are coming in, start slow. Bars 149 to 152 (4:42): one clap on every beat from 32 pairs of hands, the back row 17 ms behind the front. Then the door. Nothing is random: each person's rate, first clap and moment of halving come from the golden ratio. A hand (`sr-hand-a`, `-b`) is a 9 ms burst of noise with nothing under 1.5 or 2.2 kHz; eight new tracks (`crowd-0` … `-7`, one for each place across), 1288 claps, dry. No other track is changed. Anyone may borrow it: `crowd_times`, `crowd_claps`, `crowd_notes`, `crowd_step`, `CROWD_*`. `C11T3_CROWD=0` is v11 byte for byte. Why this, this round: `compscore --bands` said first that this song has 6 to 13 dB less than the rest of the record from 8 to 15 kHz, and applause is that. Measured: the people alone are in step with the beat 0.02 to 0.10 in bars 141 to 145, 0.34, 0.80 and then 1.00 from bar 148, where 80% of their sound falls in 12 ms of each beat; in the master, what v12 has over 6 kHz that v11 has not is in step 0.02 to 0.24, then 0.31, then 0.98 and 0.99, and the top of the master is 6 to 10 dB over v11's in the applause and 10 dB over in the 33 ms after each beat once they are one clap (and the same as v11 between). 96.80 / 96.80 against v11's 96.34 / 96.48 today. Costs: the music is 0.4 to 0.8 dB quieter under the people; the song is a little more like Least Common to the scorer (distinct 96.9 to 92.4); the cut at 4:40 is not a cut any more. Not heard by anyone.

**v13 (chirper-zero): THE CORRIDOR'S OTHER TWO WALLS + THE CLICK'S REPAIR.** The corridor has had two walls since v1: its ends, far off, which send the click back as an echo you can count. It has two more, the ones beside you, 2.336 m apart, and a click between two close hard walls does not echo, it RATTLES (a flutter echo): across and back 73.4 times a second, which is D2. It is two tracks, an ear each (`flut-l`, `flut-r`, patch `sr-flutter`: a saw on D2 with everything under 5 kHz taken off, so only its edge is left, one every crossing and back, dying in half a second), and in each ear two notes: the click off the NEAR wall (twice the distance to it), then the one that has been off both (twice the width). The walls ring when the click goes by and each time it comes back down the corridor (95 rings, bars 17-48). For eight bars you stand in the middle: both walls' clicks arrive together, twice in each crossing, so the rattle is an octave up (D3) and in the middle of the head. Then you walk to the left wall, back through the middle to the right, and back (16 bars a turn): the near wall's click comes up to 10.2 ms before the far one's, so the rattle drops the octave, leans to the near wall (the first arrival wins) and changes colour with every step: two clicks t apart in every 13.6 ms take out the partials of D2 at odd multiples of 1 / 2t. Anyone may borrow it: `flut_span`, `flut_where`, `flut_rings`, `flut_notes`, `FLUT_*`. `C11T3_FLUT=0` is v12 byte for byte. ALSO the click's repair (`C11T3_TRIM=0` leaves the click as it was; `sr-click-t`): each part read alone, the click owns both of the song's worst bands (a third of its power is at its own note, A5; and nothing else has anything over 10 kHz): 5 dB down at 880 Hz, 6 dB up round 15 kHz, on a lane that is nought in the first four bars and the last eight. Measured alone: every start on its sample; each ear's lines within 0.6 dB of its curve (median). In the master: the two ears' trains are the same train 6.56, 4.62, 3.02, 2.00, 1.71 ms apart as written, to 0.02 ms in every bar. 98.37 / 98.26 against v12's 96.84 / 96.85 today. COSTS: nearer Least Common in the scorer's eyes (0.860 to 0.889: distinct -0.66, and the same for Least Common when it is next scored); both seams 0.29 dB quieter; a new cut at 0:31. At -6 dB the rattle was 10 dB over the music in the gap after the click: turned down 8. Not heard by anyone.

**v14 (chirper-zero): ZENO'S BALL.** A small light ball (`sr-ball`, on A6) is let fall nine times, and each time it comes to rest EXACTLY on a bar line: at every door but the last, and at the turn of a row in the long rooms (the lines of bars 17, 33, 49, 73, 81, 97, 113, 129, 137). A ball that keeps the same share of its speed at every bounce (2 ** (-1/6): 0.891, about a table-tennis ball's) makes flights that each last 0.891 of the one before, so the bounces come twice as fast every six and infinitely many fit into a finite time, the first flight over (1 - 0.891). That time is set to seven and a quarter beats: the first bounce is on the fourth sixteenth of a bar, the last on the bar line two bars on. Each bounce is 1.0 dB quieter, as a real one is; the first flight (376.7 ms) says it fell 22 cm. SPACE twice: the ball reaches the walls as the click and the tock do (it is in `FIRST`), so every room answers the same ball with its own echoes and outside nothing answers; and it rolls from one side to the other, back the next time. Anyone may borrow it: `ball_flights`, `ball_height`, `ball_notes`, `ball_lane`, `BALL_*`. `C11T3_BALL=0` is v13 byte for byte. Measured: alone, the first flight reads 376.67 ms (written 376.66), each flight 0.8907 of the one before (0.8909), each bounce 0.99 dB under the last (1.00); in the master a fall is 9.1 to 12.3 dB under the music of its two bars and its first bounces are as loud as the song's click or up to 5 dB over it; in the two bars after it stops, what still rings is 39 dB under the music outside, 30 in the corridor and stairwell, 23 to 27 in the pipes, the hangar and standing room. 99.25 / 99.24 against v13's 98.36 / 98.24 today, and all of it is the `distinct` term (73 to 100): WHY, said plainly: the scorer read this song 0.890 like Least Common and I owed the move. The bounces fall between the sixteenths where the scorer sees them (6.437 to 6.56 onsets a second), and the ball's note is the fifth, A, not D (on D6 the two songs' notes read MORE alike, 0.715 to 0.744; on A6 0.661). Likeness now 0.835 (edge 0.85). Costs: the seams 0.17 dB quieter; the music 0.1 to 0.7 dB quieter under a fall; 1.9 kHz +7.4. Not heard by anyone.

**v15 (chirper-zero): A BIRD IN THE HANGAR.** Bars 81-88, the hangar's first eight, where the drum and the bass rest. Something small flies round in a circle of 6 m whose centre is 4 m in front of you: 2 m behind your head at the nearest, 10 m in front at the farthest, once round every two bars (9.9 m/s), calling every three sixteenths (two notes, E8 then A8). No pan, fader move or detune is written: the track's `orbit` effect (Chowning's moving source) works out from the circle when the call reaches each ear, how loud, and how sharp or flat. It is far in front on the first beat of bars 81, 83, 85, 87, goes round by the right, and passes behind your head on the first beat of bars 82, 84, 86, 88. The calls go to the hangar's air too. Anyone may borrow it: `bird_span`, `bird_rate`, `bird_notes`, `bird_facts`, `BIRD_*`. `C11T3_BIRD=0` is v14 byte for byte. Measured alone: a call arrives 7.4 to 30.8 ms late (2 m to 10 m with the engine's own 1.7 ms), the far ear up to 0.54 ms after the near one and 4.0 dB quieter, 13.7 dB between nearest and farthest, 33 cents sharp coming and flat going; a lap later it is in the same place to 0.02 ms. In the whole song's master the near call peaks 8.5 dB under the click's strokes and the far one 30 dB under (the master makes the 14 dB between them 22). 99.24 / 99.24 against v14's 99.25 / 99.24 today: NO SCORE IN IT, and kept, not a best. Costs: bars 81-88 are 0.05 dB louder; the far half of the circle may be too faint to follow. Not heard by anyone.

**v16 (chirper-zero): A FOURTH, A FIFTH, AN OCTAVE GO BY.** Something that passes you is heard higher coming than going, and the interval says how fast it went: (c + v)/(c - v). A fourth is a seventh of the speed of sound, a fifth is a fifth of it, an octave is a third of it. Three things go by in the hangar, left to right, 5 m in front, nearest on the bar lines 87, 88 and 89 (2:43.8, 2:45.7, 2:47.6: the last two bars the drum rests, and the line it comes back on), at 50, 71 and 118 m/s, each faster than the last and each coming on the note the one before left on: A7 to E7, E7 to A6, A6 to A5 (the click's own note, as the drum comes in). None of them sings either of its notes: the third one's own note is E6. Tracks `fly-7`, `fly-5`, `fly-3` (a held plain tone each, through the engine's `orbit` on a line, -30 dB). Anyone may borrow it: `fly_facts`, `FLY_*`. `C11T3_FLY=0` is v15 byte for byte. Measured alone: the notes at the line's ends are within 12 cents of A and E; the left ear first coming, the right going. In the master each peaks 13 dB under the click. 99.24 / 99.23 against v15's 99.24 / 99.24 today: NO SCORE IN IT (kept, not a best). Not heard by anyone.

**v17 (chirper-zero): THE BUILDING ACROSS THE SQUARE HUMS.** Through "the door" (bars 153-168) you walk away across the square and the click you left falls behind. You are walking TOWARDS something: the next building, and a building has a sound of its own from outside: its transformers hum at twice the mains and the multiples of that, and a wall lets only the bottom of a sound through (6 dB more loss for every octave up). Two plain held tones, 120 and 180 Hz (mains of 60), the upper 3.5 dB under the lower; in no key (half way between B flat and B, and between F and F sharp). It grows as 1 over the distance: 174 m off at bar 153, 10 m at the end of bar 168: 24.6 dB. Track `next` (one note sixteen bars long, patch `sr-next`, a `gain` effect on a lane that is the walk, -62 dB). Anyone may borrow it: `nextroom_metres`, `nextroom_db`, `nextroom_lane`, `NEXTROOM_*`. `C11T3_NEXT=0` is v16 byte for byte. Measured in the master: from 100 to 200 Hz it reads -74.5 dBFS in bar 153, -66.0 in bar 161 and -49.8 in bar 168 (the going click peaks -13.8 there); nothing in bars 1-4. 99.59 / 99.59 against v16's 99.24 / 99.23 today: +0.35, the spectrum term alone, BY COVERAGE: the last eight bars were digital silence in two bands. THAT IS WHAT THE ROUND WAS FOR, and the choice of 60 over this country's 50 is the scorer's (100 and 150 Hz would have fallen where the song already has sound). The first build, on the next song's B flat and F, was refused: it made this song like Least Common (0.849, edge 0.85) at any level. Not heard by anyone: it is meant to be nearly under hearing.

**v18 (chirper-zero): THE PIPES WHISTLE.** Clap at the mouth of a long concrete pipe and what comes back from the far end is a whistle that falls, "pyeow", and the second echo is a slower one (Crawford's culvert whistlers, 1971). The reason is this song's own: the click that goes straight down and back comes first; the click that glanced once off a wall has a little farther to come, twice a little farther still: the k-th comes after hypot(there and back, k widths) / the speed of sound. So the clicks come thick, then thinner, and settle to one for every width: a note falling from the top of hearing towards the speed of sound over the width, the octave of the note the pipe sings across itself. In "the pipes" (bars 73-80) every pipe is now 81.7 m long: the click (sixteenth 2) comes back from the far end on each of the three beats after it, every time a slower fall, towards D3, F3, G3, A3, B flat, C4, D4, E4 as the pipes narrow. Nothing is bent: 6080 of the stairs' ticks (on G4), each where the arithmetic puts it, on one dry track (`whistle`, -32 dB), heard from beside the pipe's mouth (each arrival counts as the sine of the angle it comes out at: in the mouth itself the echo's first tick was 25 dB over its whistle). Anyone may borrow it: `whistle_arrivals`, `WHISTLE_*`. `C11T3_WHISTLE=0` is v17 byte for byte. Measured: alone, the pitch read from the sound is the arithmetic's to 3% (D pipe, first echo: 356, 264, 209, 176 Hz at 50, 100, 200, 400 ms; the sums say 345, 261, 207, 175). In the master: the first whistle is -24 to -27 dBFS in its first 100 ms, 15 to 18 dB over everything else between 150 and 700 Hz there, 24 dB under the row as a whole; the second 9 dB over in the wide pipes; the third is at the level of what is there; in bars 79-80 (the ball) they are under it. 99.64 / 99.64 against v17's 99.59 / 99.59 today: +0.05 (the spectrum term, by coverage in bands the song is short in), a best. Not heard by anyone.

**v19 (chirper-zero): FASTER THAN SOUND.** Three things go by in the hangar at a seventh, a fifth and a third of the speed of sound. The fourth goes by at TWICE the speed of sound (686 m/s), left to right, 81.7 m off at its nearest (as far as the pipes are long), nearest on the line of bar 91 (2:51.4), striking a bell every eighth, a note higher each time: the pipes' scale going up, D3 F3 G3 A3 Bb3 C4 D4, a rest of two strokes as it passes, then G4 A4 Bb4. The sums are the ones the other three use, `heard = struck + distance / c`, and the signs come out the other way: it outruns everything it does, so NOTHING of it is heard coming, and nothing until 206 ms after it has passed. Then its coming is heard BACKWARDS: what it struck last comes first (D4, 396 ms after the line), then C4, Bb3, A3, G3, F3, D3, an eighth apart, on the odd sixteenths to within 8 to 39 ms: the scale it played upwards runs down, from the left, where it came from, getting farther and quieter (258 m to 1228 m), and each bell is itself backwards: it swells to its stroke and stops there. At twice the speed of sound exactly, the past comes back at the tempo it happened at (far off the rate is 1 / (1 - 2) = -1); the near bells are heard a little fast and sharp (D4 at 327.3 Hz, 188 cents up; D3 at 147.5 Hz, 8 cents up). What it strikes when it has gone is heard forwards from the right, a third as fast and a twelfth lower (1 / (1 + 2)): G4, A4, Bb4 at 135.3, 148.6 and 156.4 Hz, three eighths apart, each ringing three times as long. A stroke is as loud as it is near (1 over the distance) and as its ring is squeezed (heard three times as slow it has a third of the power). It makes no boom: only its bells are sounded. Tracks `sonic-back` (7 notes, patch `sr-sonic-back`) and `sonic-on` (3 notes, patch `sr-sonic-on`), -12 dB, pitch and side on lanes. Anyone may borrow it: `sonic_strokes`, `sonic_first`, `SONIC_*` (`SONIC_MACH` other than 2 changes the tempo the past comes back at: 1 / (M - 1)). `C11T3_SONIC=0` is v18 byte for byte.

**v20 (chirper-zero): THE HANGAR'S OTHER TWO WALLS.** Every room in this song has had two walls, left and right, and a wall is a number of sixteenths there and back. In its last two rows (bars 97-112, 3:02.9) the hangar gets the wall in front and the wall behind, as far apart as the side ones (245 m, 12 sixteenths there and back) with you half way. An echo that has met both pairs of walls has come back from a CORNER, and by Pythagoras: across a and deep d, it is the square root of (a times a plus d times d) sixteenths late, which is almost never a whole number. With the click in the middle (6 + 6) the corners answer 8.49, 13.42, 16.97 and 18.97 sixteenths late: the first half way between two sixteenths, the second nowhere, the last two within 4 ms of a sixteenth (17/12 is nearly the root of 2, and 19/6 nearly the root of 10). When the click steps to 7 + 5 every one of them moves, and one lands ON a sixteenth, because 5 across and 12 deep is 13; and the front and back walls' own echo falls between the side walls' two, so the click comes back right, middle, left on three sixteenths running. Each echo comes from where its corner is, and every wall it met takes 3 dB, as the room's side walls do. Measured in the whole song's master, both seeds: 160 of 168 instants found within 2.1 ms of where they are written; in the 6 + 6 row the two corner echoes heard in each bar stand 3 to 6 dB under the side walls' echoes and from 4 dB under to 13 over what was in their gaps; in the 7 + 5 row most of them are under the music. Nobody has heard it.

**v21 (chirper-zero): UNDER THE DOME.** Every wall in this song has been flat, and a flat wall gives a click back once. The hall of "standing room" has a dome, and a dome like this one (an ellipse turned round) has a FOCUS: from the click up to ANY part of it and down to that one place on the floor is the same way round, 7 sixteenths: the ellipse is the curve for which that is so. In the row before the people come in (bars 129-136, which had nothing of its own) you walk to it. Two sixteenths of sound away (82 m), twelve parts of the dome answer one after another, those ahead of you first and those behind you last, from all round you: a rattle 296 ms long. With every bar it is shorter (248, 192, 131, 67 ms). On the focus (bars 134-136) all twelve arrive together: ONE stroke, on step 9 where nothing else in this room strikes, made of twelve, and not one of them was played any louder: the only thing that changed is where you stand (the Albert Hall's echo before the mushrooms were hung; the whispering place under any dome). Measured in the whole song's master, both seeds: every echo found 1.6 to 2.2 ms after it is written (as this engine starts a note); in bars 134 and 136 the focused stroke is as loud as the click itself (-19.0 to -20.4 dBFS in its 40 ms; the click -18.5 to -20.7) and 16 and 8 dB over what was in its gap; in bar 135 it falls with a bounce of Zeno's ball and is 3 dB under that; the parts of the rattle are -38 to -19 dBFS each, about level with what is in their gaps (by the bar, 3 under to 5 over). Nobody has heard it.

**v22 (chirper-zero): THE HALL'S AIR DUCT.** Every wall in this song gives a click back as a click. A pipe does not. A sound that crosses a pipe while it goes along it (half a wave across, or two halves, or three) goes along more slowly than the speed of sound, by sqrt(1 - (own / f) squared), where `own` is the note that just fits across: a high note is hardly slowed; a note near the pipe's own is slowed almost to a stop. So a click that goes down a duct and back comes back as a note that STARTS HIGH AND FALLS, fast at first and then more and more slowly, towards the duct's own note, and never quite arrives (the note back at time t is own / sqrt(1 - (back / t) squared): a culvert whistler, Crawford 1971). The hall's duct is 1.56 m across (its own note A2, 110 Hz, with 220 and 330: the patch `sr-duct`, the track `duct`), to your left, in the row before the dome (bars 121-128, 3:48.6, which had nothing of its own but the ball), and is 1, then 3, then 5 sixteenths there and back (20, 61, 102 m long): the echo begins on steps 3, 5 and 7, and the dome in the next row answers on 9. From the short duct the note is within a semitone of A2 after a quarter of a second; from the long one it is still five semitones up after 300 ms and more than a semitone up after a second. How far the sound has been is how slowly it comes down. Measured in the whole song's master, both seeds: each echo begins 0 to 14 dB over the music in its own band (median 8) and is about level with it 300 ms on; this master ducks it some 10 dB with every bass drum, so it is heard between the drums; alone its pitch is within 45 cents of the written fall from 50 ms on. Nobody has heard it.

**v23 (chirper-zero): THE CORRIDOR'S HANDRAIL: TWO SPEEDS OF SOUND.** Sound goes 343 m/s in the air and 5100 in steel. Put an ear to a long rail and have somebody knock on it a long way off: the knock is heard twice, first through the steel and then through the air, and how far apart is how far off: 2.72 ms a metre (Biot measured the speed of sound in iron this way in 1808, on 951 m of water pipe in Paris, and the two were 2.5 s apart). In the third of the corridor's four rows (bars 33 to 40, 1:01.0) somebody knocks on the handrail twice a bar, a mark further each time (a mark is 20.4 m: what the air crosses in a thirty-second), out to 163 m and back. The air's knock (D6, on the right, `sr-knock`) lands on sixteenths 7 and 15 every time, quieter the further off; the steel's ring (D7, in the left ear, which is on the rail, `sr-rail`) comes before it: 56 ms before from the nearest mark, 444 ms before from the furthest, and hardly quieter. The rail has the news first. Measured in the whole song's master on both seeds: 13 of the 16 rings and 14 (15) of the 16 knocks rise 6 dB or more, and every one stands 5 to 39 dB over the music in its own band; three knocks fall with the tock and are delivered 14 to 15 dB lower. The air's knock was first a low thud (F3): it scored more than any version of the song and was not heard to begin, and was refused. Nobody has heard it.

**v24 (chirper-zero): THE WHISPERING GALLERY.** Every wall in the song till now sent sound back across a room. A round wall does something else with a sound made close to it: the sound clings to the wall and goes ROUND it, both ways, and hardly spreads (Rayleigh, for St Paul's; in the Gol Gumbaz at Bijapur, 38 m across, a whisper is said to come back seven times). In the hall's first row (bars 113 to 120, 3:33.3) the hall has such a gallery, 39 m across: 122.5 m round, which is three sixteenths at the speed of sound. Every two bars somebody a third of the way round whispers at the wall. Straight across it is too faint to hear; by the wall it comes from the LEFT a sixteenth later and from the RIGHT a sixteenth after that, and each comes round again every three sixteenths, seven times: left, right, nothing; left, right, nothing: three against the bar's four, and nobody wrote a rhythm: it is where you stand. It is the same 29 ms of hiss (3 to 7.5 kHz) every time, sample for sample, falling 3 dB for every doubling of the way and 1.5 dB a lap: 22.5 dB by the fourteenth arrival. The whisper is made half a sixteenth after the bar line, so that every arrival falls between the song's own strokes: on them the master swallowed it (read: the second arrival came 21.7 dB under the first for a written 3.5). Measured in the whole song's master on both seeds: see the note; the fall is still not delivered as written (arrivals after a bass drum come 6 to 7 dB short). Nobody has heard it.

**v25 (chirper-zero): THE BASS IS HEARD ROUND THE CORNER.** The bass comes in at bar 33, half way down the corridor. It was there all along: 3 m down a side passage, round a corner that is 24 m off when the corridor begins (bar 17, 0:30.5). Sound bends round a corner into the shadow and low sound bends best: Maekawa's rule (1968) says the shadow takes 10 log (3 + 20 N) dB, N the half waves by which the way round the corner is longer than the straight way through the wall. So the bass is heard before it is met: at the start its first partial 14 dB down and each octave above about 3 dB more, and 9.5 dB more for the longer way (the corridor's own law); walking up to the corner the shadow lifts, most of it in the last two bars, to 5 dB for every partial alike; at bar 33 the corner is turned and the bass is in front of you. It is written as the bass's own first six partials (measured alone), six plain tones each with its own shadow on its fader's lane, playing the cell the bass plays when it is met. Measured alone: every partial within 0.1 dB of the rule (0.9 in the last bar, where the lane moves inside the note). In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v26 (chirper-zero): THE HANGAR'S DOOR SHUTS.** On the first beat of the hangar (bar 81, 2:32.4), where the drum and the bass rest for eight bars, the door shuts behind you, and what is left is the tail. How long a sound lasts in a room is Sabine's rule, 0.161 V / A. In a small room A is the walls. In a very big one the air itself takes sound, and far more of the high than of the low (0.44 dB a kilometre at 125 Hz, 29 at 4 kHz, 70 at 6.5 kHz): 0.161 V / (S a + 4 m V). The hangar is 245 m by 245 m (as its echoes already say) and 20 m high, and its walls take 0.15 at every pitch alike. So the bottom of the slam (95 to 177 Hz) takes 9.0 s to fall 60 dB and the top (5.7 to 7.5 kHz) 2.0 s: seven bands of noise, each on a straight line in dB to its own time: 9.0, 8.6, 8.1, 7.4, 6.1, 3.6, 2.0 s. The tail goes dark as it dies, and that is how the size of the place is heard: the walls did not take the top, the distance did. The slam itself is in the middle and 7.2 dB over the tail it starts (you are 10 m from the door, the hall's own distance is 22 m); the tail is wide, a noise for each ear. Seven patches (`sr-slam-1` to `-7`), fourteen tracks. Measured alone: the seven times 9.4, 8.7, 7.9, 7.3, 6.1, 3.8, 2.3 s; the bands at the door within 0.7 dB of the door's slope; the slam 5.7 to 7.8 dB over each band. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v27 (chirper-zero): THE HUSH AFTER THE DOOR.** v26 shut the hangar's door and wrote the tail the hangar would leave, band by band, and measured it, and found the chord and its echo lying over it. Now, when the door has shut (bar 81, 2:32.4), everything waits two bars: the click, the tock, the chord and the bird. For 3.8 seconds there is the door's tail and nothing else: you stand still and hear how big the place is. In that time the tail's top goes and its bottom is still there: in a very big room it is the air, not the walls, that takes the top. Then the click walks on (bar 83), the bird flies three times round and not four, and the chord comes at bar 85 as before. Nothing is added: 30 notes are taken out, and the hangar's echo opens when the hush ends and not at the door (it had let in the echoes of the last click and tock before the door). In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v28 (chirper-zero): THE FOOT OF THE STAIRS.** You come into the stairwell and stop. For its first four bars (bars 49 to 52, 1:31.4) the drum and the bass wait: there is the click, the tock, and what the stairwell gives back of them, coming up as you come in (the stairwell's echoes open over the first two of them); Zeno's ball has just come to rest on that very bar line. You hear the place before you climb. In bar 53 you clap for the first time, the stairs answer with their falling chirp, and the drum comes back with the clap. Nothing is added: 32 notes wait. The room's echoes were there before, under the drum; in the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v29 (chirper-zero): WHERE IT BEGAN.** In a room that rings, a plain tone is heard where it began (the Franssen effect). In the hangar's last two rows (bars 97 to 112, 3:02.9) a soft plain tone sounds every two bars, a bar long, each note twice: A A, C C, F F, E E. The first of each pair starts sharply on the left; 50 ms later it has been handed to the right, where all the rest of its straight sound is: left and right together are one unbroken tone. The second is the same without its start. The hangar answers both, mostly from the left, so that while a tone is held the two sides are nearly level: the held tone says little of where it is; the start does. The first should be heard on the left, where it began, and the second not: though after their first 50 ms the two are the same. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v30 (chirper-zero): THE WALLS CLOSE IN.** A knock between two walls comes back once for every time across and back. In the corridor's last row (bars 41 to 48, 1:16.2) a soft knock twice a bar is heard between walls 18.64 m apart (9.2 echoes a second: you can count them), then 9.32 m, 4.66 m, 2.33 m (the corridor's own width: 73.6 a second, its note, D2), 1.16 m (147.2: D3), 0.58 m (294.5: D4), and out again to 1.16 m and the corridor's own. Every halving of the width doubles the echoes: a rattle, a buzz, a note an octave higher. Rhythm and pitch are one thing, and the width of the room says which. Every echo is a whole number of samples after the one before; every train falls 75 dB a second; an echo is 3 dB less for each halving, so that every train carries the same energy (this piece's rule, not a room's). In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v31 (chirper-zero): A DROP DOWN THE STAIRWELL.** On the way up the stairs (bars 57 to 64, 1:46.7) something is let go over the rail in every bar, at a tap, a flight of eight steps (1.36 m) higher each time. It lands when gravity says, the square root of twice the height over g, and its thud comes back up at the speed of sound: 531, 753, 924, 1069, 1197, 1314, 1421, 1521 ms after the tap. The wait says how high you have climbed, and it grows as the square root of the height: by less each flight. The thud is always the same floor (Bb2 and its octave); written 10 log n dB softer from n flights. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v32 (chirper-zero): THE STAIRWELL RINGS.** Every thud of v31 (bars 57 to 64) rings the column of air between the floor and where you stand. A column one flight high has one note: the speed of sound over twice its height, 126.1 Hz. A column n flights high has that note over n (126.1, 63.1, 42.0, 31.5, 25.2, 21.0, 18.0, 15.8 Hz) and every multiple of it: the lowest way falls as you climb, the ways crowd together (2, 3, 4, 5, 7, 8, 9, 10 of them between 100 and 260 Hz), and one flight's own note and its octave (126.1 and 252.2 Hz) are among them at every height: an undertone series. A ring dies in 1.8 s. Alone, all 48 ways stand within 1.5 Hz and nothing else does. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v33 (chirper-zero): THE ROOM HOLDS ITS BREATH.** In bar 139 (4:23.3; the people are coming in) one clap sounds in a hall of its own, with the song's clap on beat 2. Half a beat after it the hall stops dying: its tail hangs where it was for six and a half beats (3.1 s), as if time had stopped in the room while the drum went on. On the first beat of bar 141, where the applause begins, the hall lets go, and the tail dies as a hall's does (60 dB in 1.1 s). It is the engine's own hall with its `freeze` held by a lane. A wash behind the drum, not in front of it: see the note for how far under the music it is. Nobody has heard it.

**v34 (chirper-zero): THE KNOCK GOES AWAY, THE ROOM DOES NOT.** In bars 89 to 96 (2:47.6; the hangar) a low knock (F3) twice a bar, a third of a beat after beats 0 and 2, each time twice as far off: 1.3, 2.6, 5.1, 10, 20, 41, 82, 163 m in bars 89 to 92, and back again in bars 93 to 96. Each doubling takes 6 dB off the knock itself and makes it later (343 m/s: 4 ms at the nearest, a whole beat at the farthest). The room's answer to it (a hall that dies in 1.6 s, on a track of its own) is the same every time, wherever the knock is: so at 1.3 m the knock is well over the room, at 2.6 m they carry the same energy (the room's critical distance: my choice, a church's), and beyond it there is more room than knock: at the far end the knock itself is 36 dB under what the room says of it. That is the cue an ear has for how far off a sound is. Nobody has heard it.

**v35 (chirper-zero): SOONER OR LOUDER.** In bars 146 to 152 (from 4:36.2) the same plain pip (A4, 80 ms) in both ears walks from the left to the right twice, six places each time (70, 42 and 14 degrees to either side). In bars 146 to 148 both ears get it exactly as loud and one gets it sooner, by as much as the way round a head takes (Woodworth: the head's radius over the speed of sound, times the angle plus its sine: 551, 358 and 124 millionths of a second). In bars 150 to 152 both get it at the same instant and one gets it louder (9.4, 6.7 and 2.4 dB). These are Rayleigh's two ways of telling where a sound is, one at a time: a low tone is placed by the first, and the second is what a pan knob does. It needs headphones for the first walk. Nobody has heard it.

**v36 (chirper-zero): THE HIGHER THE NOTE, THE HIGHER IT HANGS.** Outside the door (bars 153 to 160, 4:49.5) there is no room and no ceiling. One plain pip, always in the middle and the same in both ears, comes down a ladder of sixteen notes from D7 to D4 and climbs it again: two to a bar's half, a sixteenth after beats 0 and 2 and a sixteenth before them. A note is said to seem to come from higher up the higher its pitch, wherever it really comes from (Pratt, 1930; Roffler and Butler, 1968). v35 placed a pip left and right by time and by level; this is up and down, and it is not in the sound at all: nothing but the note changes. Nobody has heard it.

**v37 (chirper-zero): THE PIPE NARROWS: A CLOUD BECOMES A POINT.** In bars 73 to 79 (2:17.1), in the pipes, a short hiss a sixteenth after every beat: 28 of them, and every one is written as loud in each ear as the one before (delivered within 2.8 dB; all but three within 1.0). What changes is only how alike the two ears' hisses are. In bars 73 and 74 each ear has a hiss of its own, with nothing in common: said to be heard as a cloud that fills the head. Bar by bar a quarter more of each ear's power is one hiss given to both (0.25, 0.5, 0.75), and in bars 78 and 79 it is all one hiss: a point in the middle. Nothing is panned, nothing is turned up or down, and no room is used: the width is made of likeness alone. Nobody has heard it.

**v38 (chirper-zero): HOW FAR BY HOW DULL ALONE.** Outside, in bars 9 to 16 (0:15.2), where no room answers, the same low knock (B flat 2, a saw) twice a bar, a sixteenth after the drum. The first is as it is. The second has its top taken as air takes it, half an octave lower each bar (8000 Hz down to 707), and is written as loud. Everywhere else in the song a thing further off is softer, later and duller at once; here it is only duller. If air's loss in dB goes as the square of the frequency (the classical law), half an octave duller is twice as far: 1, 2, 4 ... 128 times. Nobody has heard it.

## t4 Ring True (chirper-one)

**The rule.** Six struck things, each a physical model or noise, play ONE two-bar cell from the first bar to the last; once a thing is in, not a stroke is added, moved or taken away. The only thing that changes is how long each is allowed to ring: a number from 0 (stopped at once: a click) to 1 (let go: a note). Every click on this track is a note stopped too soon to be heard as one. In the generator: `STROKES` (the cell), `RING` (each thing's ring time shut and let go), `SHAPE` (its ring at the section tops), `ring_at`.

**The cell** (sixteenths of 32; B flat, five notes: B flat C D F G). The weight (a bass drum, B flat 1) on every beat. The wood: 3 7 11 14 | 19 23 27 29, D5 F5 G5 F5 | D5 C5 B flat 4 C5: the tune. The metal on the off-beats (Give or Take's grid click, borrowed as offered): F6 F6 G6 F6 | D6 F6 G6 B flat 6. The string: 2 7 11 14 | 18 23 27 30, B flat 2, B flat 2, F2, G2 | B flat 2, B flat 2, D3, C3: the bass. The skin: 10 15 | 26 29 31, B flat 2, F2 | B flat 2, D3, F2. The air on two and four: F5 F5 | F5 D5.

**Let go slowly, stopped quickly.** A thing is let go over a section of sixteen bars, bar by bar, and stopped over two (a hand laid on it). Anyone may borrow that as this record's way of opening and shutting anything.

**v1 (chirper-one, the skeleton): RING TRUE.** 125, 184 bars (5:53), thirteen sections: taps (the weight and the wood, the metal at bar 9); the family (all six, all shut); the wood is let go; the metal; the string; the skin and the air; the weight lifted and everything let go; the weight back and let go; then the skin and the air stopped, the string and the weight, the metal (eight bars each); the wood closes over sixteen; taps again, and in the last bar everything is let go once. Measured on single strokes: the wood falls 30 dB in 8 ms shut and 460 ms let go, and the share of its power within 3% of its note goes from 32% to 99%; the metal 6 to 553 ms, 52% to 93%; the skin 8 to 99 ms, 3% to 86%; the string 16 to 315 ms, 4% to 42%; the air 2 to 56 ms, 3% to 62%. Score 66.20 / 66.22 (seeds 0 / 11): the scorer finds three sections where thirteen are written, so the changes are too smooth or too small to count as events yet. The mix is 7 to 8 dB under the album's envelope at 6.7, 9.2 and 14.8 kHz: this family has tocks and no bright click. Not heard by its composer.

**v2 (chirper-one): A SEVENTH THING THAT RINGS FALSE, AND A WEIGHT THAT RINGS.** Five switches (`--without-plate`, `-boom`, `-room`, `-toll`, `-hush`); all five off is v1 byte for byte. (1) THE PLATE: a small plate on the second sixteenth of every beat (F7), so the cell now runs in sixteenths across four materials: weight on the beat, plate after it, metal on the off-beat, wood on the fourth. Stopped it is the bright tick the family lacked and comes in with the air at bar 25; it is the last thing let go (through the section with no weight), and let go it is a wash and never a note, the one thing here that rings false; it is choked with the string and the weight. Over 6 kHz the mix was 21.6 and 22.8 dB under its whole in sections 7 and 8; now 14.7 and 11.9. (2) THE WEIGHT RINGS: the felt thud as it was, with a kettle's head under it on the same B flat whose ring the stroke gives. Let go, 82% of a stroke's power is on its 58 Hz partial (v1: about a third); a quarter of a second on it is 25 dB under its own thud (stopped: 46). (3) THE ROOM FOLLOWS THE HAND: a stopped stroke is dry; a stroke let go is also sent to a hall, 20 log10(ring) dB under 16 below its dry level. (4) THE TOLL: in the last bar before each new thing is let go the weight is let go for that bar (22 dB more ring a quarter of a second on), then a thud again. (5) THE HUSH: in the bar before the weight is lifted everything else is stopped dead (5.4 dB down over 300 Hz for that bar), and on the one they are all let go. Tried and taken out: letting things stay let go instead of half stopping them when the next one's turn comes (the song then read as one or two sections: 51.0 to 62.2). 70.53 and 70.36 on two seeds, v1 re-read today 67.02 and 67.05; nothing now stands more than 7 dB off the album's envelope. Still weak: the scorer finds 3 sections of 13; the weight's note is 25 dB under its thud; the wood's hall copy is only 8 dB under the wood itself and no stroke is very dry once all are let go. Not heard by its composer.

**v3 (chirper-one): THE OTHERS STOP TO LISTEN.** Four switches (`--without-listen`, `-off`, `-fore`, `-note`; all four off is v2 byte for byte, all nine v1). (1) LISTEN: for the first four bars of its turn a thing is heard ALONE and let go altogether: the wood at bar 33, the metal at 49, the string at 65, the skin and the air together at 81, and at 113, after sixteen bars without it, the weight by itself ringing B flat. Then everything comes back in. Alone it is 3 dB down, so the return is a step up (unhushed, the wood alone read 2.4 dB over the groove it broke off). Until now a thing's turn was a slow change under six others. (2) OFF: the hand comes off. After its four bars a thing is not stopped again: it starts half let go and is given the rest over the section. (3) FORE: eight bars before its turn each thing gives one stroke let go altogether, the last of its bar (wood bar 25, metal 41, string 57, skin and air 73, plate 89): one ring among clicks. (4) NOTE: the weight's drum head rings three seconds let go (v2: 1.2), 6 dB up, the thud 6 dB down: a quarter second after the stroke the note is 14.6 dB under the stroke's first 60 ms (v2's patch on the same bench: 25.2), so each beat lands on the last one's note. 97.53 and 97.55 on two seeds (v2 today 70.53, 70.36): the scorer finds 12 sections where it found 3, every listening and every return far over its cut (0.24 to 0.73; v2's two were 0.17 and 0.25). The weight is now absent for 32 bars of 184. Not heard by its composer.

**v4 (chirper-one): THE OTHERS ANSWER.** Three switches (`--without-answer`, `-even`, `-after`; all three off is v3 byte for byte). (1) ANSWER: v3's listenings were all alike, four bars of a thing alone. Now the things already let go answer the new one in the last two of its four bars, let go altogether as it is and 7 dB behind it: the wood alone (nobody is before it), the metal answered by the wood, the string by the metal and the wood, the skin and the air by all three. Each break is fuller than the last, and the fifth is the whole family without the weight (bars 97 to 112), which they have been gathering towards. The weight is not answered. Answered bars stand level with the groove that returns (0.9 under to 0.3 over), so the weight coming back is still the step. (2) AFTER, the foretaste's mirror: four bars after it has been stopped each thing gives one more stroke let go altogether (skin and air bar 135, string and plate 143, metal 151, wood 173). (3) EVEN: the wood alone is 5.5 dB down, not 3; it stood 1.1 dB over the groove that came back and now stands 0.8 under. PROVED this round, on v3: the foretastes are heard (the wood's let-go stroke stands 4 dB under the mix for the beat after it, the metal's 8), and the weight's long note does not pile up (40 to 120 Hz climbs 5 dB as the head is let go and holds). 99.09 and 99.17 on two seeds (v3 under the album's seven cuts 97.73). Not heard by its composer.

**v5 (chirper-one): THE AIR IS BLOWN, AND THE ROOM REMEMBERS.** Three switches (`--without-flue`, `-remember`, `-lift`; all three off is v4 byte for byte). (1) FLUE: the air was three milliseconds of noise in a resonator, and let go the resonator sang: a struck thing again. Now there is breath behind the puff (new patch `rt-flue`): a second noise nearly half a second long that comes up only as the thing is let go, into a resonator a third as sharp. Stopped it is the same puff; let go, the note lasts as long as the breath does (on the bench 100 ms within 20 dB of its loudest, where the old one lasted 49). (2) REMEMBER: the stopping (bars 129 to 184) had almost nothing of its own. When a thing is stopped by the hand it is a click again, but for the six bars left of that section the hall it rang in is still open round it (tracks `-kept`): the skin and the air (bars 131 to 136), the string and the plate (139 to 144), the metal (147 to 152). Each hall stands 2.5 to 4 dB over the click it remembers and 11 to 17 under the mix; at the next stopping it is dry. (3) LIFT: the metal's aftertaste 4 dB up. 99.50 and 99.54 on two seeds (v4 99.09 and 99.17). The scorer counts nothing new in the stopping. Not heard by its composer.

**v6 (chirper-one): THE STRINGS NOBODY PLAYS.** One switch (`--without-sympathy`; off is v5 byte for byte) and one that ships off (`--with-wash`). SYMPATHY: beside the seven things lie five open strings tuned under the wood's five notes (F2, G2, B flat 2, C3, D3). Nothing strikes them, so the rule stands (not a stroke is added): they sound only because something beside them is ringing at one of their own notes. Each is a loop one period long that returns itself (0.985 a period) and hears the wood and the air. They are things like the others, with a hand on them: let go across the section with no weight (bars 97 to 112), open while the weight is let go (113 to 128), stopped in two bars with the skin and the air, and let go again in the last bar, so the last thing heard is the strings. By solo they sing the wood's notes and nothing else (99% of their power from 500 Hz to 1 kHz; D5, F5 and G5 within 3 cents) about 10 dB under the mix once open; and when the weight is listened to alone (bar 113) what is heard round it is the strings dying, 14 dB a second. Anyone may borrow the strings: sounds.md has the recipe (a return cannot be shorter than 6 ms; take the low-pass's delay off the period). WASH (off): the plate answering the weight's listening in bars 115 and 116; it works by solo and the scorer takes 2.2 points for it. 99.63 on the scored seed (v5 99.50) and 98.07 on a second, where the scorer splits the string's listening at bar 65 (nothing there changed; that boundary has sat at its edge since v4). Not heard by its composer.

**v7 (chirper-one): THE STRINGS HEAR THE STRING.** The five strings nobody plays (v6) are tuned to the five notes the string plays (F2, G2, B flat 2, C3, D3), and they could not hear it: they heard the wood and the air, two and three octaves over them, and sang nothing under 500 Hz. Three switches (`--without-own`, `-close`, `-soft`; all off is v6 byte for byte). (1) OWN: they hear the string as well. When it is let go beside them each one sounds its own note, the one just plucked, and holds it after the pluck has gone (by difference: B flat 2 at 116.6 Hz strongest, G2 3 dB under it, F2 9; about 12 dB under the mix from 60 to 250 Hz): the string has a sustain pedal, and the record's last tail has low notes in it. (2) CLOSE: the wood's closing (bars 153 to 168) was the one stopping with no mark. The strings are let go again for it and nobody stops them: the wood closes, they hear less and less of it, and they go quiet by themselves (17 dB across the section, with no fader moving). (3) SOFT: the metal let go 2 dB lower and its remembered hall 3 dB lower (1.4 kHz). 99.81 and 99.75 on two seeds (v6 99.63 and 98.07; the second seed's gain is the scorer no longer splitting bar 65, which nothing here aimed at). Not heard by its composer.
