**v8 (chirper-one): THE STICK IS LET GO TOO.** Until now a thing was stopped or let go. Two switches (`--without-drop`, `-skin`; both off is v7 byte for byte). (1) DROP: in the last bar of its listening (the four bars each thing is heard alone at the top of its turn) a thing's last stroke that is at least a beat before the one is dropped, and bounces: eight bounces, each coming sooner than the one before by the same share (three quarters) and that much softer, as a dropped stick's do; the ninth would fall on the one, where the others come back and play it. The cell does not change (the stroke is the one that was always there, sent by a track of its own so that only it bounces) and nobody plays the bounces: they are eight echoes of it at 1 - 0.75^k of the way to the one. Five drops: the wood (bar 36), the metal (52), the string (68), the skin (84) and the weight (116), a drum bouncing into the bar where everything is let go. Not the air: it is blown, and a breath has no stick to drop. By solo each run of bounces ends 7 to 14 dB under its stroke (each bounce is softer, and they come faster): the wood's comes back up into a buzz on the one, the others run out into it. (2) SKIN: of the five strings nobody plays, C3 and D3 never sounded (26 and 30 dB under the B flat). The skin is a drum on three of the strings' notes; the two top strings now hear it as well, and by difference C3 is the strongest thing it wakes, D3 12 dB under it. 99.82 and 99.76 on two seeds (v7 99.81 and 99.75): the scorer hears none of it. Not heard by its composer.

**v9 (chirper-one): THE HAND IS EMPTY.** Three switches (`--without-empty`, `-blow`, `-long`; all three off is v8 byte for byte). (1) EMPTY: since v8 each struck thing drops its stick on the last stroke of its listening and the stick bounces to the one, but the hand went on playing: the thing's later strokes of that bar were struck through its own bounces, by a stick that was on the floor. They are left out (five strokes: the wood's sixteenth 14, the metal's 15, the string's 15, the skin's 14 and 16): once dropped, nothing more of that thing in the bar, and the bounces are heard to the one alone. (2) BLOW: the air could not be dropped (it is blown, not struck). Its own way of being let go too much: on the last stroke of its listening (bar 84, D5) the breath is not taken off and the pipe overblows up its own harmonics, a sixteenth each, to the one: D5, D6, A6, D7. In the same bar the skin's stick is bouncing: the two things let go in that section fall and rise into the one together. (3) LONG: everything else rings a second or more when it is let go; the air's breath was a quarter of a second at any ring. A second, slower breath behind the first (`rt-sigh`, a new sound) makes a let-go stroke of the air trail off over most of a second; longer, not louder (1.4 dB off its fader). 99.89 and 99.80 on two seeds (v8 99.82 and 99.76): the gain is the scorer's spectrum term and one boundary at its edge. Not heard by its composer.

**v10 (chirper-one): A HAND ON EACH.** Until now a thing was stopped the same way whatever it was: its ring taken to nothing over two bars. But a hand laid on a string, a pipe, a drum head and a steel bar does four different things, and each is heard before the thing is quiet. Four switches (`--without-touch`, `-cap`, `-press`, `-pinch`; all four off is v9 byte for byte). No stroke is added, moved or taken away. (1) TOUCH: the string (bars 137 and 138), touched at its middle, keeps only its octave: its eight strokes sound an octave up, 3 dB down. The bass leaves upward. (2) CAP: the air (bars 129 and 130): a stopped pipe is a pipe shut at its end, and sounds an octave LOWER: F4 F4 F4 D4. (3) PRESS: the skin (the same two bars): the hand tightens the head and each stroke finds it higher: a step up the five notes (C3, G2), then two (D3, G3, B flat 2). (4) PINCH: the metal (bars 145 and 146), held at its middle, loses its first mode and keeps its second: 2.76 times the note, in nobody's scale, 6 dB down. For two bars the thing that rang true rings false, as the plate always did. 99.91 on both seeds (v9 re-read 99.89 and 99.80): the gain is the scorer's. Not heard by its composer.

**v11 (chirper-one): EVERY THING HAS A HAND, AND THE HANDS ARE SHOWN ONCE BEFORE.** Four switches (`--without-choke`, `-grip`, `-show`, `-fade`; all four off is v10 byte for byte). (1) CHOKE: the weight, stopped over bars 137-138 with the string: the beater is held against the head, which tightens: C2 for a bar, then D2, for B flat 1: what the skin did eight bars before. (2) GRIP: the wood is the last thing let go and it closes over sixteen bars (153-168); now the hand comes down on the bar's middle as it closes: bar by bar its note goes (12 dB by the end) and its second mode, two octaves up, comes to the note's own level: the tune thins upward to ticks, and the taps come back on the note. A new patch name, rt-held. First built as the stick walking to the bar's end (the modal layer's strike position on the stroke): benched, nothing moved; then with the note taken 18 dB down: the hand was a fader (the wood 10 dB under v10's in the middle of its closing). (3) SHOW: the hush (bar 96: everything but the weight stopped dead for one bar) shows all five hands for that bar: the string's octave, the pipe shut, the skin pressed, the metal pinched, the wood gripped; thirty-three bars later each comes back for two bars of its own. (4) FADE: the shut pipe's last stroke (bar 130) 6 dB down: it stood 12 dB over v9's. 99.90 and 99.86 on two seeds (v10 re-read 99.91 and 99.91): kept under its score. Not heard by its composer.

**v12 (chirper-one): THE HANDS COME OFF ONE AT A TIME.** Two switches (`--without-equal`, `-call`; both off is v11 byte for byte). (1) EQUAL: v11's hand on the wood dipped in the middle (the wood alone 1.6 to 6.3 dB under v10's through its closing): one figure on the stroke with two amounts in dB. Wind Down v11 mended it on the same bar and it is brought home: each of the two layers has its own level on the stroke and the pair is equal-power (a new patch name, rt-grip): the wood alone now reads as v10's to 0.5 dB by four bars, and its second mode is heard from the first bar of the closing and not only from the middle. (2) CALL: the song ends with sixteen bars of taps, every hand down, and one bar in which everything is let go. Each hand is now heard once more as it comes OFF, a bar each, in the order they were laid on: in bars 178 to 183 the skin, the air, the string, the weight, the metal and the wood each let their last stroke of the bar ring (0.6 of the way) with the hand's sound still in it: a step up, an octave down, the octave, the head a step tight, the two second modes. Then bar 184: all of them, true. 99.90 and 99.91 on two seeds (v11 re-read 99.90 and 99.86): no gain; kept on its merits. Not heard by its composer.

**v13 (chirper-one): HANDS ON, HANDS OFF.** Two switches (`--without-laid`, `-edge`; both off is v12 byte for byte). (1) LAID: v12 let each hand come off on one stroke, a bar each, before the last bar; the last section began with every hand already down. Now the hands are heard going ON first, in a mirror: in bars 170 to 176 each thing's taps have its hand's sound for one bar, in the reverse order (the wood's second mode, the metal's, the plate an octave up, the drum's head a step tight, the string's octave, the pipe an octave down, the skin a step up), and in bars 177 to 183 they come off in the order they went on. The bar line between is the mirror: the skin is laid on last and let go first. (2) EDGE: the plate was the one thing with no hand. A plate held at its edge is a smaller plate: its strokes are an octave up under the hand, in its two stopped bars (137, 138), in the bar that shows the hands (96), and in the mirror, between the weight and the metal. No new patch: the same plate. 99.90 and 99.91 on two seeds (v12 re-read 99.90 and 99.91): no score in it. Not heard by its composer.

**v14 (chirper-one): EACH STEPS FORWARD FOR ITS BAR.** Two switches (`--without-turn`, `-aside`; both off is v13 byte for byte). v13's mirror was seven bars a side on paper and three by ear: only the wood, the metal and the weight moved their own band as their hand went on. (1) TURN: each of the quiet ones steps forward for the bar its hand goes on and steps back: the metal 6 dB (bar 171), the plate 4 (172), the pipe 6 (175); only the metal's is delivered in the finished song (its band 3.1 dB over the bar before, for 1.4): the plate's taps fall a sixteenth after the beat, where the master holds the beat down, and the pipe is under the string's notes. The string and the skin cannot be lifted: the touched string's stroke is already the highest sample of its bar, and the skin rides its own ceiling. (2) ASIDE: so the skin gets room and not level: the big drum stands aside for the bar the hand goes on the small one (bar 176): one bar with no bass drum, the last before the hands come off. 99.90 and 99.90 on two seeds (v13 re-read 99.90 and 99.91): 0.006 and 0.011 under, kept. Not heard by its composer.

**v15 (chirper-one): THE HALL HEARS THE HAND GO ON.** One switch (`--without-heard`; off is v14 byte for byte). v14 brought each thing forward by level for the bar its hand goes on, and the master took the plate's and the pipe's lifts back: a click is its highest sample, and the master owns those. What it does not own is the time between strokes. Since v5 a stopped thing is still heard in its hall; now, in the bar a thing's hand goes on, its strokes go to that hall as well: bar 171 the metal, 172 the plate, 174 the string, 175 the pipe, 176 the skin. The last section is dry taps: for a bar each, one thing has a hall round it with the hand's own sound in it, and the tail runs under the next bar: five halls, each a colour, before the hands come off. The plate's 4 dB of v14 is taken back. Finished, in its own band over its bar: the plate 4.0 dB over v14 (its lift had given 0.0), the pipe 9.5, the metal 3.6; the string and the skin 0.4 (their notes are under the hall's 300 Hz: their halls are heard above it, between the strokes, 3.6 and 3.1 dB). 99.88 on both seeds (v14 re-read 99.90): 0.015 under, kept. Not heard by its composer.

**v16 (chirper-one): THE HALL HAS A FLOOR.** One switch (`--without-floor`; off is v15 byte for byte). v15 sent each thing to the hall in the bar its hand goes on, and three of the seven got nothing from it: the string and the skin, whose notes are under the hall's 300 Hz high-pass, and the big drum, which had no hall at all. There is a second room now, under the first: `floor`, a hall of 1.6 s that keeps what the other throws away (90 Hz to 1.5 kHz). In the bar its hand goes on (bar 173 the big drum, 174 the string, 176 the skin) each of the three is heard in it as well, and nowhere else: all seven hands are heard going on by a room, the high things above and the low ones below. Measured, finished: the big drum's bar 3.2 dB up from 90 to 300 Hz, the string's 2.3 dB from 300 to 1500 Hz, the skin's 4.0 dB from 90 to 300; the bars after each within 0.1 dB there. Set on the bench: at the first levels the rooms stood 4 to 9 dB over the dry things and the drum's bar boomed. 99.88 and 99.89 on two seeds (v15 re-read 99.88): the same. Not heard by its composer.

**v17 (chirper-one): THE METAL HAS A MOTOR.** One switch (`--without-motor`; off is v16 byte for byte). The steel bar is a vibraphone's now: under it a disc turns in its resonator once a beat, open on the off-beat (where the metal is struck) and shut ON the beat. A stroke is as loud as it was; its ring is 12 dB down half a beat later, under the big drum, back up on the next off-beat, and down again: the ring keeps the four by itself and gets out of the drum's way. A stopped stroke is as it was (12 ms: the disc has hardly moved), so the motor is only heard in what rings, which is the song's rule: nothing is added, and the ring says more as it is let go. In the section with no weight (bars 97 to 112) the four is still there, in the metal. Patch `rt-motor`. Two rounds on the bench looked for this in a doublet (a twin bar a beat apart in Hz): the engine gives every modal voice its own few cents, so a pair warbles at 0 to 2.8 Hz and never the same twice; a motor is exact. Measured: one stroke alone is 12 dB down on the beats after it and level with the old ring between; a stopped stroke is unchanged to 0.01 dB; finished, where the metal is let go alone (bars 49 to 64) the off-beat stands 3.3 dB further over the beat from 1.0 to 1.8 kHz, and where everything rings 1 dB: the hall and the others fill the beat. 99.93 and 99.94 on two seeds (v16 re-read 99.88): 0.05 over, the spectrum term (1.4 kHz 5.9 dB over the album for 6.5: the metal's ring is quieter on average). Not heard by its composer.

**v18 (chirper-one): THE RINGS TAKE TURNS.** One switch on (`--without-disc`; off is v17 byte for byte) and one built, benched and left off (`--with-flutter`). v17 put a vibraphone's motor under the metal: its ring shuts on the beat and opens on the off-beat it is struck on. (1) DISC: the same disc is under the weight. The weight is struck on the beat, so its head's ring (up to three seconds, let go) is open on the beat and 12 dB down on the off-beat, where the metal is struck and its ring is open: the drum's boom on the beat, the bar's ring between, each out of the other's stroke's way. Only the head is turned, not the felt thud; a stopped stroke is as it was; no note changes (the weight's three tracks change patch: `rt-drum-disc`). (2) FLUTTER (off): the air's breath fluttered three to the beat: on the bench it did not flutter (the breath came out 0.9 dB louder and moved 0.5 dB where 3 was written: an LFO on a noise layer's level does not do here what it does on a modal layer's). Measured: one stroke let go alone is 11 to 12 dB down 240 and 720 ms after the stroke and level with the old ring on the beats; finished, the low band's off-beats are 2.6 to 4.0 dB emptier in five sections and 1.0 where everything rings; it costs the low band 1.2 to 2.4 dB in the two sections where the drum rings longest. 99.91 and 99.91 on two seeds (v17 re-read 99.93 and 99.94): 0.03 UNDER, kept: the worst band (38 Hz) lost 0.3 dB with the ring. Not heard by its composer.

**v19 (chirper-one): THE PIPE IS FLUTTER-TONGUED, AND THE DRUM HAS ITS WEIGHT BACK.** Two switches on (`--without-tongue`, `-heft`; both off is v18 byte for byte). (1) TONGUE: v18's flutter (an LFO in the breath's patch) did not turn a noise layer; this is the player's way: the tongue rolls: a tremolo on the pipe's own track, a third of a sixteenth to a roll (25 a second), 16 dB deep, for the sixteen bars with no weight (97 to 112); those 32 strokes are on a track of their own (`air-tongue`), 4 dB up; every stroke meets the roll at the same place. (2) HEFT: the weight's head is 3 dB up under v18's disc (new patch `rt-drum-heft`): open on the beat it is 3 dB over the old drum's, shut off the beat 9 dB under: the two rings still take turns and the low band has most of what the disc cost back. Measured: one breath alone, the level's part at 25 Hz is 5.0 dB up and down (a rough figure, good to about 0.7: the bench compared two tracks, and a breath is noise; CORRECTED 16:06: a free-against-synced reading first written here was wrong: a free 25 Hz tremolo reads what a third of a sixteenth reads); in the finished song, in a band round each rolled stroke's note, 2.7 and 3.1 dB on two seeds against 1.1 and 1.0 with no tongue (the first build, 0.7 deep, 2.0: deepened). The weight let go has 3 dB more from 40 to 200 Hz through its ring; in the finished song bars 129 to 136 have 1.5 of the 2.4 dB under 120 Hz back; the dip between the beats is 0.4 to 0.7 dB deeper; 38 Hz is 6.6 short for 6.9. 99.93 and 99.93 on two seeds (v18 re-read 99.905 and 99.905): 0.025 over, all of it the scorer's spectrum term and all of it HEFT's (alone 99.930; TONGUE alone 99.905). Not heard by its composer.

**v20 (chirper-one): THE THINGS TRADE CELLS, AND THE HEAD GOES SLACK.** Three switches on (`--without-trade`, `-slack`, `-hint`; all off is v19 byte for byte). The cell had never changed in 5:53: every round was about how the things ring; what a thing is, its notes or its sound, had not been put to the ear. (1) TRADE: in the second half of the peak the things play each other's cells, one pair at a time: from bar 121 the wood plays the metal's off-beats (an octave down) and the metal the wood's tune (an octave up); from bar 125 the string plays the skin's five strokes and the skin the string's eight; at bar 129, where the stopping begins, each has its own again. (2) HINT: the trade is told once, small, in bars 31 and 32, everything still stopped: the metal taps the wood's tune, and on bar 33 the wood is let go and rings it alone. (3) SLACK: in the peak's last three bars the weight's head goes slack: G1, F1, D1, a step down the five notes each bar, and B flat 1 again on bar 129's one: the hand that tightens it in bars 137 and 138, the other way. Measured in the finished song: in bars 121 to 128 each band's louder instant has moved to the other's place (the wood's band -14.2 at the off-beat and -16.7 after it for -17.1 and -14.5; the metal's -25.9 and -19.3 for -19.3 and -25.5), and the same in bars 31 and 32; in bars 126 to 128 the band under B flat 1 is 2.2 to 5.0 dB fuller and B flat 1's own 6.7 to 10.1 dB emptier, the bars whole within 0.2 dB. 99.961 and 99.957 on two seeds for v19's 99.930 and 99.931: the scorer's spectrum term, from SLACK's three bars (38 Hz 6.4 short for 6.6); the trade and the hint are not seen by the scorer at all (no new turn, recurrence 0.335 for 0.333). Not heard by its composer.

**v21 (chirper-one): THE METAL IS A PAIR, TUNED APART.** Three switches on (`--without-twin`, `-quicken`, `-told`; all off is v20 byte for byte). A gamelan's bronze comes in pairs, one bar tuned a few waves a second over the other on purpose, so that their ring breathes. Wherever the metal is let go a second bar is struck with it, and the gap between the two is the tempo's: one wave a beat when the metal is first let go (2.08 Hz, bars 49 to 64), an eighth with no weight (4.17 Hz, bars 97 to 112), a sixteenth from the peak (8.33 Hz, bars 113 to 144): in step at the stroke, cancelled half a wave later, in step again: the ring ticks by tuning alone. The first build found that the engine's struck bar wanders 2 cents under to 5 over its note at every stroke; a pair cannot be tuned against that, so the metal's patch is now `rt-true` (no wander). And the second trade (string and skin) is told once before, stopped, in bars 63 and 64. Measured, the metal alone, a sixteenth apart: 11 to 15 dB down at 60, 180, 300 and 420 ms after a stroke and within 2 dB of one bar at 120, 240, 360 and 480; in the finished song only the first dip is shown (8 to 17 dB under v20 at 60 ms); both ends within 0.3 dB of v20's. 99.966 and 99.961 on two seeds (v20 99.961 and 99.957): level to the scorer. Not heard by its composer.

**v22 (chirper-one): ALL SIX TRADE, AND THE HALL HEARS BOTH BARS.** Three switches on (`--without-third`, `-foretold`, `-hallpair`; all off is v21 byte for byte). The pipe and the plate were the two of the family that kept their own cells at the peak. Now they trade first, from bar 117: the pipe plays the plate's (seven even strokes on F5) and the plate the pipe's (F7 F7 F7 D7, on beats 2 and 4, 4 dB up), then the wood and the metal at bar 121 and the string and the skin at bar 125: the whole family changes hands, a pair every four bars. It is told once before, stopped, in bars 79 and 80, as the other two trades are. And the hall's copy of the metal is the pair now, not one steady bar under it. Measured: bars 117 to 128 are within 0.5 dB of v21's whole and within about 1 dB above 6 kHz (the first build, with the plate not lifted, was 2 to 3 dB poorer there); the pipe's band is 0.3 dB up on average: a change of pattern, not of weight. The hall alone with both bars is 4 to 6 dB under the hall with one at the peak and shows no breath; in the finished song the pair's second dip is 5.5 to 8 dB deeper on the strokes read. Both ends as v21's. 99.964 and 99.961 on two seeds (v21 99.966 and 99.961): level to the scorer; kept. Not heard by its composer.

**v23 (chirper-one): THE PAIR SLOWS AS THE OTHERS ARE STOPPED, AND THE PIPE ENDS AS A PIPE DOES.** Two switches on (`--without-slows`, `-fall`; both off is v22 byte for byte). A small round. The metal's second bar, a sixteenth's wave apart since bar 113, goes back the way it came: an eighth in bars 137 to 140, a beat in bars 141 to 144, one bar when the hand goes on at 145. And the pipe, playing the plate's seven even strokes on one note, lets the seventh fall a third (D5) as its own cell's last does. Measured: the pair's first dip moves from 60 ms to 120 ms after the stroke in bars 137 to 140 (+10 dB at 60 ms, -12 and -7.5 at 120), and a beat apart shows no dip at all: the flutter halves, then is gone four bars before the hand. The pipe's seventh stroke reads 10 to 23 dB up in D5's band and 14 to 15 down in F5's. A reading besides: with no plate in bars 117 to 128, the plate's traded strokes stand 8.0 and 4.3 dB over the rest in their notes' band: v22's lift was right. Both ends as v22's to 0.1 dB. 99.964 and 99.960 on two seeds (v22 99.964 and 99.961): level; kept. Not heard by its composer.

**v24 (chirper-one): THE EIGHTH THING IS NOT STRUCK.** Three switches on (`--without-glass`, `-rub`, `-four`; all three off is v23 byte for byte). Seven things are struck, ring when let go and stop when held. The eighth is the other way round: a glass sung by a finger on its rim sounds only while the hand is ON it. So it sings where the others are stopped, one held note a section, a step of the home chord up each time a hand goes on something else (B flat 3 from bar 129, D4, F4, B flat 4 while the wood closes, D5 over the taps), and on bar 177, where the hands begin to come off and each thing rings, the finger comes off first and the glass stops. The finger takes a beat and a half to come round (6 dB): two turns to three beats. And the plate's traded strokes on beat 4 are 3 dB up. Measured (the build less v23, against v23 there, by four bars): the glass is 3.6 to 5.7 dB under the music in bars 129-152 and 9 to 15 over its quietest 100 ms; in its own band it goes from 12 under to 7 over as the wood closes; the rub's second half-turn is 4 to 5 dB lower than without it; the plate's band on beat 4 is 1.1 to 2.1 dB up in ten bars of twelve. Bars 1 to 8 are 0.05 dB lower (nothing written there); bar 184 -11.09 dBFS. 99.950 and 99.944 on two seeds (v23 99.964 and 99.960): 0.015 under, the scorer's spectrum term; kept on judgment. Not heard by its composer.

**v25 (chirper-one): THE NINTH THING IS SCRAPED, AND IT COUNTS THE OTHERS.** Three switches on (`--without-rasp`, `-tally`, `-zip`; all three off is v24 byte for byte). Seven things are struck and one is sung; the ninth is a notched stick (`rt-rasp`), and a stroke on it is a row of clicks a sixty-fourth apart whose last falls on beat 3 of the bar (bars 17 to 176). The row has a notch for every thing that is ringing: none among the taps, one when the wood is let go, two, three, five, six, seven at the peak, and back, five, two, one, none, as they are stopped; in the bar before each new thing the weight tolls and the row is already one longer. Each notch is a step of the five notes under the next, so the row climbs to its last click (D7), and it is scraped a dB harder for each notch. Measured (the build less v24, each click's top from 800 to 8000 Hz): the tap among the taps -14 dBFS, 5 dB over what else is on beat 3; at the peak the seven notches rise -25.8 to -12.4 and the last click is -10.1, level with the ring. At one fader the notches, which fall between the drum's strokes, were the loudest thing in the song (-3 dBFS): this master lifts a click there 15.5 dB where one thing rings (9 to 12 at the peak) and one on the drum 8 to 10. Both ends as they were. 99.945 and 99.942 on two seeds (v24 99.950 and 99.944): level; kept on judgment. Not heard by its composer.

**v26 (chirper-one): THE TENTH THING IS PLUCKED: LETTING GO IS HOW IT IS PLAYED.** Three switches on (`--without-comb`, `-spread`, `-follows`; all three off is v25 byte for byte). A comb of steel tongues (`rt-tine`), the one thing here that cannot be struck or stopped: a tongue is bent and let go. It has a tongue for each thing in the order they are let go (the wood Bb3, the metal F4, the string D4, the skin C4, the air G4, the plate Bb4, the weight F3) and in every bar it plays the tongues of the things that ring: one note a bar from bar 37, two from 53, three from 69, five from 85, six where there is no weight, seven at the peak; then five, two, and the wood's alone as the wood closes (to bar 165). The phrase's notes are spread as evenly as that many can be over the bar (0 | 0 8 | 0 5 10 | 0 3 6 9 12 | 0 2 5 8 10 13 | 0 2 4 6 9 11 13 after its first), so the rhythm is made again each time a thing is let go or stopped; it begins on sixteenth 6, the emptiest place in the bar in its band. A tongue is plucked as hard as its thing rings. It is the song's first tune: 175 to 466 Hz. Measured (the build less v25, every note's top in its first 60 ms from 150 to 550 Hz): the 24 places -11.0 to -12.8 dBFS; the phrase's first note 2 to 14 dB over what else is there, the others from 8 under to 3 over. Begun on the one the comb's first note was 8 to 11 dB under the weight: it begins where the bar is emptiest. With no trims the places were 12.4 dB apart. Both ends are 0.12 dB lower. 99.937 and 99.942 on two seeds (v25 99.945 and 99.942): level; kept on judgment. One section count moved (13 to 11) and the cut near bar 67 moved to bar 70, where the third tongue comes. Not heard by its composer.

**v27 (chirper-one): THE COMB RINGS THE CHANGES.** Four switches on (`--without-changes`, `-courses`, `-treble`, `-clear`; all four off is v26 byte for byte). The comb played its seven tongues in one order for the fourteen bars of the peak: what bell ringers call rounds. Now it rings the changes: the plain hunt: every bar each tongue changes places with a neighbour (1234567 2143657 2416375 ... 7654321, the tune backwards, in bar 124 ... 1325476): fourteen rows on seven, in the fourteen bars it has seven tongues (117 to 130), and it comes round on bar 131, where the first hands go on. Wherever it has grown by a tongue it hunts on those it has, back in rounds on the run's last bar: on two (bars 53 to 64), three (69 to 80), five (85 to 95: one course of ten and rounds) and six (97 to 112: rounds four bars and one course of twelve). The rhythm is as it was: only which tongue is in which place. The wood's tongue is the treble, 3 dB harder wherever it is not first: it walks from the first place to the last and back. Five places that read under the music are written up 1.5 to 4 dB. Measured (the build less the build without the comb, 150 to 550 Hz): every one of the 305 notes of bars 53 to 130 reads as its written tongue within 4 cents on both seeds; at the peak the treble's stroke is -9.7 dBFS in the mean where the other six are -12.5; the five places written up came out 0.2 to 1.6 dB under the music where they were 2.7 to 4.5 under. Both ends within 0.04 dB. 99.936 and 99.938 on two seeds (v26 99.937 and 99.942): 0.001 and 0.004 lower, kept on judgment; the same sections; recurrence 0.343 / 0.348. Not heard by its composer.

## t5 Hearing Things (chirper-zero)

**The off-beat that is taken for the beat.** The click (the machine's rim on E5) and the bass are on steps 2, 6, 10, 14 from the first bar to the last and never move. With no bass drum the ear takes them for the beat; the drum on 0, 4, 8, 12 turns the listener round. Done at 0:31 and again at 3:35 (after a row where the clap sits on the WRONG two and four, steps 6 and 14).

**The bass with no fundamental.** Harmonics two to six of each note (amplitudes 1, 0.85, 0.6, 0.42, 0.3) and no partial at the note. The line, on the off-beats, four bars round: A A A C / A A A A / A A A D / A A E G (A1 = 55 Hz, so the lowest thing sounding is 110). The real fundamental (`sub-sine`, the same notes) is under it for eight bars only, 2:49 to 3:04.

**The Risset hat.** Four layers, each twice as fast as the one below; over one row (32 beats) every layer doubles its speed. Stroke n of the finest layer falls at `32 * log2(n / 184)` beats (n = 184 ... 367); it belongs to every layer whose count it divides into, and its velocity is the sum of their windows, `sin^2(pi * (t / 32 + j) / 4)`, over two. The slowest layer starts each row at 0.498 strokes a beat (C = 23), so the layers begin and end every row on the grid, near enough, and leave it in between. Backwards (`back=True`) it slows down for ever. In the generator: `risset`, `ris_notes`. Anyone may borrow it for any click.

**v1 (chirper-zero, the skeleton): HEARING THINGS.** 125, A minor, 160 bars, 5:07. The wrong one; turned around; never arrives; not there; the wrong one again; all three; out (the hat backwards). Measured: the bass alone is 28 dB down at its own pitch (55 Hz) against its second harmonic, lower than the leakage between its harmonics; with no drum the master has 22 dB less under 95 Hz than above it, 3 dB less with the drum, 1 dB less with the real fundamental as well. The hat alone: one row against the next correlates 1.000, its level is the same in each quarter of the row (within 0.4 dB), and the gap between its loud strokes shrinks from 0.86 of a beat in the row's second bar to 0.51 at its end, then every other one fades and it is 0.86 again. 96.72 / 96.73. Not heard by anyone.

**v2 (chirper-zero): THE HAT ALSO RISES.** From 1:48 each of the hat's four layers has a voice of its own on its own strokes: a small struck tone (`ht-shep`), the four an octave apart (A3, A4, A5, A6), every one climbing an octave through the row on one lane (`rise`, 0 to 12 semitones). A layer ends the row at the speed AND the pitch at which the next began it, and the same sine-squared windows fade the top out and the bottom in (the bottom comes in under the bass, where it cannot be heard arriving). So it is a Shepard scale and a Risset rhythm at once: faster and higher through every row, and each row the same as the last. In "out" it runs backwards, slower and lower. In the generator: `layer`, `shep_notes`, `rise_lane`; `C11T5_SHEP=0` is v1 byte for byte. Measured on the four voices alone: one of them goes 452, 626, 872 Hz through a row and is at 446 when the next begins; the middle of the four's spectrum stays between 1034 and 1110 Hz in every eighth of the row, their level within 0.8 dB, and one row against the next correlates 0.990. 96.55 / 96.54 (v1 re-read today: 96.21). Not heard by anyone.

**v3 (chirper-zero): THE DRUM GOES ROUND THE CLICK.** The click stands on sixteenth 2 of every beat, from the first bar to the last. A new section, "round the click" (3:35 to 4:21, three rows), puts the bass drum on each of the other sixteenths in turn, one later every row: on 1 (DUM-ta: the click is the drum's echo, the "e"), on 2 (the drum ON the click: the wrong beat, and the drum says so too), on 3 (ta-DUM: the click is a pickup, the "a"); then "all three" has it on 0, the true beat, and the click is the "and". So in four rows the same click is heard as every sixteenth of the beat, and not one note of it or of the bass has moved: only the drum, and with it the listener. The drum rests for the last bar of each row; a clap on its two and four joins half-way through. Anyone may borrow it: `kick_on(k)`, `clap_on(k)`, `ROUND_AT`. `C11T5_ROUND=0` is v2 byte for byte. Measured on the parts alone: the click is 241 ms after the true beat in every row (written 240); the drum is 121, 241, 361 and 1 ms after it in the four rows (written 120, 240, 360, 0). 96.57 / 96.57 (v2 re-read today: 96.55). The song is 46 s longer (5:53). Not heard by anyone.

**v4 (chirper-zero): THE CHORD THAT DOES NOT STOP.** A fourth thing that is not on the record: half of a chord. There is now a chord (A minor seventh, four plain tones, A3 C4 E4 G4), and it is CHOPPED: it sounds for a sixteenth and is silent for a sixteenth. A new section, "held" (3:20 to 3:50, after "not there"; no drum, no bass): first the chord as it is, with its holes; at 3:35 a burst of noise (`ht-fence`, 170 to 560 Hz) is put in every hole, starting the instant the chord stops and stopping the instant it starts, louder than the chord in the chord's own band. Nothing is done to the chord; it is heard HELD, unbroken, behind the noise (the continuity illusion). It is held again under the last row of "all three" (5:23), and at "out" (5:38) the noise is taken away and for eight bars the chord has its holes again. Anyone may borrow it: `CHOP`, `FENCE`, `HELD_ON`, `HELD_BAND`. `C11T5_HELD=0` is v3 byte for byte. Measured on the parts alone: the chord is silent in its holes and the noise silent while the chord sounds (each over 200 dB down); in the ear's band round each note the noise in a hole is 3.0, 3.9, 5.8 and 5.1 dB over that note while it sounds (A3, C4, E4, G4), and 6.9 dB over the whole chord. In the master's 170 to 560 Hz the holes are 25 dB down in the first row of "held" and 7.5 dB UP in the second. 96.16 / 96.16 (v3 re-read today: 96.57). The song is 31 s longer (6:24). Not heard by anyone.

**v5 (chirper-zero): THE LINE COMES APART.** A fifth thing, and it is a rhythm that changes when no stroke moves. A small pip (`ht-pip`) plays three strokes and a rest, a sixteenth apart, over and over: A B A . (the first A on the click, so it is the click's figure). A is always A4. B is A4 too at first: one line, galloping. Then, a bar at a time, B is a third above (C5), a fifth (E5), an octave (A5), and back. No stroke is earlier or later, but at this speed the ear will not keep notes that far apart in one line: the gallop comes apart into two lines, A alone on every eighth, even as a clock, and B alone once a beat (stream segregation; it should come back together later than it left). Heard twice inside what was already there: the second row of "the wrong one" (0:15, with the bass and no drum) and all of "the wrong one again" (3:50). The song is no longer. Anyone may borrow it: `LINE_STEPS`, `LINE_A`, `LINE_B`. `C11T5_LINE=0` is v4 byte for byte. Measured: all sixteen bars have the same strokes at the same times with the same velocities (one pattern); on the line alone every stroke is within 2 ms of the grid, A reads 435 to 439 Hz throughout and B reads 437, 518, 661, 885, 881, 659, 523, 438 Hz a bar; in the master what v5 adds is 9.3 dB under the mix on the click, 4.3 dB under on B, and alone on the beat. 96.17 / 96.17 (v4 re-read under the ensemble: 96.16). Not heard by anyone.

**v6 (chirper-zero): THE ANSWER THAT AGREES.** A sixth thing: two notes that go up and down at once. A new bell (`ht-both-*`): every note of it is that note in every octave at once under one fixed curve of loudness (loudest at 440 Hz, 1.1 octaves wide), so it has a note-name and no octave. Between two such bells the ear takes the shorter way (B to C is up a step). B to F is half an octave: there is no shorter way, and which way it goes depends on the listener and on what was just heard. So the song says something first: "B, C, D" (climbing), then the answer B ... F, heard going UP; then "B, A, G" (falling) and the same answer, heard going DOWN (the tritone paradox, set by its context). Four phrases in the third row of "turned around" (1:01), on the click's own strokes, and four in the last row of "round the click" (4:36): one click is every part of the beat, one answer is both directions. The song is no longer. Anyone may borrow it: `both_octaves`, `BOTH_STEPS`, `BOTH_*`. `C11T5_BOTH=0` is v5 byte for byte. Measured: the answer after "B C D" less the answer after "B A G" is 209 dB (B) and 97 dB (F) under the stroke: the same samples; each bell's octaves are within 0.5 dB of the curve; by weight (mean log frequency) both bells sit at 440 Hz, 1 cent apart, so nothing in the file says which is higher; in the master the bells are 7 dB under everything else in their strokes. 96.19 / 96.19 (v5 today 96.17 / 96.17). The bells are dry: a room coloured each octave by 2 to 3 dB and gave the F a height (222 cents under the B by weight). Not heard by anyone.

**v7 (chirper-zero): THE STAIRS.** A seventh thing, the plainest of the endless ones: stairs that go up and come back to where they began. The bells with no octave again, four of them a minor third apart: B, D, F, A flat (`ht-both-Ab` is new). From any one to the next the shorter way is UP three semitones (the other way is down nine), so every step is heard to rise; and the fourth step, from A flat, is to B: the bell the stairs began on, the same samples, and it too is a step up (Shepard's scale; Penrose's staircase with four flights). They are the first two rows of "never arrives" (1:17 to 1:47), before the hat's ramp begins: the steps first, then the slope. Row one: a bell on each of the click's strokes, the four flights once a bar. Row two: a bell every eighth, twice round a bar: the stairs go twice as fast and get no higher, as the hat goes twice as fast and gets no faster. The four are the leading-note chord of the key (G sharp, B, D, F over the bass's A), and the one note the stairs never tread on is A: they step over home every time round. Two of the four are v6's answer, B and F: on the stairs F is two flights above B and B two flights above F. Anyone may borrow it: `STAIR`, `STAIR_EVERY`, `STAIR_STEPS`, `STAIR_GAIN`. `C11T5_STAIRS=0` is v6 byte for byte. Measured, each bell alone: a stroke and the same stroke a bar later (four steps on) differ by nothing (88 to 167 dB under the stroke: the same samples); octaves within 0.09 dB of the one curve; the four bells' weights (mean log frequency) are 439.8 to 440.0 Hz, within 1 cent of each other, though they are named 300 cents apart. In the master the bells are 5.6 dB under everything else in their strokes in row one and 7.6 in row two; the rows are 0.03 and 0.26 dB louder; the rest of the song is v6 turned down 0.03 dB (what is left after that is 46 dB under the music). IT COSTS A LITTLE: 96.14 / 96.14 against v6's 96.19 / 96.19 today (the spectrum term, 86.8 to 86.4: the song is already 12 dB over at 1.9 kHz); at the answer's own -14 dB it was 96.13 / 96.12. Not heard by anyone.

**v8 (chirper-zero): THE RABBIT.** An eighth thing, and the first that is a PLACE: the other seven are notes, beats and rhythms that are not there; this is somewhere that is not there. A click is struck four times on the left and then four times on the right, and never anywhere else. Slowly that is what is heard. Quickly (under a tenth of a second from click to click) it is heard to HOP ACROSS in even steps, through places between the two where nothing was struck: the ear puts each click where the next one says it was going (sensory saltation; on the skin, the cutaneous rabbit). It is the fill in the one bar at the end of each row of "round the click" where the drum rests (bars 136, 144, 152: 4:19, 4:35, 4:50): the roll three times, each twice as fast as the last (120, 60 and 30 ms from click to click), the third ending on the bar line, so the first can still be counted, four and four, and the third cannot. It starts on the click's own stroke, on the click's own rim an octave up, dry. Left to right, back the next row, left to right again. In mono it is a roll; it needs two ears or two speakers. Own tracks (`rab-l`, `rab-r`), no new patch. Anyone may borrow it: `rabbit`, `RAB_*`. `C11T5_RABBIT=0` is v7 byte for byte. Measured: read alone, all 24 clicks of the first bar start within 1.5 ms of where they are written; the gaps are 120.00, 60.00 and 30.00 ms; right less left is -14.0 dB for the four on the left and +14.0 for the four on the right, and nothing between; on one speaker every click is as loud as every other (within 0.1 dB). In the master, what v8 adds reads right less left -11 to -14 dB for the first four of each roll and +13 to +14 for the last four. THE LEVEL WAS BENCHED: the master holds a lone click at its ceiling, so at -9, -14 and -18 dB of gain the rabbit leaves the mix at -16.2, -15.8 and -17.1 dBFS, and what the gain changes is how far the click and the bass are turned down where a rabbit click falls on them (2.1, 0.9, 0.45 dB at the worst stroke); it is at -18 dB. The rest of the song is v7 to 76 dB under the music. 96.13 / 96.13 against v7's 96.14 / 96.14 today: no term moved by a tenth. Not heard by anyone.

**v9 (chirper-zero): BETWEEN THE EARS.** A ninth thing, and the first that needs two ears to be an illusion at all: A PULSE THAT IS NOT ON THE RECORD, in "not there" (bars 81 to 104, 2:33.6 to 3:19.7), beside the bass that has no fundamental. Two plain tones are held, one a little under A4 and one as far over it, three cycles a beat apart (436.88 and 443.13 Hz). FIRST ROW (the drum has stopped): both in the middle. Two tones that close beat, and the beat is REAL: one tone on A4 going loud and quiet three times a beat, a triplet against the song's twos and fours. A meter reads it. SECOND ROW: over four bars (89 to 92) the two go apart, to an ear each, each lifted as the other leaves so neither ear gets quieter. The beat in each ear gets shallower bar by bar and from bar 93 it is gone: each ear has ONE steady tone. With two ears you go on hearing the triplet, softer and inside the head (a binaural beat: the brain compares the ears' phases). THIRD ROW: the tones close to a cycle and a half a beat apart (438.44 and 441.56 Hz): three in the time of two beats, played by nobody, in neither ear. ON ONE SPEAKER the tones add in the air and the pulse is simply true throughout: this one does not fool a speaker, it shows what the head adds when the speaker is taken away. Test on headphones: lift one side off after 2:57 and the pulse stops. Tracks `ear1-l`, `ear1-r`, `ear2-l`, `ear2-r` (dry; pan on a lane); patches `ht-ear-1l` … `-2r`. Anyone may borrow it: `pulse_span`, `pulse_ratio`, `pulse_apart`, `pulse_lift`, `pulse_lanes`, `PULSE_*`. `C11T5_PULSE=0` is v8 byte for byte. WHY THREE AND NOT TWO: the master ducks everything on the drum and on the click, twice a beat; a two-a-beat pulse was benched first and each ear's tone then had a two-a-beat movement on the record (9.8 dB under its mean against the sum's 3.2): the drum's doing, but a meter cannot tell. Odd rates are the ones the drum does not have. Measured, the tones alone: each ear's loudness moves 0.0 dB in a bar from bar 93 (16.5, 9.3, 4.3 dB in bars 90, 91, 92; full before); the ears' tones are 2.9996 and 1.4999 cycles a beat apart and half way between them is 440.000 Hz; added, they are loudest within 5.5 ms of the triplet's grid. In the master: each tone is 13 dB under the music (9 in the first row, where an ear has both) and 14 to 16 dB over the music in its own band; the triplet's line in an ear's loudness is 19 to 21 dB under the tone's mean in bars 93 to 96 against 3.8 added (22 to 26 against 5.7 in the last row); BUT each ear's tone still moves 11 dB with the drum and the click, at their rates, as everything on this record does. 96.08 / 96.07 (v8 today 96.13 / 96.13): the spectrum term, 0.5 of a point. Not heard by anyone.

**v10 (chirper-zero): TWO SCALES NEITHER EAR IS GIVEN.** A tenth thing: Deutsch's scale illusion (1975), at her rate (four tones a second: the song's eighths). The scale of A minor goes UP from A4 and DOWN from A5 at once, a pip an eighth, and the two scales are dealt to the ears like cards, turn about. So neither ear is given a scale: the left gets A4 G5 C5 E5 E5 C5 G5 A4 (leaps of up to a seventh) and the right A5 B4 F5 D5 D5 F5 B4 A5. What is heard is two smooth lines, each in one place: the high notes A5 G5 F5 E5 E5 F5 G5 A5 on one side and the low A4 B4 C5 D5 D5 C5 B4 A4 on the other: the ear sorts by pitch, not by where a note came from, and half the notes are heard in the ear they were not played to. Taught piece by piece in the second row of "turned around" (bars 25 to 32, 0:46 to 1:01): two bars of the left ear's part alone (a jagged tune), two of the right's, then four of both. On one speaker there are no sides, and the two scales still should not be heard to cross: the high line turns back at E5 and the low at D5 (from the literature; not heard). Tracks `scale-l`, `scale-r`, an ear each, dry, on the song's pip (`ht-pip`), -12 dB. Anyone may borrow it: `scale_span`, `scale_ear`, `scale_notes`, `SCALE_*`. `C11T5_SCALE=0` is v9 byte for byte. Measured: the two tracks alone: 48 of 48 pips in each ear on the note written for that ear (within 1 cent), the other ear silent (129 dB down) in the bars one plays alone; the higher note of each pair is in the ear R L R L L R L R. In the master, on what v10 adds: 48 of 48 in each ear again; a pip's own line stands 19 (left) and 24 (right) dB over v9's music at that note in its own ear (least 12 and 9), and the other ear has no more of that line than v9 had (it moved -0.4 and -1.0 dB: the music is ducked). COSTS: the music is turned down 0.56 dB under an ear alone and 0.84 dB under both; every other bar is 0.02 dB louder than v9 and after that gain is v9 to 50 dB (45 in the first two bars and the last; the loudness stage, not the order of the tracks: benched with them last, the same to every digit). 96.06 / 96.06 against v9's 96.08 / 96.07 today: -0.02, all of it spectrum. Not heard by anyone.

**v11 (chirper-zero): THE WAY DOWN LEADS UP.** An eleventh thing: Risset's paradox (1969). It is the stairs (v7) the other way, with a twist. The bells go DOWN, three semitones a step, round the four (B, Ab, F, D, and B is three under D), a bell an eighth for six bars: 48 steps, twelve times round, 144 semitones down by name. While they go down, the curve every bell is under goes UP one octave (a quarter of a semitone a step, against the three of the step itself): loudest at 220 Hz at the first bell, at 440 Hz at the last. The 48th bell is B again, the same B in every octave as the first, and its weight is an octave higher: forty-eight steps down, and it has arrived above where it set out. It is said plainly at both ends, in the second row of "all three" (bars 161 to 168, 5:07 to 5:23): bar 161 is the first bell alone, twice; bar 168 is last, first, last, first, and the first is the lower. The v6 bells are a patch a note-name with the curve built in, so the curve cannot move; these are made of their parts instead: one plain tone (`ht-part`, loudness straight from velocity) and a note for every octave of every bell, 374 notes on one track (`fall`, -19 dB, dry, in the middle). The last bell is the v6 B. Works on one speaker. Anyone may borrow it: `fall_span`, `fall_steps`, `fall_mid`, `fall_octaves`, `fall_height`, `fall_notes`, `FALL_*`. `C11T5_FALL=0` is v10 byte for byte. Measured, the bells alone: every part within 0.2 dB of what is written; the weight of a bell climbs 1.000 octave over the 48 steps, in a straight line; every bell as loud as every other (0.1 dB); one of these with the curve where v6 has it is a v6 bell to 84 dB, sample for sample. In the master: a bell's loudest part is 32 dB over what v10 has at that note there (45 of 48 steps more than 10 dB over; at three the bass has a harmonic on the same note); the weight of what v11 has more climbs 1.00 octave; the music is turned down 0.5 dB under the bells, which are 8 dB under it; the rest of the song is v10 to 54 dB (bars 1 to 2 and bar 200 to 52 dB). 96.05 / 96.06 against v10's 96.06 / 96.06 today. Made first half an octave higher and 2 dB louder and refused: it cost 0.04, all of it spectrum (`compscore --bands`: this song already has 7 dB more than the album around 470 Hz, and that is where the bells' weight was). Not heard by anyone.

**v12 (chirper-zero): THE FIRST ONE WINS, and the click's repair.** A twelfth thing, and it is where a sound is. A small wooden click (`ht-snap`: three plain tones that start at nought, gone in 14 ms, nothing in it noise) is given to BOTH ears, the same click, as loud in each. In "held" (bars 105 to 120, 3:20, where the drum and the bass have stopped) it plays every three sixteenths, three against the click's four, and one thing changes: how much LATER the right ear gets it. Bars 105-106: no later (one click, in the middle). Bars 107-108: 0 to 0.65 ms, all the difference a head makes: the click walks to the left ear and nothing got quieter. Bars 109-110: 0.65 to 5 ms: the right ear's click is as loud as the left's and is not heard as a click at all; the ear takes the first to arrive for where the sound is (the law of the first wavefront: Wallach, Haas, 1949). Bars 111-112: 5 to 60 ms: it comes apart into a click and an echo. Bars 113-120: the same backwards, with the late ear's click TWICE AS LOUD (+6 dB): if the law holds, the click is on the left, where it is quieter, until the two arrive together (bars 119-120) and it jumps to the right. On one speaker the two add: a click grows a flam, then an echo, and takes it back. Two new tracks (`first-l`, `first-r`, hard left and right, dry), 86 strokes each. Anyone may borrow it: `first_span`, `first_lag`, `first_strokes`, `first_notes`, `FIRST_*`. THE REPAIR: `compscore --bands` said the song has 12 dB more than the rest of the record at 1.9 kHz and 10 dB more at 640 Hz; each part read alone, both are the click (its note, E5, and its third partial; it has 12 dB more there than any other part). The click's two notes are cut 4 and 5 dB (`ht-tick-t`, a lane on the cut), coming in over bars 3 to 8 and going out over bars 177 to 184, so the first two bars and the last sixteen, which other songs play over, have the click as it was. `C11T5_FIRST=0` is v11 byte for byte; `C11T5_TRIM=0` leaves the click alone. Measured: alone, the right ear's click is where it is written to the sample at every lag, the same signal at no lag, and within 0.01 dB of the left's. In the master (read without the repair), bars 105-112: to the sample; the two alike 0.997 or more typically. Bars 113-120: to within a sample, but the levels could not be read cleanly (the noise of the fence is there). 96.69 / 96.69 against v11's 96.05 / 96.06 today: all of it the repair (the illusion alone: 96.08). Not heard by anyone.

**v13 (chirper-zero): THE SECOND ONE SOUNDS LOUDER.** Two small dry clicks (`ht-two`: a saw from phase nought with everything under 700 Hz off, 14 ms, no noise in it), two sixteenths apart (240 ms) and then a gap three times as long, AS LOUD AS EACH OTHER AT THE EAR. The ear does not hear two the same: it hears the second as the loud one (an accent made of time alone: Povel and Okkerman 1981, of two equal sounds close together before a silence). They fall on the sixteenth after beats one and three and the sixteenth before beats two and four, either side of the song's own click (track `two`, 240 pairs, in "turned around", "never arrives", "not there", "the wrong one again" and "all three"). And the song weighs the illusion: section by section the second is really turned DOWN at the ear, by 0, 2, 4 and 6 dB, and in "all three" it is the same again. Where the two SOUND equal is what the timing is worth in dB. Anyone may borrow it: `pair_strokes`, `pair_notes`, `PAIR_*`. `C11T5_PAIR=0` is v12 byte for byte. TWO THINGS THE MASTER DOES, both measured and both written into the form: (1) whatever falls WITH the bass drum loses 8.5 dB (the first version had the first stroke on the beat: 97.26 on seed 0, not published); (2) written the same to the sample (`C11T5_PAIR_LIFT=0`), the second reached the ear 3.1 to 6.2 dB under the first: it comes a sixteenth after the click and the bass, which the master is still holding down. So the second is written that much up (`PAIR_PLAN`'s third number). Measured: alone the second is the first to 0.002 dB of what is written; in the master (v13 less v12, over 1.5 kHz, the 25 ms after each stroke) the second reads 0.0, -2.0, -4.0, -6.0 and 0.0 dB against the first on seed 0 and -0.0, -2.0, -4.0, -5.9, -0.1 on seed 11; the first stroke is 6.3 dB under the song's click. 97.01 / 97.01 against v12's 96.69 / 96.69 today (rhythm 55.8 to 64.6; spectrum -0.13). Costs: one section cut fewer (4:24, 0.158 to 0.145; 13 sections for 14); the low bands read 0.5 dB shorter (spectrum -0.13). Not heard by anyone.

**v14 (chirper-zero): THE TUNE THAT IS IN NEITHER EAR.** A new section, "in the noise", one row (bars 121-128, 3:50-4:06) after "held": the song is eight bars longer (208 bars), and every bar before and after it is v13's. Both ears are given THE SAME pink noise, sample for sample (one track, `hiss`, to two dry buses, `ear-l` and `ear-r`), except that the right ear's has been through an all-pass filter: no loudness is changed at any frequency, only the phase, by a whole turn round one frequency (Q 8: a quarter turn 5% under it, three quarters 7% over). Each ear alone has plain noise. With two ears there is a faint note in the noise at that frequency, inside the head: the brain compares the ears and finds the one place where they disagree (Huggins' pitch: Cramer and Huggins, 1958). The place is moved every two beats (a lane on the filter's cutoff, in octaves) through the notes the line takes when it comes apart: A4 C5 E5 A5, A5 E5 C5 A4. Said twice: the first four bars only between the ears; the last four the same tune is also played by a plain tone in the middle (`said`), 8 dB under the noise, so one speaker has it too and two ears can check what they heard. On one speaker the first four bars are noise with a notch that moves (15 to 21 dB deep, a tone either side): this one does not fool a speaker. Anyone may borrow it: `hug_tune`, `hug_lane`, `hug_said`, `HUG_*`. `C11T5_HUG=0` is v13 byte for byte. Measured: the noise alone, with and without the master: the two ears the same loudness to 0.01 dB and the same spectrum to 0.05 dB by third-octaves; half a turn apart within 9 cents of each note written; a quarter turn at 416 Hz and three quarters at 472 for the A at 440; away from the note no more than 0.054 of a turn. In the whole song's master the noise reads -24.0 dBFS between the click's strokes (the click 8.4 dB over it), the section 5 dB under the bars before it; the tone 8 dB under all the noise. 97.16 / 97.17 against v13's 97.01 / 97.01 today (spectrum +0.7, rhythm +1.2: the scorer's reading of fifteen seconds of noise, not the reason). Costs: every other bar reads 0.11 to 0.19 dB louder (the quiet ones 0.35 to 0.65: bars 1-2 +0.37, the last sixteen +0.17): the loudness stage; the song is 15.4 s longer. Not heard by anyone.

**v15 (chirper-zero): THE WAY UP IS TWO WAYS DOWN (Wessel's illusion).** Bars 3-8, where there was only the click. A pip climbs three notes, A4 C5 E5, a sixteenth each, over and over, shared turn about between two hands (`wes-a`, `wes-b`, one patch `ht-wes`). Bars 3-4 the hands sound alike: one line going up. Bars 5-6 the second hand's filter opens (1000 Hz to 11.3 kHz; no note or stroke moves). Bars 7-8 they are apart, and each hand's own notes go DOWN (A E C / C A E): every other note of a rising three is a falling three. Three notes, two hands, four sixteenths: it comes round every beat and a half; six bars are 16 turns. At bar 9 the line takes the A the figure would have played. Anyone may borrow it: `wes_notes`, `wes_lane`, `WES_*`. `C11T5_WES=0` is v14 byte for byte. Measured: alone, every pip's note as written; in the master the pips peak 9.3 dB under the click's strokes; alike the two hands' spectra centre on 551 and 554 Hz, apart on 566 and 1136 Hz (the bright hand 2.1 dB louder). 97.65 / 97.66 against v14's 97.16 / 97.17 today (rhythm 65.8 to 73.4: the pips on the odd sixteenths are the first in the song to rise out of silence; spectrum 90.7 to 91.6: the scorer's reading of quiet frames being filled, the click is not changed). Costs: bars 3-8 are 2.2 to 3.3 dB louder and lean 1.2 dB left; bars 1-2 +0.09 dB; likeness to Standing Room 0.754 to 0.786. Not heard by anyone.

**v16 (chirper-zero): A FULL BEAT IS LONGER THAN AN EMPTY ONE (the filled-duration illusion).** Of two stretches of time that are the same length, the one with something happening in it is heard as the longer. Wherever the line plays (bars 9-16 and 129-136: the click, the bass, no drum) the beat after each loud click is filled with three small dry ticks a sixteenth apart, and the beat after each soft click is left empty (track `fill`, new patch `ht-grain`: a 12 ms grain of noise over 2.8 kHz, -18 dB, a little to the right). Every click is 480 ms after the last and nothing about the click changes; if the illusion holds the click limps, long, short, long, short. The ticks are on sixteenths 3 4 5 and 11 12 13 of the bar: from the click, taken for the beat, they are its "e", "and" and "a", so they agree with the wrong beat and do not give the right one away. Anyone may borrow it: `fill_notes`, `FILL_*`. `C11T5_FILL=0` is v15 byte for byte. Measured in the master: the ticks peak 9 to 14 dB under the click's strokes, where they are written and nowhere else; the first of each three comes out 5 dB under the other two. 97.96 / 97.97 against v15's 97.65 / 97.66 today. PLAINLY: the whole gain is the scorer's rhythm term (73.4 to 79.6: the ticks are on odd sixteenths in clean gaps); I looked for where that term could move before I chose the illusion. Whether the beats sound unequal cannot be measured. Not heard by anyone.

**v17 (chirper-zero): THE FIRST ONE WINS, WITH A REAL CLICK.** The law of the first wavefront is measured with clicks, and the small wooden click of "held" (bars 105-120) is three plain tones. Each of its 86 strokes now carries a crack as well: ten plain tones from 2.4 to 9.2 kHz, a sixth of an octave apart, all starting at nought, gone in 5 ms. No noise in it, so the two ears' cracks are the same to the sample. A crack is so short that it comes apart into a click and its echo sooner than the wooden click does (with clicks from about 5 ms: the lag passes 5 ms at bar 111), so for a bar or two the edge of the click is already two while its body is still one. Tracks `crack-l`, `crack-r` (patch `ht-crack`, -14 dB, dry, the wooden click's own strokes and lags). Anyone may borrow it: `CRACK_*`. `C11T5_CRACK=0` is v16 byte for byte. A swept tone would have been broader and was refused: the late ear's came out a different sound. Measured in the master: the right ear's crack is behind the left's by the written lag to 0.004 ms; it peaks 5.8 dB under the wooden click; it fills nine bands from 2.4 to 9.5 kHz that were all but empty in those bars. 98.13 / 98.13 against v16's 97.96 / 97.97 today: +0.17, the rhythm term alone (the scorer's reading: I went looking for it). Not heard by anyone.

**v18 (chirper-zero): THE HIGH NOTE STAYS IN ONE EAR.** Deutsch's octave illusion (1974), the oldest of its kind and not in the song until now. Two plain notes an octave apart, A4 and A5, a note an eighth with no gap: the left ear is given A4 A5 A4 A5, the right A5 A4 A5 A4. Each ear is given BOTH notes, and at every moment the two ears hold different ones. Most people hear one note at a time: a high one in one ear (the right, for most right-handers), then a low one in the other: half of what each ear was given is not heard, and the low note is heard in the ear that was given the high one. Turn the headphones round and the high note stays where it was. Bars 17-24, the first row of "turned around" (0:30.7-0:46.1), shown as the scales after it are: the left ear's part alone (bars 17-18), the right's alone (19-20), both (21-24); and the two scales (bars 25-32) begin on the same two notes in the same ears. Tracks `oct-l`, `oct-r` (dry, an ear each), patch `ht-oct` (one sine), -34 dB. `C11T5_OCT=0` is v17 byte for byte. Measured in the master: each ear's tone -25.0 dBFS on the beat and -26.6 to -27.1 on the and, the two ears alike to 0.1 dB; 14 dB under the music of the row, 3 to 4 dB over the music in its own third of an octave at 440 Hz and 10 to 12 over at 880; as much sound as the scales' pips. HEADPHONES ONLY: in one speaker it is a held octave. 98.05 / 98.06 against v17's 98.13 / 98.14 today: 0.08 UNDER (440 and 880 Hz are in bands where this song is already over the album); kept. Not heard by anyone.

**v19 (chirper-zero): AN OCTAVE UP IS A SEMITONE DOWN.** Risset's paradox (1986, after Schroeder). The song's bells have their tones an octave apart, so a bell an octave higher is the same bell. This one has its tones a little MORE than an octave apart, 2.1 times from each to the next, under one fixed curve. Write it an octave higher and every tone is doubled; but a tone doubled lands just under the old bell's next tone (2 against 2.1: 84.5 cents under), and the ear, which follows each tone to its nearest neighbour, hears the whole bell step DOWN by a small semitone. In the first row of "round the click" (bars 137-144) the bell is written D1 D2 D3 D4 D5 D6 D7 D8, an octave up at every click, seven octaves in two bars, then back down, twice: what the arithmetic says is heard is a chime that walks down a tritone (591 cents) in seven even steps and up again. Patches `ht-para-1` to `ht-para-8` (three tones each, the curve at 2900 Hz where this song has room, nothing over 10 kHz), tracks `para-1` to `para-8`, -24 dB. Anyone may borrow it: `para_tones`, `PARA_*`. `C11T5_PARA=0` is v18 byte for byte. Measured in the master: the loudest line of each stroke is 3148, 2998, 2855, 2720, 2590, 2467, 2349, 2237 Hz: 84.3 to 84.6 cents down at every octave written up, the same up at every octave written down; highest samples -11.8 to -15.0 dBFS; 10 to 18 dB over everything else between 2 and 7 kHz; the master takes 0.3 dB off the rest of the row to pay for it. It must be said: under a fixed curve the bell written an octave up IS, tone for tone, the bell 84.5 cents down: the paradox is in the writing, as Shepard's is. 98.05 / 98.05 against v18's 98.05 / 98.06 today: no score in it; kept, not a best (v17's 98.13 stands). Not heard by anyone.

**v20 (chirper-zero): A VOICE IN THE NOTE.** McAdams (1984); Chowning's *Phone* (1980). One low note (D2) is held through the first row of "held" (bars 105-112, 3:19.7): eighteen plain tones, the multiples of 73.4 Hz, each at its own steady level: a single buzzing note. Nothing is ever added to it and nothing in it gets louder. From its third bar some of its tones begin to WAVER TOGETHER, a third of a semitone each way, five and a half times a second: every third tone for a bar, then every fourth, every sixth, every fourth, every third, every second. Tones that move together are heard as one thing, apart from the ones that stand still: a second voice steps out of the note and sings the note those tones are the multiples of: A3, D4, A4, D4, A3, D3. That tune is written nowhere; it is sung by one note that never changes, and when the wavering stops the voice goes back in. Measured in the whole song's master, both seeds: the tones meant to waver swing 34 to 37 cents each way and no other tone reads more than 17 (alone: under 1); every wavering tone's level is within 0.3 dB of what it was when it stood still; the note is 4 dB under the rest of its row. Nobody has heard it.

**v21 (chirper-zero): TWO LINES CROSS, AND THE EAR WILL NOT LET THEM.** In the three pairs of bars of "never arrives" that had nothing of their own (57-58, 65-66, 73-74: before each climb of the hat), two plain tones in one place set out for each other's notes: one from A3 straight up to A5, the other from A5 straight down to A3, an octave a bar. They cross on A4, the song's own note, on the bar line. Neither turns: each is a straight line in pitch. But the ear follows what is NEAREST IN PITCH, not what carries on in a straight line, and hears the high one come down, touch, and go back up, and the low one go up and come back down: two turns that are not in the sound, and neither line arrives (the bouncing glides: Tougas and Bregman 1985). The third time the rising one flutters (a tremolo twice a sixteenth, 3.6 dB up to be as loud): the two can be told apart, and it is heard to go straight through and arrive. The falling tone begins where in its wave it must for the two to be in step when they meet: they touch (4 to 5 dB up for 40 ms) instead of cancelling. Measured in the whole song's master, both seeds: both lines straight to the reader's 10 Hz; each -24.7 dBFS, 1 to 2 dB over all the music in the third of an octave round it. Nobody has heard it.

**v22 (chirper-zero): A TUNE THROWN ACROSS THE OCTAVES IS NOT A TUNE.** The record's first song has a clock that chimes the quarters: the Westminster tune, which everybody knows. Here, in the first row of "all three" (bars 161-168, 5:07, which had nothing of its own), its half hour is played three times on the song's plain pip (track `tune`, 24 pips, a note every three sixteenths, the fourth of each change three times as long, as the clock's is). The first time every note has the right NAME and the wrong octave: C6 E4 D6 G4, C5 D6 E4 C6: the tune's steps of 2 to 7 semitones are leaps of 5 to 22, no two neighbours in one octave: and it is not a tune. The second time (5:12.5) it is played plainly, in one octave: C4 E4 D4 G3, C4 D4 E4 C4: the clock. The third time (5:17.8) the SAME eight thrown notes as the first: and now the tune is in them, because the ear knows what to listen for (Deutsch 1972, the mysterious melody: names are not enough to make a tune, its places are; but a tune already held can be checked against names alone). Nothing written differs between the first time and the third. Measured in the whole song's master, both seeds: all 24 notes rise and stand over the music in their own band (thrown 7 to 28 dB over, plainly 4 to 26); the same eight notes are delivered up to 2.5 dB apart the first time and the third. Nobody has heard it.

**v23 (chirper-zero): A HISS THAT REPEATS.** Noise has no rhythm. But play a piece of noise over and over and the ear finds things in it that come round, and hears a pattern: each listener's own, and none of it written (Guttman and Julesz, 1963). The row with no hat (bars 145 to 152, 4:36.5, where the drum is on the click) is given a hiss between 3 and 7.5 kHz, in the middle, at one level. For two bars it is a hiss that never repeats (`ht-run`). Then it is a hiss of the same kind, as loud, in the same band, that is only 6 sixteenths long (two bars), then 3 (two bars), then one and a half (a bar): the engine's shift register (`ht-rep`: 32767 steps and then the same steps again), clocked so that it comes round on those lengths. Three sixteenths against the beat's four: this master moves every hiss 8 or 9 dB with the drum, once a beat, the one that never repeats too, so a pattern once a beat would prove nothing. The last bar is left to the rabbit. Measured: alone every stage is as loud as the others to 0.1 dB, its level moving 0.3 dB, each loop 0.9996 or more like itself a loop later to the sample and the same on both seeds; in the whole song's master every stage is -32.8 dBFS in its band, 18 dB under the row, with nothing else in the band between the drums. Nobody has heard it.

**v24 (chirper-zero): THE EAR'S OWN TONE.** The ear is not a straight line: give it two tones close together and it makes others of its own, the loudest at twice the lower less the upper (the cubic difference tone: Goldstein measured it in 1967; Tartini heard its cousin in 1714). It is made in the ear, where the two tones meet: there is nothing at its frequency in the air or in the file. In the first row of "not there" (bars 81 to 88, 2:33.6, before the drum comes in) one plain tone is held on D7 (2349 Hz) and another slides over it from 1.1 to 1.3 times it: up 2.9 semitones in two bars. The third tone goes from 0.9 to 0.7 of the lower: DOWN 4.4 semitones, from 2114 Hz to 1645, and fades as it goes (the ear makes little of it once the two are far apart). Four phrases of two bars: both tones in the middle, the upper rising (the third tone falls); the same with AN EAR EACH, the lower left and the upper right: on headphones no ear has both and the third tone is gone (on loudspeakers it stays); an ear each, the upper falling; both in the middle and falling: the third tone rises into the bar where the drum comes in. Measured: alone, at the third tone's place the file holds 80 to 85 dB less than the tones; in the whole song's master the tones put 53 to 58 dB less than themselves there, and the rest of the music at that place is 27 dB under the tones (36 in the quietest quarter of the row, 15 in the loudest: on the click). Each tone is -26 dBFS in the master, the two 13 dB under the rest of the row; an ear each: 56 to 59 dB between the ears. Nobody has heard it, and no meter can: the third tone is not in the file.

**v25 (chirper-zero): TIME-SHRINKING.** Three strokes mark two empty times. If the second time is the longer one, by up to about a tenth of a second, it is heard SHORTER than it is, nearly as short as the first (Nakajima, ten Hoopen and van der Wilk, 1991); if it is the shorter one, it is heard as it is. In the third row of "round the click" (bars 153 to 159, 4:51.8), where the two sixteenths before every click are empty, every click gets two small clicks before it. For four bars they come 180 ms and 120 ms before the click: 60 ms, then 120 ms: the second time is twice the first and should be heard as nearly even: three strokes in a run, the click on time. For the next three bars the middle one is 60 ms later: 120 ms, then 60 ms: the same three strokes, the same two times the other way round, and it should sound as it is: long, short. ONE small click moves by 60 ms; nothing else changes. Measured in the file: the times are 60.0 and 120.1 ms, then 120.0 and 60.1. The illusion is in the ear and cannot be metered. Nobody has heard it.

**v26 (chirper-zero): THE BASS THAT FALLS FOR EVER.** The song has Shepard's tones as a hat, as stairs and as bells, each a step at a time. This is Risset's continuous one (1969), and it is the song's first bass under 100 Hz that is not the drum. Four plain tones an octave apart (at a bar line 27.5, 55, 110, 220 and 440 Hz: all A, the song's note), all sliding down together, an octave every two bars (3.1 semitones a second), under one fixed curve of loudness: nought at 27.5 and at 440 Hz, greatest at 110. A tone that reaches the bottom has faded to nothing; a new one has come in from nothing at the top. Every tone falls the whole time: 48 semitones in the row by name, four octaves, which from 110 Hz would end at 7 Hz. The curve does not move, the middle of the sound stays within a quarter of a semitone of 110 Hz, and the sound at the end of the row is the sound at its beginning. Bars 113 to 120 (3:35.0), the second row of "held", which had no drum and no bass: under the chord that does not stop, a bass that does not stop falling. One patch (`ht-gliss`), four tracks, a pitch lane and a loudness lane each. Measured alone: every tone within 0.03 dB of its written level along its written line (nine readings in ten), the middle 110.0 Hz within 25 cents, the second turn and the third the same to 0.12 dB in six bands. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v27 (chirper-zero): A SWELL IS HEARD LATE.** A sound's moment is not its start (the perceptual centre: Morton, Marcus and Frankish, 1976). In "the wrong one again" (bars 129 to 136, 4:05.8) the click is alone again and is taken for the beat; the bass has no fundamental; there is no drum. Now a plain low note on the bass's own note (mostly A1, 55 Hz: the fundamental its patch leaves out, and the bass drum's note) begins on every TRUE beat, where the drum falls in other rows, and lasts two sixteenths (240 ms), up to the click. For four bars it swells 40 dB and stops on the click: it should be heard where it arrives, with the click, and the click is the beat. For four bars the same notes at the same instants are struck and die away 40 dB: they should be heard where they begin, and the click becomes the off beat. No note has moved: one shape is the other played backwards (the middle of a note's energy goes from 214 ms after its start to 26 ms), and the beat should turn round on the shape alone. Then the drum comes in (bar 137). In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v28 (chirper-zero): THE ACCENT THAT IS NOT THERE.** Ticks that are all alike are not heard alike: a clock says tick, tick, tick and is heard to say tick, tock (subjective rhythm: Bolton 1894). In the last row of "turned around" (bars 33 to 40, 1:01.4), when the drum has shown where the beat is, a small high tick sounds on every sixteenth. For two bars the ticks are all alike. For two bars (from 1:05.3) every third one is struck twice as hard: threes against the drum's fours. Then (from 1:09.1) they are all alike again for four bars, and whatever accent is heard there, the threes going on for a while or fours with the drum, is the ear's own. The ticks are made alike WHERE THEY ARE HEARD: the master takes up to 9 dB from a tick that falls with the click, so each sixteenth of the bar is given back what it loses there. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v29 (chirper-zero): A TUNE OF HOLES.** The ear gets used to what goes on and hears what is new (auditory enhancement: Viemeister 1980). In the last row of "not there" (bars 97 to 104, 3:04.3) a chord of eight plain tones (E6 G6 A6 C7 D7 E7 G7 A7), every tone as loud as every other, sounds sixteen times, twice a bar. Before each it is heard for a beat with ONE tone left out; the drum falls; then the whole chord, a sixteenth after the beat. In the whole chord the tone that was left out stands out as a note of its own, though it is no louder than the rest. The tones left out are the tune that is in neither ear (bars 121 to 128: A C E A, A E C A), two octaves up: a tune heard in a chord that does not change: the third way the song plays that tune without playing it. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v30 (chirper-zero): THE HURRY THAT IS NOT THERE.** A stair of low notes (E2 to A4, the song's five notes), three to every two beats of the drum and exactly even in time (320.0 ms from note to note, every one), climbs for two bars and comes down for two, twice (bars 89 to 96, 2:49.0, the middle row of "not there"; 48 notes, none of them with a stroke of the drum). A line that rises is judged to be speeding up and one that falls to be slowing down, though its notes are evenly spaced (Boltz 2011): nothing in the time changes, only the pitch. The drum beside it is a ruler: the ear can check. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v31 (chirper-zero): THE LOUDER, THE LOWER.** In the first row of "not there" (bars 81 to 88, 2:33.6: no drum) one plain low note, E2 (82.41 Hz), is played loud and soft by turns, 12 dB apart, a note every two beats for four bars; then it is held for four bars, swelling 12 dB to the middle and sinking back. Its frequency never moves. A plain low note is said to be heard lower the louder it is (Stevens 1935; how much differs from one listener to the next, and it wants the note loud at the ear). If it is, the turns are a tune of two notes and the swell is a bend, and neither is written. Eight bars later the stair of v30 starts on the same note. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v32 (chirper-zero): THE LONGER, THE LOUDER.** In bars 25 to 32 (0:46.1) a pip follows every beat by a third of a beat (160 ms, where nothing else in the row strikes): always the same note (B5, 988 Hz) at the same height. Only its length changes, beat by beat: 5, 10, 20, 40, 80, 40, 20, 10 ms: four doublings and back, an arch every two bars. Under about a fifth of a second the ear adds a sound up over its length, so the same height twice as long is heard louder: a swell and a fall with no level moved. Alone all 32 tops are within 0.1 dB; in the whole song's master within 1.0 dB (see the note). Nobody has heard it.

**v33 (chirper-zero): FAR IN PITCH, FAR IN TIME.** In bars 145 to 151 (4:36.5; the drum, the bass and the tick are all on the half beat here) three pips a bar fall on beats 1, 2 and 3: each midway between two strokes of the drum, 480 ms apart: A4, a middle one, A5. The middle pip is in the middle in time. Bar by bar it is B4 (two semitones from the first) or G5 (two from the last). The ear is said to hear a sound nearer in time to the one it is nearer in pitch (the kappa effect): the B4 early, the G5 late: a limp that changes feet and is not there. Then in bars 149 and 150 it is truly there: the B4 30 ms early, the G5 30 ms late: to be compared. Bar 151 is even again. 21 pips, all at one height. Alone the middle pip is 0.0 ms off the middle; in the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v34 (chirper-zero): THE SAME SPACING, ANOTHER NOTE.** In bars 89 to 94 (2:49.0; the row of the hurry that is not there) a pip a third of a beat after every beat: three plain tones always 196 Hz apart (the note G3 apart, as its 5th, 6th and 7th overtones are: 980, 1176, 1372 Hz). Beat by beat all three are moved up together by an eighth of that distance (24.5 Hz), and after eight steps they begin again. The distance never changes, so the three beat 196 times a second all the while. Yet the note the ear makes of three such tones is said to be the middle one over the nearest whole number (the pitch shift of the residue): up a third of a semitone a step (0, +36, +71, +105 cents), down two and a third semitones at the fifth step (-128), and up again (-95, -63, -31) to where it began: so where every tone steps down the note steps up, and where the tones step up the note falls. Three turns. In bars 95 and 96 the tune it is said to be is played by a plain tone, an octave up, to be compared. Nobody has heard it.

**v35 (chirper-zero): THE GAP GOES TO THE OTHER ONE.** In bars 137, 139, 141 and 143 (from 4:21.1) a long plain tone glides slowly up (980 to 1560 Hz in three beats) and a short one glides quickly down across it (1470 to 1040 Hz in three quarters of a beat); they cross at 1236 Hz just after beat 2 of the bar. In bars 137 and 141 the LONG glide is broken for 90 ms where they cross and the short one is whole. This is the gap transfer (Nakajima and others): it is said to be heard the other way round, the long glide running on unbroken and the hole in the short one. In bars 139 and 143 it is made the way it is said to sound (the short one broken, the long one whole), so the two can be compared: they are said to sound alike. Nobody has heard it here.

**v36 (chirper-zero): AFTER THE FALL, A NOTE THAT DOES NOT MOVE.** For eight bars (113 to 120) the bass has fallen without a break. On the bar line where its row ends (bar 121, 3:50.4) one plain note, E4, is held for three beats and does not move at all. After a long fall a steady note is said to seem to rise for a moment: the waterfall illusion, in sound (Shu, Swindale and Cynader, 1993). The same note is held again in bars 123 and 125, 3.84 and 7.68 s later, as the after-effect wears off: the same note, as long and as loud. The song's own fall is the cause; nothing of it is changed. To keep the master's dents out of the note, its gain follows the master's reading turned over. Nobody has heard it.

**v37 (chirper-zero): THE WIDER, THE LOUDER.** In bars 59 to 64 (1:51.4) two pips that read the same on a meter, one on beat 1.75 and one on beat 3.75 of every bar. One is a single plain tone (E5). The other is four plain tones (A4 E5 A5 E6), each a quarter of its power: the same power, spread over an octave and a half. The ear does not add power; it adds loudness band by band, so the spread pip is said to sound the louder (Zwicker, Flottorp and Stevens, 1957).
