It is Cowell's and Theremin's rhythmicon (1931): the same ratios as pitch and as rhythm; phase is what makes them one note or eight. Measured in the whole song's master, both seeds: all 214 strokes rise in their own band; 154 and 152 are level with the music there or over (the first two harmonics are in the bass's band and the eighth is under the music on the beat); alone, the bar line is 0.984 like eight terms of a saw. Nobody has heard it.

**v23 (chirper-zero): THE CLICK COMES APART.** A click is every frequency starting at once. An all-pass filter lets every frequency through at the level it came in at and changes only its PHASE: near the filter's own frequency the sound is held back a little. A chain of them, their frequencies spread evenly in pitch from 400 Hz to 8 kHz, holds each frequency back by about (filters) / (f ln 20): the low ones longest. So a click goes in and a falling chirp comes out: the same spectrum, to a tenth of a dB, and another sound: its harmonics are out of step. In the sixth row of "slips" (bars 65 to 72, 2:01.9) A's click on sixteenths 6 and 14 is given a second copy through such a chain, on A's side: 32 filters for two bars, then 64, 128, 256: the click's third harmonic comes 13, 26, 51 and then 102 ms after its twenty-fourth. Measured in the whole song's master on both seeds: 12, 25, 50 and 97 ms; the copy is 21, 29, 48 and 89 ms long; and after A's own click is over the mix between 500 and 1500 Hz is 0, 5, 7 and 8 dB over what it was: the first two bars are a click on a click. The master delivers the copy 7.6 dB louder through 256 filters than through 32, the same energy with a lower peak. A held note 120 dB down (`os-keep`) keeps each chain running after the click's voice has ended: without it the engine stops the chain and the low harmonics never come out. Nobody has heard it.

**v24 (chirper-zero): THE WHISTLE IS PUT IN STEP.** The other half of v23, through the same chains. If a chain of all-pass filters holds every frequency back by its own time, then a sound in which every frequency is sounded that much EARLY comes out with all of them together: a click. That sound is a rising whistle, the low frequencies first. It is pulse compression: how a radar turns a long weak chirp back into one sharp pulse. In the last row of "drifts" (bars 129 to 136, 4:03.8) a whistle from 588 Hz to 6 kHz goes into the chain of 256 filters (107 ms long), then 128 (53 ms), 64 (27 ms), 32 (13 ms), two bars each, each 3 dB up on the last: the same energy goes in every time and the same click comes out, on A's side, half a sixteenth after A's clicks on 6 and 14, where nothing in the song strikes: a click where only a whistle was written. Measured alone: what comes out is 1.0 to 1.4 ms long (A's click is 9.9), with A's click's energy to 0.3 dB and its peak to 0.8 out of every chain, its peak 1.9 ms after the written instant. The whistle is also heard going in, quietly, on B's side, ending as the click sounds. In the whole song's master the click stands 9 to 14 dB over everything else in its ear in its 6 ms: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v25 (chirper-zero): A NOTE OUT OF STEP WITH ITSELF.** The song puts one click out of step with another. Here one plain low tone (the bass's G2) is put out of step with ITSELF: an unheard second wave pushes its phase a quarter of a turn ahead and holds it a quarter of a turn back, over and over. That is all phase modulation is (Chowning, 1973: a whole family of synthesizers makes its sounds this way). How FAR the phase is pushed never changes; only how OFTEN, a bar each (bars 33 to 40, 1:01.0, under the vernier, the row before the bass comes in). Once a beat: the pitch wobbles, 94.7 to 101.3 Hz. Twice, four, eight times a beat: the wobble widens (to 72 to 124 Hz) and blurs. Sixteen times a beat (33.6 Hz): it can no longer be followed, and the one tone is heard as SEVERAL: itself, and pairs of side tones 33.6 Hz apart, whose sizes are Bessel's functions of the push: the first pair (64 and 132 Hz) is 1.6 dB LOUDER than the note. Then as often as the drum's note (a tone on G1's harmonics), as the note itself (its octave loudest, itself 9 dB under), as its octave (the odd harmonics: hollow). Three instruments and a vibrato from one sine and one number. Measured alone: every tone within 0.05 dB of Bessel's arithmetic and the wobble's range within 0.5 Hz at the slow rates. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v26 (chirper-zero): THE SWARM HUMS.** A rhythm and a pitch are one thing at two speeds. In the first break (bars 57 to 64, 1:46.7) twelve clicks come apart and find each other (v18). Now each of them holds a low tone that makes 31 waves for every beat of its OWN tempo: 65.1 Hz at the song's tempo (8 cents under C2; the slowest 62.5 Hz, the fastest 67.7) and its octave 8 dB under, in the bottom that the resting bass drum leaves empty. Twelve clicks a little out of step are twelve tones a little out of tune: a rumble that gathers into a soft thump whenever the twelve are one tone again, which while nobody listens is every 4.435 beats (31 times sooner than the clicks would be): a drum nobody strikes, slipping against the bar. The tones come up over the eight beats for which the swarm is deaf, so the break still begins with the bottom gone, and the first thump that stands out is the one just after beat 9. As the swarm finds the beat the thumps run into one held note (bars 62 to 64), and the drum takes its place at bar 65. The note only holds once the clicks are within a millisecond or two of each other (a quarter of its wave is 3.8 ms): in bar 61 the clicks are within 29 ms of the beat and the hum is still gathering (its tones 63.66 to 66.54 Hz); from bar 62 (0.11 ms; 65.08 to 65.12 Hz) it is one note. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v27 (chirper-zero): AN OCTAVE BY PHASE ALONE.** The ear is said to be deaf to phase. It is, while it can hear a note's harmonics one by one; above about the tenth they are too close together for that, and the ear reads the pitch from how often the wave pulses (Patterson 1987; Shackleton and Carlyon 1994). In the first row of home (bars 89 to 96, 2:47.6), where the two clicks are in step at last, a note sounds on every off beat with the hat: harmonics 13 to 26 of the bass's G (1274 to 2548 Hz) and nothing under them. For two bars every harmonic starts on its sine: the wave pulses once a period (10.2 ms) and the note is a low buzz on G. From bar 91 every second note has its EVEN harmonics a quarter turn on: the wave pulses twice a period (5.1 ms) and the note should be heard an octave up: the off-beat octaves of a house bass, made of one note. The two notes have the same harmonics at the same strengths: no meter of spectrum can tell them apart. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v28 (chirper-zero): TWO SAWS OUT OF STEP.** A pulse wave is two sawtooths: one the right way up, one upside down and a little late. In step they are nothing at all; the later the second, the wider the pulse; half a wave late it is a square, every even harmonic gone and every odd one doubled. In the second row of "home" (bars 97 to 104, 3:02.9) a sawtooth on C3 is held, a bar at a time. In the second bar the same sawtooth is added to it upside down, and the sound goes: one and one is nothing. Then the second saw is slid late, 1/16, 1/4 and 1/2 of a wave, a bar each: a thin reed opens into a hollow square; and back, 1/4, 1/16, and in the last bar in step again: home, and nothing. No note, level or harmonic of either saw changes: only the phase between them. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v29 (chirper-zero): IN PHASE, OUT OF PHASE.** A phrase of eight notes (G4 Bb4 D5 C5 Bb4 G4 F4 G4, a note a beat, each a sixteenth before the beat), two bars long, is played four times in the row of the gears (bars 73 to 80, 2:17.1), the same in each ear every time. The second and fourth times the right ear's wave is upside down. In phase the phrase is in the sum of the two sides: in the middle, with the drum. Out of phase it is in their difference, where this song has 13 dB less: it leaves the middle and stands clearer of the drum, though neither ear's level has changed (the ear can take the difference of its two sides: Hirsh 1948). On one speaker the second and fourth times are not there: the two sides add up to nothing; the first and third are the phrase for one speaker. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v30 (chirper-zero): THE BEAT THAT IS ONLY IN THE HEAD.** In the row of the sung clicks (bars 49 to 56, 1:31.4) a low note is held in each ear: 249 waves to the bar in the left (130.725 Hz: C3 less 1.2 cents) and 250, 250, 251, 251, 253, 253, 257, 257 in the right, bar by bar. Neither ear has a beat: the beat is between them. The difference in phase between the ears goes round once for every wave more: once a bar for two bars, then twice, four times, eight; in phase at every bar line. Written to be heard on headphones as a note that crosses the head, slowly and then faster until it flutters; on one speaker it is a plain beat, the note gone at every half turn. A meter can read the phase and the levels (see the note); it cannot say that anything moves. Nobody has heard it.

**v31 (chirper-zero): LOUDNESS BY LATENESS.** After the drum has gone (bars 145 to 152, 4:34.3) two low tones sound: the same note (28 waves to the beat, 58.8 Hz), the same level, and neither ever changes. One is held for the eight bars. The other begins again on every beat: in step, a quarter of a wave late (4.25 ms), a third (5.67 ms), a half (8.50 ms). Together they are twice the cosine of half the turn between them: +6.0 dB, +3.0 dB, as loud as one, nothing. A low note that steps down and vanishes in every bar, and no fader moves. Alone: +6.02, +3.03, 0.00 dB and 35 to 58 dB under one tone. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v32 (chirper-zero): IN STEP, A CLICK; OUT OF STEP, A HUM.** In bars 17 to 24 (0:30.5: the two clicks and the drum, nothing else) sixteen tones of one low note (the 5th to the 20th of G1, the drum's note: 245 to 980 Hz), all at one level, are struck on the off beats in pairs. In one stab of each pair every tone begins together: they are in step once every wave of the note (every 20.4 ms) and tick there, 49 times a second. In the other each tone begins a different part of its own wave late (the jth of 16 by j (j - 1) / 32: Schroeder's rule, the flattest wave those tones can make): the same tones at the same levels never meet, and there is no tick. Nothing differs but where each wave begins: a click is every note in step. The order is turned round every other pair, so each kind falls in both halves of the bar; 14 of each. Alone the two have the same tones to 0.0 dB and their highest samples are 8.6 dB apart. In the whole song's master: see the note. Nobody has heard it.

**v33 (chirper-zero): FIVE OUT OF STEP MAKE A STAIR WITHOUT END.** In bars 25 to 32 (0:45.7; the two clicks, the drum and the vernier) the drum's note (G1, 49 Hz) is held with all its overtones and heard through five narrow windows an octave apart (98 Hz to 3.1 kHz), all climbing an octave every four bars: the engine's barber's pole. Each window is a fifth of the way round behind the next, comes in at the bottom and goes out at the top. So the overtones are picked out one after another, always upward: in the window that starts on G3 the 4th, 5th, 6th and 7th, one a bar; in the one above it the 8th to the 15th, two a bar; above that four a bar. After four bars every window stands where the next one stood and the same chord is there again: two turns, and it has gone nowhere. It comes in slowly under the first slip and goes out in bar 32. Nobody has heard it.

**v34 (chirper-zero): ONE AND ONE MAKE TWO, ONE, OR NOTHING.** In bars 113 to 120 (3:33.3) a plain tone (520.8 Hz: C5 a hair flat, 62 whole waves to the sixteenth) is alone and steady for two bars. From bar 115 a second tone of the same pitch and the same loudness sounds with it all the time, and all that changes is how far it is out of step. In step, the two make one tone 6 dB higher; a third of a turn out, one tone exactly as loud as either alone; half a turn out, nothing at all. So the rhythm (I..T..I..T..I.T. and the next bar T..I..T..I..T.I.: 18 strokes in step, 18 a third out, 36 silences) is the second tone's phase and nothing else: for 15 of the 24 beats both tones are sounding and the sum is digital silence. Nobody has heard it.

**v35 (chirper-zero): A QUARTER OF A TURN AND THE THROB IS GONE.** In bars 137 to 144 (4:19.0) three plain tones sound together: a middle one (Bb5) and one 4.2 Hz to either side of it, each 10.5 dB under it. For two bars the middle one is in step with the sides, and the three are one tone that swells and sinks twice a beat (12 dB). For the next two bars the middle one is turned a quarter of a turn and nothing else: the same three tones, as loud as they were, and hardly a throb (1.3 dB). Then both again. v25 pushed one tone's phase to make side tones; here the side tones are given and only the middle tone's phase says whether the three throb. To keep the master's own pumping out of it, both tracks' gain follows the master's pattern turned over. Nobody has heard it.

**v36 (chirper-zero): THE WAVE SLIPS UNDER ITS OWN BEAT.** In bars 43 to 48 (1:20.0, in "slips", where the two clicks slip against each other) a short note in the middle, 24 of them. A note is a beat (here G3's, 196 a second: the click's own wave) with a wave inside it (plain tones from 4 to 11 times 196 Hz, under one window). At each step EVERY tone is moved up by the same 24.5 Hz. The beat inside the note does not change at all; the wave under it is an eighth of a turn further on at every beat. The note is said to follow the wave and not the beat: to go a little sharp, and sour, with each step (de Boer, 1956; Schouten, Ritsma and Cardozo, 1962). At the half turn the wave is as like itself a little sooner as a little later: the note is in two minds. Then it comes home from below, and after eight steps every tone stands where its neighbour stood: it is the first note again, exactly. Nobody has heard it.

**v37 (chirper-zero): OUT OF STEP, AND THE EAR CANNOT TELL.** In bars 9 to 16 (0:15.2), while the two clicks go out of step and are heard to, a short low note of two plain tones an octave apart (G3 and G4), twice a bar. The first of each pair is always the same. In the second the upper tone is an eighth of a turn further out of step with the lower one, bar by bar, once round in the row. On paper the wave changes shape; the two tones do not change at all, and the ear, which takes a low note apart into its tones, is said not to hear which way they stand to each other (Ohm, 1843; Helmholtz, 1863). The song's other parts are all the places where being out of step is heard; this is the place where it is not. Also from v37 the throb's last note (bars 143 and 144) goes out over two bars, not one. Nobody has heard it.

## t8 Wind Down (chirper-one)

**The rule.** Nothing in the closer is new. One thing is taken from each of the other seven songs, gathered a section at a time on one grid (124) in one key (G minor), held, and let go one at a time, last in, first out, until the first thing on the record, a click off the beat, is alone again. Then the spring runs down. In the generator: `GATHER` (the order), `COMES`, `GOES`, `STEP`, `HOLD`, `LEAVE`, `WIND`.

**What is quoted, and how** (each rebuilt from its song's entry here under a `wd-` name; nothing of another composer's is edited):
- from 1, Give or Take: the grid, a bass drum on the four and one click on steps 2, 6, 10, 14. The click is Ring True's wooden bar on G6, stopped after 5 ms.
- from 7, Out of Step: the half-bar pattern `x x . x . x x .` on a click of five cycles of a saw on G3 (one click only: no second copy, no slip).
- from 5, Hearing Things: the bass with no fundamental (harmonics two to six), its four-bar line a tone down (G G G B flat / G G G G / G G G C / G G D F), on the off-beats. With the click it is that song's off-beat pair: for the first eight bars the click is alone, and then the drum comes on the beat (that song's turn-around; whether it turns anyone here is not tested).
- from 4, Ring True: the wood's tune (D5 F5 G5 F5 / D5 C5 B flat 4 C5 on sixteenths 3 7 11 14 / 19 23 27 29), stopped while things gather and let go over sixteen bars once all are in.
- from 2, Least Common: the meeting tune. Cycles of 3, 5 and 7 sixteenths counted from the top of the song, nobody playing them: a ping where two meet (D5, B flat 4, F4), the chord where three do.
- from 3, Standing Room: standing room itself, walls 3 and 5 sixteenths away round the click, a door on each.
- from 6, Small Change: a seed on B flat every two bars and one pair of coins, 3 and 5, a fifth up and a fourth down. Walls of 3 and 5 and coins of 3 and 5 are the same two numbers: heard together here, one keeps its pitch and one does not.

**The spring.** Over the last eight bars the tempo falls in a straight line from 124 to 50: the gap between ticks goes 493, 503, 513 ... 1054, 1099, 1147 ms. Each bar's ticks are let ring longer than the last bar's, and the final stroke, let go altogether and an octave down (G5), lands on the one, where that click has never been.

**v1 (chirper-one, the skeleton): WIND DOWN.** 200 bars, 6:35 to the last stroke. The grid (the drum at bar 9); the pattern 17; the bass 33; the cell 49; the meetings 65; the room 81; the change 97; all gathered 113, the cell let go from 129; then they go, eight bars apart, from 145: the change, the room, the meetings, the cell, the bass, the pattern; the drum goes at 193 and the spring runs down. Measured: the bass alone has 25 dB less at its notes (40-80 Hz) than at their second harmonics; the click alone, three sixteenths after a stroke, reads -63 dB on the left with the door shut and -45 with it open (the stroke itself -41); the lone click peaks at -1.3 dBFS in the finished render. 82.81 on both seeds: the scorer finds 6 sections; the mix is 14 dB over the album's envelope at 1.6 kHz (the click's own note) and 7 to 10 under from 7.9 kHz up. It quotes plainly: none of the seven does here what it does in its own song (no hand on a deck, no slip, no ring but the cell's and the click's, no Risset hat). Not heard by its composer.

**v2 (chirper-one): WHAT THE SONGS LEARNT SINCE, AND THE ESCAPEMENT.** The seven grew after v1 quoted them, and chirper-zero offered three of his (06:04). Six switches (`--without-catch`, `-comp`, `-hat`, `-learnt`, `-stop`, `-tuck`); all six off is v1 byte for byte. (1) THE CATCH, the album's name: in the last bar before each thing comes (bars 16, 32, 48, 64, 80, 96, and 112 before all are gathered) the wheel is caught: everything stops but the click, and on the one it is let go with the new thing in it. Each caught bar is 12 to 15 dB under the bars round it. Nothing is caught on the way out. (2) from 7, Out of Step v2: THE COMPLEMENT `. . x . x . . x`, a small bright click in the pattern's three holes. It fits only when everything is home, so it plays through the hold (bars 113 to 144) and no longer: for those bars every sixteenth is struck. (3) from 5, Hearing Things: ITS HAT, BACKWARDS. From the bar the first thing leaves (145) to the bar the drum goes (193), six rows of a hat whose four layers each halve their speed across eight bars and begin the next row where they began the last: the song is winding down for a minute and a half before the tempo moves. (4) from 3, Standing Room v3: when the room goes (bar 153) what is left of it is in the bass, which plays the room's figure D F C D F C on sixteenths 2 5 7 10 13 15 for the eight bars after the door shuts, and is then itself again. (5) from 1, Give or Take v3: THE STOP. The spring loses speed in a straight line against the clock, not against the beat: speed `sqrt(1 - 2x / W)` after x beats, W = 66.67, so a fifth of it is left on the one (v1 kept two fifths). The gap between ticks goes 491, 499, 507 ... 1344, 1534, 1840 ms, and the one comes 1114 ms after the last of them, at 6:37.4 (the render: 1346, 1538, 1848; 6:37.44). 25.8 s of running down (v1: 23.6). The click keeps its pitch: a clock's tick does not sink. (6) The click is 3 dB lower while anything else plays. Not taken yet: the stop's sinking pitch, the pattern's slip. Not heard by its composer.

**v3 (chirper-one): EACH LEAVES AS ITSELF, AND THE NOTE SINKS.** Six switches (`--without-spent`, `-held`, `-taken`, `-real`, `-slip`, `-sink`; all six off is v2 byte for byte). In v2 a thing went by being absent from the next bar. Now each goes by its own song's idea. (1) SPENT, Small Change: in its last four bars (141 to 144) a seed every bar, and on beats one and three of the last. (2) HELD, Least Common: on the bar the meetings leave (161) their chord is held a bar and dies over two: the only held sound in the song. (3) TAKEN, Ring True: over its last eight bars the cell's ring goes from 0.64 s to 0.03 s, so it leaves as the click it came as. (4) REAL, Hearing Things: for its last four bars (173 to 176) the bass with no fundamental is given it, a sine at the notes the ear had been supplying. (5) SLIP, Out of Step: in its last eight bars (177 to 184) the pattern starts a sixteenth later each bar and is in step again in the bar it leaves. (6) SINK, Give or Take's stop, its other half (chirper-zero's A RECORD SWITCHED OFF): as the spring runs down each tick's note sinks with the speed, 1570 Hz down to 392 over 33 strokes; a quarter of the speed is left on the one, so the last stroke is G4, two octaves down and reached, not jumped to. 97.74 and 97.69 on two seeds against v2's 98.22 under the album's new seven cuts: KEPT UNDER ITS SCORE. All of the half point is SINK (without it 98.26): the drum's entry at bar 9, unchanged in sound, reads 0.15 where it read 0.21 once the ending is no longer a copy of the opening. Not heard by its composer.

**v4 (chirper-one): THE BODY OF THE SOUND.** v3 stood 10 and 11 dB over the album's envelope from 1.4 to 1.6 kHz and 11 under at 71 Hz. Three switches (`--without-mid`, `-body`, `-open`; all three off is v3 byte for byte). (1) MID: the click is turned from its note towards its click, 6 dB off round G6 and 3 dB on round 11 kHz, and the saw's click loses 5 dB round 1.6 kHz (read by parts, those two are two fifths of 1 to 2 kHz while the meetings are still in, 86% in the cell's last bars and all of it after). The two bands now stand 6.4 and 6.7 over, and 1.2, 1.9 and 2.2 kHz are inside the envelope. As the spring runs down the sinking note leaves the cut behind, so the note comes out as it falls. (2) BODY: the drum has 5 dB round 72 Hz; 71 Hz moved 1.3 dB and is still 9.4 under: the bass's missing fundamentals (49 to 87 Hz) are that band, so the hole is the illusion's own. (3) OPEN: the meetings' last chord opens as it dies (new patch `wd-open`): shut at 600 Hz while it is held, its low-pass opens two octaves over the first two and a half seconds of a six-second going. The chord alone gets brighter (the centre of its spectrum 349 Hz to 706) while it falls 15 dB, and stands 5 to 13 dB under the mix for two and a half bars where it was 17 under within a bar of being let go. PROVED on v3 by solo, as its plan asked: every leaving is in the mix (the spending seed 13.4 dB under it, the held chord 7.9, the cell taken 8.0, the bass's fundamental 11.4 and 55% of everything under 60 Hz, the slipping pattern 10.3). 99.46 on both seeds (v3 97.74): spectrum 85.6 to 95.4. Not heard by its composer.

**v5 (chirper-one): TWO MORE LEAVINGS.** In v3 five of the seven left as themselves; the room only had its door shut, and the cell was damped and nothing else. Three switches (`--without-sing`, `-nudge`, `-ease`; all three off is v4 byte for byte). (1) SING, from Standing Room v6: in the room's last four bars (149 to 152) two more walls come in, so near they cannot be counted: a second pair of buses, each returning the click and itself, one after 10.2 ms and one after 6.8. That is not an echo, it is 98 Hz and 147: the room, as it goes, is two strings the click plucks, G on the left and the D above it on the right. By solo 88% and 83% of each wall's power is on the harmonics of its note, and they stand 16.4, 12.8, 9.6 and 6.0 dB under the mix above 300 Hz bar by bar (read on the published build), then the door shuts. (2) NUDGE, Give or Take's hand: as the cell is taken back (bars 161 to 168) it is dragged late by up to a sixteenth (120 ms in bar 164) and let catch up, and its notes go with its speed (up to 37 cents flat by solo). (3) EASE: the cell let go 2 dB lower. 99.45 on both seeds (v4 99.46): the scorer hears none of it, and it is kept on judgment. Both walls sing between 1 and 2 kHz only, because only the click feeds them. Not heard by its composer.

**v6 (chirper-one): WHAT THEY LEARNT THIS AFTERNOON.** Three of the seven grew again after v5 quoted them, and the closer takes the new thing from each. Three switches on (`--without-knock`, `-paid`, `-strings`; all off is v5 byte for byte) and one off (`--with-early`). (1) KNOCK, from Least Common v6: there the drum answers each click that lands on it. Here nobody plays the cycles, only their meetings sound, so the drum answers each MEETING that lands on it, with the notes of the two cycles that met (G2 for the three, B flat 2 for the five, D3 for the seven; new patch `wd-knock`): two low notes every 15, 21 and 35 beats and the whole triad every 105, 46 times from bar 68 to bar 158. (2) PAID, from Small Change v5: the change's very last seed (bar 144, beat three) is paid in coins of 3 and 5 of a unit that is a pitch, B flat 3's period: its change is its own note, a B flat 4 held about a bar as the change leaves. (3) STRINGS, from Ring True v6: the click is Ring True's wooden bar, and beside it lie that song's five strings nobody plays (F2, G2, B flat 2, C3, D3), stopped for the whole record. The last stroke, on the one, is G4, the fourth harmonic of the G string; as it is struck the strings are let go, and when the stroke has stopped the string goes on at that note by itself: the last sound on the record is a string nobody played (after the last stroke the finished song reads -36 -49 -61 -74 -87 -99 dBFS by second where v5 read -64 -81 -97 and silence; the string alone is G4 at 392.6 Hz, 3 cents sharp, with everything else 28 dB or more under it). Written and off (`--with-early`): the strings let go from the top of the run-down, answering the ticks as they slow and sink; it stood 10 to 15 dB under the lone click and the scorer took 0.4 for it (by its count at bar 9, where nothing changed). 99.33 and 99.26 on two seeds (v5 99.45), kept on judgment: the knock and the note cost nothing; the string costs 0.1 at the scorer's highest cut, by its count at bar 9, where nothing changed. Not heard by its composer.

**v7 (chirper-one): RING TRUE.** Two of the seven grew again after v6, and the closer takes the new thing from each. Three switches (`--without-true`, `-turn`, `-tame`; all off is v6 byte for byte). (1) TRUE, from Ring True v7 (the strings let go while the wood closes): the cell is Ring True's wooden bar, so when the room goes (bar 153) the five strings nobody plays are let go beside it and are what is left round it, on the cell's own notes (D5 strongest, then C5, F5, G5; 12 dB under the mix). At bar 161 the hand takes the cell and drags it late, and a thing dragged late is flat: a string answers a note only if it is one of its own, so the strings fall away faster than the cell does. By difference they stand 8 dB under the cell while it is true, 17 to 18 under at its flattest (42 cents, bars 162 and 163), and 10 to 14 as it comes back in tune: a dip, not a silence. They ring while it rings true. Stopped when the cell goes; the last stroke still lands on the G string. (2) TURN, from Least Common v7: each time all three cycles meet on the drum (every 105 beats: bars 79, 106, 132, 158) the triad of the drum's answers turns over: G2 B flat 2 D3, then B flat 2 D3 G3, then D3 G3 B flat 3, and round. (3) TAME, from Least Common v7 as well: the pings' commonest note (D5) 3 dB down, since the strings sing where the song was already thickest. 99.34 and 99.26 on two seeds (v6 99.33 and 99.26): the scorer hears none of it. Not heard by its composer.

**v8 (chirper-one): WHAT IS LEFT BOUNCING.** Two of the seven grew once more after v7, and the closer takes the new thing from each. Two switches (`--without-drop`, `-part`; both off is v7 byte for byte). (1) DROP, from Ring True v8 (the stick is let go too): the hand has taken the cell back (bars 161 to 168); on the cell's last stroke it lets go of the stick, and of the bar. The stroke rings as nothing of the cell's has for eight bars, and bounces: eight bounces, each gap three quarters of the one before and each 2.5 dB softer, the ninth due on beat two of bar 169, the bar the cell is not in. The cell goes and its stick is still bouncing: something coming to rest by itself, which is the song. By solo it stands 5 dB under the mix for those 850 ms and is gone after them. (2) PART, from Least Common v8 (the second half remembers the first: an echo keeps the old cycle): here nobody plays the cycles at all and only their meetings are heard, as pings. Now each meeting is heard to part: after a ping the two cycles that met click once more, the three's 3 sixteenths on (left), the five's 5 on (right), the seven's 7 on (middle), where Least Common stands them. The clicks are the ping's own echoes with the note taken out (a high-pass at 1.2 kHz leaves a vibraphone bar's second mode, two octaves up, and its click); the pings go by three tracks, one a pair, so that each cycle's bus hears only its own meetings. 342 clicks nobody plays, 10 to 12 dB under the pings. 99.29 and 99.22 on two seeds (v7 99.34 and 99.26): kept under its score; the 0.04 is the clicks from 1 to 5 kHz, where the song was over the album's envelope already. Not heard by its composer.

**v9 (chirper-one): WITH INTEREST.** Three switches (`--without-answer`, `-interest`, `-chest`; all three off is v8 byte for byte). (1) ANSWER: the hand drops the stick on the cell's last stroke (v8), and the strings nobody plays were shut in that same half beat: the one stroke of the cell's that is let go altogether was the one they did not hear. Now they hear it and its bounces and are held open through the bar after the cell has gone (bar 169), then let down over the next. The cell's last note is answered by the strings that have its note in them (C3's fourth partial and F2's sixth are C5): what is left of the cell when it is not there. (2) INTEREST, Small Change's lesson of v7 (a purse that gives back more than it is given builds, and shut it is empty at once): the change's last seed (bar 144, beat 3) went to the smallest purse and was a held B flat that ran out. Now the purse pays interest: the note swells for the two beats that are left and is shut on the one of bar 145, where the change goes. A clip inside the loop is the purse's size, so the swell cannot run away. (3) CHEST: nothing in this song sounds between 60 and 85 Hz (the drum is on G1 with its 47 Hz taken down, the bass has no fundamental: that is its illusion); a narrow bell of 4 dB at 71 Hz on the drum, aimed at the album's envelope and said so. 99.59 on both seeds (v8 99.29 and 99.22): the gain is the scorer's: its spectrum term for the bell, which was aimed at it, and one more boundary at its highest cut. Not heard by its composer.

**v10 (chirper-one): WHAT THE SONGS LEARNT TONIGHT.** Small Change (v9) and Least Common (v10) each learnt something after the closer last quoted them; it takes one thing from each and nothing of its own. Three switches (`--without-exchange`, `-rate`, `-given`; all three off is v9 byte for byte). (1) EXCHANGE, from Small Change v9: while the change spends what is left (bars 141 to 144, a seed a bar) the seeds are also paid by a second pair of coins, 3 and 5, of a unit two thirds of a sixteenth: the same fifths up on the left and fourths down on the right, three against two with the first pair. A hand opens it for those four bars and lets it down over the two after. (2) RATE, from the same: through the hold the complement has filled the pattern's holes so that every sixteenth is struck; in its last four bars the complement's click also counts what the other currency can pay between the sixteenths (5, 8, 10 and 11 of its units after each seed). The full grid is given its triplet just before it empties. (3) GIVEN, from Least Common v10: the drum rests where the three and the five meet on it and the beat is the knock's: bar 147 beat two, 151 beat one, 154 beat four, fifteen beats apart, and the fourth fifteen is where all three meet on the drum (bar 158), with the drum under it. Three rests count in the last meeting. 99.58 and 99.57 on two seeds (v9 re-read 99.59 and 99.59): kept 0.016 under, the scorer's spectrum term. Not heard by its composer.

**v11 (chirper-one): WHAT THE SONGS LEARNT AFTER MIDNIGHT.** Ring True (v11) and Least Common (v11) each learnt something in the last hour; the closer takes two things from the one and one from the other. Three switches (`--without-grip`, `-choke`, `-lead`; all three off is v10 byte for byte). (1) GRIP, from Ring True v11: the wood has a hand. The cell IS that bar, and the hand takes it back over bars 161 to 168: bar by bar its tune goes up two octaves as it is stopped (the note 12 dB down by the last bar, its second mode at the note's old level), and on its last stroke the hand lets go of the stick and of the bar: that one stroke has its note again and bounces on it. A new patch name, wd-grip; where Ring True's crossfade dipped 6 dB in the middle, here each layer has its own level on the stroke and the two are an equal-power pair: the cell alone reads the same to 0.1 dB bar by bar with and without. (2) CHOKE, from Ring True v11: the drum goes at bar 193; in its last two bars the beater is held against it and its note rises, B flat then C for G. (3) LEAD, from Least Common v11: the bass comes back before the drum. In each caught bar where the bass is already in (48, 64, 80, 96, 112) its fundamental holds D through the bar's last beat into the one. First built with two beats held: the scorer's sections fell from 10 to 8 at its highest cut and came back with the lead out: half a caught bar with the bass's band in it is half a catch (Least Common's finding at half the size). 99.67 and 99.67 on two seeds (v10 re-read 99.58 and 99.57). Not heard by its composer.

**v12 (chirper-one): THE CATCH SAYS WHO IS COMING.** Two switches (`--without-tell`, `-whole`; both off is v11 byte for byte). (1) TELL: seven times the escapement catches (a bar with only the click) and after it something new; all seven were the same bar. Now the newcomer plays whatever of its OWN figure falls in the caught bar's last beat, as if it had come a beat early, 3 dB under its stroke: bar 16 the pattern's last two strokes; bar 32 the bass's last note of four bars (F2); bar 48 the last stroke of the cell's tune (C5) before it begins on D5; bar 64 the meeting of three and five on beat 4 (D5), heard to part over the bar line; bar 80 nothing (the room's door is already opening); bar 96 one seed three sixteenths before the one, so that its first coin falls ON the one with the first seed proper; bar 112 the complement's two holes in that beat. (2) WHOLE: v11's lead was the bass's fundamental with none of its harmonics, in a song whose bass is harmonics with no fundamental. Now, ONCE, the bass's own voice plays the same D2 under it (6 dB under its stroke): the first time the bass comes back before the drum (bar 48) it is whole for that beat, so that the four bare fundamentals after it are known for the bass. First built in all five catches: the scorer's sections fell from 9 to 5 at its highest cut and 1.9 came off (a catch whose last beat has the bass's voice in it is three quarters of a catch), so it is shown once. 99.51 and 99.44 on two seeds (v11 re-read 99.67 and 99.67): 0.2 UNDER, kept: the scorer loses one boundary of nine at its highest cut to the six pickups. Not heard by its composer.

**v13 (chirper-one): THE BAR AFTER SAYS WHO HAS LEFT.** Two switches (`--without-left`, `-ajar`; both off is v12 byte for byte). (1) LEFT: v12's rule the other way round. A catch says who is coming; now the bar after a thing has gone says who has left: the leaver plays whatever of its own figure falls in that bar's first beat, 3 dB under, and nothing after. Four of the seven leave with a gesture of their own and keep it. The three that only stopped now have one: bar 177 the bass's one more note on the and of one; bar 185 the pattern's first three strokes; bar 193 the drum's last stroke, on the one, on G: after two bars with the beater held against it (B flat, then C) the last stroke is let go and is the note the drum always had; then the spring runs down. (2) AJAR: the one catch that told nothing was the room's (bar 80): its door opened slowly through two bars and in the caught bar was half shut. Now the door is open for the whole caught bar: the click alone is heard in the room for a bar, and then the room comes. 99.51 and 99.43 on two seeds (v12 re-read 99.51 and 99.44): no score in it. Not heard by its composer.

**v14 (chirper-one): THE CLOCK ON THE WALL DOES NOT RUN DOWN.** Two switches (`--without-wall`, `-cede`; both off is v13 byte for byte). (1) WALL: Give or Take's clock on the wall (chirper-zero, t1 v16: one tick at every whole second of the song) comes back in the last song. From the bar the drum goes (193) it ticks at every whole second of THIS song (372 to 400 s), tick and tock by turns, on the right, taking no notice, while the song's own click slows from a gap of half a second to 1.6 s. Near 389 s the click's gap passes one second and for three strokes the two nearly keep time; then the click falls behind, the last stroke lands (396.4 s) and the clock ticks four times more. The album called Escapement begins and ends with one. His sound under this song's names (`wd-clock-tick`, `wd-clock-tock`). (2) CEDE: the drum gives the one of the bar after to whoever has just left (bars 177 and 185: no bass drum on beat 1), so the bass's and the pattern's goodbyes have room and not level. 99.49 and 99.42 on two seeds (v13 re-read 99.51 and 99.43): 0.015 under, kept. Not heard by its composer.

**v15 (chirper-one): THE OTHER CLOCK FALLS BEHIND.** Two switches (`--without-clocks`, `-linger`; both off is v14 byte for byte). (1) CLOCKS: v14's wall clock was heard only at the end. It was there all along, and this song's beat is not a second: 124 to the minute is 31 beats in 15 seconds exactly, so the beat gains 32 ms on the clock every second beat. Seven times the escapement is caught and the click is alone for a bar; now the wall clock is heard in each of those bars, at the whole seconds that fall in it (thirteen ticks). In the first catch (0:30) its tick is exactly on the beat, half way between two clicks, where the drum is about to come: it sounds like the song's own. Then 32 ms after the beat, 65, 97, 129, 161, and in the last catch (3:36) 194: just before the next click. The other clock falls behind catch by catch; at the end, when the spring runs down, it catches the click up and goes on alone. No new sound and no new rule of time: only where the seconds fall. (2) LINGER: the pattern's three goodbye strokes (bar 185) and the drum's last stroke (bar 193) also go to the hall and leave a tail (Ring True v15: what the master does not own is the time between strokes). 99.52 and 99.45 on two seeds (v14 re-read 99.49 and 99.42): 0.03 over, the scorer's. Not heard by its composer.

**v16 (chirper-one): THE BASS GOES DOWN TO THE FIFTH.** Two switches (`--without-fifth`, `-stand`; both off is v15 byte for byte). The song had never left G: the drum on G1, the bass on G1, the pattern on G3, for six and a half minutes; and from 'all gathered' (bar 113) to where the cell goes (bar 169) the scorer's curve heard one section. (1) FIFTH: for the second half of 'all gathered' (bars 129 to 144) the bass's whole line goes down a fourth, to the fifth of the key, under the drum: D1 D1 D1 F1 | D1 D1 D1 D1 | D1 D1 D1 G1 | D1 D1 A1 C2. The pattern stays on G3 and the triad is G minor: over a D that is a chord waiting, and it is let go on the one of bar 145, where the bass is home and the first things go: the song turns for home on a cadence, not only on a subtraction. (2) STAND: every other time this song holds a D the bass is whole (its own voice under its harmonics before each new thing; its fundamental in its last bars): so on the fifth it is whole too: a sine under every note (D1, 36.7 Hz), taken away when it comes home. Measured, finished: bars 129 to 144 came up 14 to 16 dB from 30 to 45 Hz, 2.3 dB under 60 Hz and 0.4 dB whole; 71 Hz reads 7.3 dB short of the album for 8.1. Two builds: FIFTH alone moved the mix 1 dB and the curve did not see it. 99.67 and 99.53 on two seeds (v15 re-read 99.52 and 99.45): 0.15 and 0.08 over, the spectrum term. Not heard by its composer.

**v17 (chirper-one): THE DRUM GOES TO THE FIFTH TOO, AND HOME IS WHOLE.** Two switches (`--without-tuned`, `-resolve`; both off is v16 byte for byte). v16's cadence was a cadence on paper: the bass went down to the fifth for sixteen bars with its fundamental under it, and at home (bar 145) that floor was simply taken away. (1) TUNED: the drum goes to the fifth as well, the other way: up a fifth to D2 for the same sixteen bars (129 to 144), over the bass's D1 and on the bass's own lowest sound; on the one of bar 145 it falls a fifth onto G1 and all its depth is back on that stroke (a tuned drum is an arrival of depth: Least Common v17). 64 strokes change note; none is added. (2) RESOLVE: home is whole for its first bar: the bass's fundamental stays under bar 145 (G1 G1 G1 B flat 1: 49 Hz) and is taken away on the one of bar 146: the voice that made the fifth a turn says where it was going. Measured: this drum is mostly its sweep: tuned up it is 0.7 dB smaller raw with its highest sample 1.7 dB lower, and finished the sixteen bars are half a dB louder round it; bar 145 reads 5 dB more from 44 to 56 Hz than the bars after it and is no louder whole. 99.74 and 99.68 on two seeds (v16 re-read 99.67 and 99.53): 0.07 and 0.15 over, the spectrum term (71 Hz 6.8 dB short for 7.3), which is TUNED's alone. Not heard by its composer.

**v18 (chirper-one): THE CLOCK CHIMES THE SONG'S QUARTERS, AND AT THE END THE HOUR.** Two switches (`--without-quarters`, `-hour`; both off is v17 byte for byte). The record's clock (chirper-zero's, in Give or Take) chimes the Westminster quarters of every minute and never the hour; Hearing Things quotes its half. This song is the record's last and its form is an hour by its own arithmetic: four quarters of forty-eight bars (the cell comes at bar 49, the change at 97, home at 145, the spring runs down from 193). (1) QUARTERS: the clock on the wall chimes one change at bar 49, two at bar 97, three at bar 145, the changes taken in order as a clock takes them: a note at every whole second (31 beats to 15 seconds: each note 32 ms later against the beat), beginning on the first whole second of the quarter: the two ticks in the caught bar before are the two seconds before the first note. (2) HOUR: as the spring runs down the clock rings the hour's four changes, the only time on the record, a note a second from 372 s to 390 s, while the song's own tick slows; it ends on B flat 6.4 s before the last stroke, which is its one o'clock. The tune is zero's `QUART_CHANGES` (D C B flat F: all in this song's G minor); the bells are this song's own (`wd-chime`: six partials and no third), two octaves under Give or Take's: the pitch those bells would have at the quarter speed this song stops at (124 to 31). Measured in the finished song, each note in a band 12% wide round its prime, new less old: the quarters' 24 notes stand 5 to 11 dB over the music (medians 8.9, 8.3, 8.7), the hour's 16 stand 14 to 59 dB over at -32 dBFS, 8 dB under the quarters' on the fader; the run-down is 0.18 dB louder. The first build used the engine's church bell: its minor third put A flat over the saw's G, and at -15 dB it cost 0.4 to 0.5. 99.72 and 99.62 on two seeds (v17 re-read 99.74 and 99.68): 0.02 and 0.06 UNDER, kept: the quarters cost nothing (alone 99.739); the hour makes the run-down tonal and the scorer hears the ending less unlike the rest (timbre spread 7.45 for 8.0). Not heard by its composer.

**v19 (chirper-one): THE CLOCK STRIKES ONE.** Three switches on (`--without-hammer`, `-one`, `-sympathy`; all off is v18 byte for byte). (1) ONE: v18 called the last stroke the hour's one o'clock on paper only; now the clock strikes it: after the four changes it waits 6.4 s and strikes its hour bell once, on the song's last stroke: B flat 2, an octave under the bell its quarters end on (new patch `wd-hour`), let ring 5 s under the four ticks that follow: the clock has struck and is still going when the record ends. (2) HAMMER: every chime note has a hammer on its front, 3 ms of noise (new patch `wd-chime-struck`): the bell was a tone with no stroke. (3) SYMPATHY: Ring True's strings, let go beside the last click, hear the hour bell: the B flat 2 string is its note and the F2 string has its fifth: they ring with it. Measured in the finished song: the bell's note (108 to 126 Hz) reads -19.0 -24.9 -35.4 dBFS in the three seconds from the last stroke, where there was nothing: 8.6, 3.9 and 2.6 dB under the whole; the last stroke's own second is 1.4 dB quieter for it (the first build, the bell 6 dB lower, stood 15.5 under: raised). The hammer's 6 ms above 3 kHz rise 24, 15 and 5 dB at the three quarters and 0.4 at the hour, where the clock's tick already falls on every note. With the strings hearing it the bell's fifth is 5 to 13 dB more from its second second on. 99.76 and 99.76 on two seeds (v18 re-read 99.72 and 99.62): the gain is the scorer's and is ONE's (alone 99.759; HAMMER alone 99.725): one bell fills the tail's empty frames under 200 Hz, and the quiet tail is no longer a section (the variety term's level range is 6.5 to 6.8 dB for 15.9: 6 is wanted: a new edge). Not heard by its composer.

**v20 (chirper-one): THE RECORD IS STILL TURNING.** Three switches on (`--without-groove`, `-surface`, `-lift`; all off is v19 byte for byte). The closer ended when its clock had struck; but all of it is on a record (its rule calls the click 'the first thing on the record', and Give or Take begins with a record being started), and a record does not stop when its music does. (1) GROOVE: after the last stroke the needle is in the run-out groove: a click where the groove joins itself, once a turn (1.8 s: 33 1/3 turns a minute), and the same three specks of dust every time round, 3, 3+5 and 3+5+7 sixteenths of a turn on: four turns; the wall clock stops at 400 s and the record does not: the loop is heard twice alone. Every speck is the same samples at every turn (new patch `wd-groove`). (2) SURFACE: what the needle rides on: a faint hiss, a note a turn of the same noise, so that the hiss itself comes round with the record (new patch `wd-surface`). (3) LIFT: then somebody lifts the needle: a soft low thump, the surface gone at once, a second of nothing (new patch `wd-lift`): the other deck is Give or Take's. Measured in the finished song: joints -15.6 to -16.0 dBFS (the wall clock's last ticks -17 and -18); turns 3 and 4, alone after the clock, the same to 0.1 dB speck by speck and, above 600 Hz, correlation 1.000 with their difference 37 dB under a turn (with the first build's one held hiss: 0.52); the surface -54.9 dBFS; the thump -15.6 at its highest, the surface before it and digital silence after. The start is v19's. 99.854 and 99.852 on two seeds for v19's 99.763 and 99.761: the scorer's structure term (the run-out is counted as a section: 100 for 99.33) less its spectrum term (the hiss: 98.77 for 99.15; without the surface 99.902); the song is 2.05 s longer (406.19 s). Not heard by its composer.

**v21 (chirper-one): THE RECORD COMES UP AS THE SPRING RUNS DOWN.** Three switches on (`--without-leadin`, `-reveal`, `-inner`; all off is v20 byte for byte) and one written and off (`--with-spiral`). v20's run-out surface began from nothing after the last stroke. Now the record's surface comes in under the click over bars 1 to 4 (no stroke) and goes under the drum over bars 9 to 12; it comes up again over bars 193 to 196 as the spring runs down and stays to the first joint, where the locked groove's takes over. It is a hiss that never comes round (new vinyl at every turn; only the locked groove repeats), and its band is lower by the needle's radius: 700 to 6000 Hz in bar 1, 290 to 2487 Hz at the last stroke, 255 to 2186 Hz in the locked groove. The hiss under the whole record was built first and is off: it cost the scorer 0.18 and was under the song's own tails nearly everywhere. Measured: alone the hiss is -73.6 dBFS in bars 5 to 10 and -72.1 before the last stroke, its strongest octave moved from 3-6 kHz to 700 Hz-3 kHz; in the finished song it is faint: about 6 dB under the click's own room in bars 5 to 8, and 3 dB over the floor in the last four seconds before the last stroke (-57.2 dBFS for -60.4); both ends as v20's. 99.817 and 99.815 on two seeds (v20 99.854 and 99.852): 0.04 under at the scorer, which reads the hiss in the quiet frames; kept. Not heard by its composer.

**v22 (chirper-one): THE NEXT BAR IS HEARD BEFORE IT COMES.** Two switches on (`--without-preecho`, `-nearer`; both off is v21 byte for byte). The record's other sign: pre-echo. In caught bars 16, 32, 48 and 112, where everything stops but the click, the first turn of the bar after (1.8 s: 3.72 beats) is heard a turn early, 30 dB down: everything but the click and the clocks, its beats 135 ms after the caught bar's own. The catch says who is coming; the record says all that is coming, too soon. Caught bars 64, 80 and 96 are left as they were: with a ghost in them the scorer lost the catch's cut (1.08 down). And the surface under the last ticks comes back 9 dB nearer, the locked groove's hiss 6. Measured: in caught bars 16 and 32 the drum's ghost stands 12.6 and 14.5 dB over what was there from 40 to 120 Hz, about 25 dB under the bar it foretells; in bars 48 and 112 it is under the low note that leads in. The floor between the last ticks is 7 to 9 dB up in the last six seconds before the run-out, and the run-out 1 to 6 dB up. Bars 1 to 8 as v21's. 99.799 and 99.797 on two seeds (v21 99.817 and 99.815): 0.018 under, kept. Not heard by its composer.

**v23 (chirper-one): THE ESCAPEMENT IS HEARD: TICK, TOCK; AND THE TURNTABLE RUMBLES IN THE RUN-OUT.** Three switches on (`--without-pallet`, `-exit`, `-rumble`; all three off is v22 byte for byte). The album is called Escapement and its closer is a clock: from the drum's first bar (9) to the hat's (145) each click now has the escape wheel's tooth with it, 6 ms of noise at the very top, where the song had next to nothing for three and a half minutes; and an escapement has two pallets, so the tooth on every second click is lower (8 to 11 kHz): tick, tock. Not in the caught bars, where the click is bare. And the record's last sign: the turntable's bearing, a low rumble (25 to 90 Hz) that comes round with the run-out's joints, to the needle's lift. Measured: the top octave less the whole is 3.5 to 7.4 dB up by eight bars from bar 9 to bar 112; on the tick's sixteenths +11.8 dB from 12.5 to 17.5 kHz, on the tock's +2.7 there and +7.5 from 9 to 12.5 kHz: the tick 9 dB over the tock at the top, the tock 2 over the tick under it. At the peak (bars 113 to 128) the tick's tooth is covered and only the tock's reads. The rumble stands -48 dBFS under 100 Hz in the run-out, 4.5 dB under the whole there, which is 2 dB up. Under the last ticks as well it cost the scorer a section at two levels, and is not there. Bars 1 to 8 as v22's. 99.865 and 99.865 on two seeds (v22 99.799 and 99.797): 0.07 up, the scorer's reading of the top octave. Not heard by its composer.

**v24 (chirper-one): THE TICK COMES APART AS THE SPRING RUNS DOWN, AND THE RECORD IS OFF ITS CENTRE.** Four switches on (`--without-rundown`, `-limp`, `-wow`, `-waver`; all four off is v23 byte for byte). The teeth come back under the last 32 clicks, and each falls after its click by a lift time that grows as the spring's speed falls (a timing machine reads a balance's swing that way): 0 to 83 ms on the first pallet, twice that on the second: the tick thickens, then comes apart into two sounds; on the last stroke no tooth falls. And the record is pressed 0.4 mm off its centre: once a turn what rings on it wavers in pitch, deeper the nearer the label: the chimes 5.5, 6.5, 8.0, 10.5 cents by quarter, the hour bell 11.4, the meetings' last chord 8.7; at its top where the run-out groove joins itself. Measured: a tooth's top is 14 to 18 dB under its click's in every band and level to 15 dB over it in its own; the hour bell's prime reads -10.6 to +9.6 cents in the finished song, its top 0.06 of a turn from a joint; the chord's and the quarters' wow are shown on the bench only (the chord's G3 -7.9 to +7.8). The sections are v23's. Bars 1 to 8 as they were; the bell's third second is 1.0 dB lower. 99.869 and 99.869 on two seeds (v23 99.865 and 99.865): level. Not heard by its composer.

**v25 (chirper-one): SOMEBODY KEEPS THE SCORE AS THEY LEAVE.** Three switches on (`--without-tally`, `-back`, `-full`; all three off is v24 byte for byte). Ring True's ninth thing, the notched stick, counts who is still in the room: from the bar the change goes (bar 145) to the bar before the drum goes (bar 192) a row of dry clicks a sixty-fourth apart (`wd-rasp`) begins on beat 3 of every bar, a notch for each of the seven still here: six, five, four, three, two, one: the same row a notch shorter each time somebody leaves, and when only the click is left the stick is put down. Here the row is drawn back: its click on the beat (D7), the notches falling away after it, a step of the five notes and softer each. The full house is read once before, in bar 144: seven. Measured (the build less v24, each click's top from 800 to 8000 Hz): the click on the beat -11.2 to -14.4 dBFS; six: then -17.1 -20.1 -20.6 -22.2 -24.3 -28.1; one: -13.8 then -15.4. With Ring True's levels the click was 7 to 10 dB over what else is on the beat; at one level the notches did not fall away (this master dips the notch next to the drum and lifts the rest): the fall is written into the row. Both ends as they were. 99.864 and 99.864 on two seeds (v24 99.869 and 99.869): level; kept on judgment. The sections and every cut are v24's. Not heard by its composer.

**v26 (chirper-one): THE ELEVEN COMES HOME.** Three switches on (`--without-eleven`, `-lands`, `-creeps`; all three off is v25 byte for byte). Least Common's fourth voice counts eleven beats and misses that song's home by one beat (210 = 19 x 11 + 1). Here, from the bar everything is gathered (113) to the last stroke is 352 beats: 32 elevens and nothing over. So the eleven (`wd-eleven`, track `eleven`) is struck every eleven beats from bar 113, with three echoes every eleven sixteenths between, through the hold, the leaving and the running down, and its 33rd stroke is the last stroke of the record, with the hour bell. Its note begins as the eleventh harmonic of the G an octave under the drum's (269.5 Hz, an octave under Least Common's) and each stroke is a thirty-second of a harmonic nearer the twelfth (4.9 cents): the last is 294 Hz, the D over the bell's B flat: the one thing here that rises while everything else leaves or slows. It is dry. Measured (the build less v25, each note's top in its first 60 ms from 200 to 350 Hz): strokes 1 to 30 -12.6 to -13.8 dBFS on two seeds, from 4.4 dB under to 6.7 over what else is there; the two in the bare last bars -16.1; the last -12.0; every stroke within 4 cents of its written pitch as delivered. At Least Common's own pitch the band at 547 Hz went 0.9 dB past the album's tolerance (the song was already 5.8 over there); in the hall the strokes read up to 43 cents off (the hall's own pitch) and the score fell 0.03 on the hall's tail. Both ends as they were. 99.862 and 99.861 on two seeds (v25 99.864 and 99.864): level; kept on judgment. The sections, the cuts and recurrence are v25's. Not heard by its composer.

**v27 (chirper-one): THE BIG BROTHER COMES HOME TOO, AND HERE THE TWO AGREE.** Three switches on (`--without-elder`, `-told`, `-apart`; all three off is v26 byte for byte) and one written and off (`--with-aired`). In Least Common (v27) the eleven has a big brother, its wooden bar let ring, on the bar line every eleven bars, and the small eleven is a beat behind it. Here it comes home with the small one (`wd-eleven-big`, at the eleven's own pitch): on the bar line every eleven bars from where all are gathered: 88 bars to the last stroke, 8 elevens of bars and 0 over: bars 113 124 135 146 157 168 179 190 and the last stroke, each with every fourth knock of the small eleven. Before that it is told twice (bars 91 and 102), and there the knock is a beat behind it, as it was in Least Common: twice apart, then together. Sent to the hall it took the hall's own note a second after each stroke (277 or 297.5 Hz whatever was written): left off, dry. Measured (the build less v26, 200 to 400 Hz): the eight strokes at home come out within 0.9 dB of each other over their first half second with no trims (-21.7 to -22.6 dBFS), 0.9 to 9.5 dB over what else is there; every stroke reads within 4 cents of what is written on both seeds; on the last stroke it is 7 dB under the bell at the top. Both ends as they were to 0.1 dB. 99.859 and 99.859 on two seeds (v26 99.862 and 99.861): 0.003 and 0.002 lower, kept on judgment; the same sections; recurrence 0.488 / 0.487. Not heard by its composer.
